Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)&:=Q_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho(e\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)}\left[(1-\gamma)r(e)+\gamma V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)\right]\,.\end{split}

(11)

Definition 3.9

(
k
-step planner embedded Bayesian agent)
.

A
k
-step planner embedded Bayesian agent with mixture universe model
\rho
is a policy which at time
t
returns an action
a_{t}
satisfying

a_{t}\in\argmax_{a}Q^{k}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\,.

Similar to MuZero-type algorithms, the k-step planner embedded Bayesian agent distills k-step planning with terminal values
V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
into its self-model
\rho(a\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
.
Under additional assumptions on the mixture universe
\rho
, we show in
Section

4
that the
k
-step planner embedded Bayesian agent for any
k
converges to an infinite-horizon optimal planner.
Intuitively, at the start of learning, the policy implements
k
-step optimal planning. When the self-model
\rho(a\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
distills this k-step planning during learning, the terminal value
V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})

represents a
k
-step planning policy, and hence adding
k
-step planning to that results in an effective
2k
-step planning policy and so forth indefinitely.

3.5
Convergence of the predictive distribution

We now turn our attention to the question of when embedded Bayesian agents can make accurate predictions about the future.
Theorem

3.11
below adapts
Theorem

2.4
stated for mixture environments towards mixture universes used by embedded Bayesian agents.

Definition 3.10

(The grain-of-truth property)
.

We say that a Bayesian mixture universe
\rho
satisfies the grain-of-truth property w.r.t. the ground-truth universe if
\rho
dominates
\upsilon
(cf.
Definition

2.3
).

Theorem 3.11
.

Given any Bayesian mixture universe
\rho
and ground-truth universe
\upsilon
resulting from combining policy
\pi
and environment
\mu
. If the mixture universe
\rho
satisfies the grain-of-truth property w.r.t.
\upsilon=\mu^{\pi}
, then the predictive distribution
\rho
converges to the predictive distribution of
\upsilon
almost surely:

D_{\infty}(\rho,\upsilon\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\to 0\quad\text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ D_{\infty}(\rho,\upsilon\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a_{t+1})\to 0\quad\text{as}\ t\to\infty\quad\upsilon\text{-almost-surely}.

In particular, the above holds for embedded Bayesian agents where
\pi
is an embedded best-response (equation
10
) or
k
-step planning policy (cf.
Definition

3.9
) w.r.t. the mixture universe
\rho
, where we now additionally
20
20

20

Note that we still need to assume that
\rho
dominates
\upsilon=\mu^{\pi}
.
require that
\rho
satisfies the grain-of-uncertainty property (cf.
Definition

3.1
) so that the best response policy is well-defined.

Proof.

As
\rho
dominates
\upsilon
, and dominance implies absolute continuity, the proof is a direct application of the merging of opinions theorem
(
8
;
58
)
.
∎

Remark 3.12

(Ground-truth universes)
.

The notion of a ground-truth
universe
is somewhat more subtle than that of the more-familiar ground-truth
environment
. The ground-truth universe is the combination of a ground-truth environment and a ground-truth policy. But if the ground-truth policy itself performs some type of Bayesian planning w.r.t. a Bayesian mixture with associated priors, then the ground-truth policy is causally downstream of, and a function of, both the particular planning procedure chosen and the Bayesian mixture prior weights — changing either of these will change the ground-truth policy and hence the ground-truth universe as well. In contrast, in the standard decoupled agency setting, the ground-truth environment is independent of the choice of planning algorithm and the prior weights.

Remark 3.13

(Consistent self- and mutual prediction)
.

As the ground-truth universe both contains the ego-agent and possibly other embedded Bayesian agents, the convergence theorem implies that the embedded Bayesian agent performs consistent self- and mutual prediction, ultimately leading to infinite-order theory of mind. This illustrates that the assumption that the grain-of-truth property is satisfied is a very powerful one. In
Section

5
, we explicitly construct universe classes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
on which Bayesian mixture universes satisfy the grain-of-truth property w.r.t. universes containing embedded Bayesian agents, and hence perform consistent mutual prediction.

In Sections
3.3
-
3.4
, we introduced the embedded best response policies (equation
10
) and
k
-step planner policies (
Definition

3.9
) from the point of view of optimal planning within the environment model
\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
, which required us to extract an environment model from the mixture universe (cf. equation
9
). Leveraging
Theorem

3.11
, we interpret these policies from a different angle in the remark below, without requiring that we extract an environment model from the mixture universe, which is arguably more natural in the embedded setting.

Remark 3.14

(Embedded Bayesian agents act
as if
their actions decide which universe they live in)
.

Embedded Bayesian agents act to concentrate their beliefs on highly-rewarding universes consistent with their action-percept history seen so far, and under the assumptions of
Theorem

3.11
their beliefs converge to a predictive distribution indistinguishable from the ground-truth universe, hence the predetermined ground-truth universe that contains the embedded Bayesian agent is indistinguishable from the universe that the embedded Bayesian agents deliberately “choose” to live in. To illustrate this line of reasoning, let us consider a hypothesis class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
over deterministic universes and a 1-step planner embedded Bayesian agent (cf.
Definition

3.5
) implementing a deterministic policy

\pi(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\in\argmax_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a),\quad Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a)Q_{\lambda}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)\,.

The values
Q_{\lambda}
are fully predetermined by unrolling the policy and environment described by the universe
\lambda
. As the universes are deterministic, updating the beliefs
w(\lambda\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a)
on action
a
excludes all universes that are incompatible with the ego-agent taking action
a
. The embedded Bayesian agent’s policy selects the action
a
that leads to posterior beliefs
w(\lambda\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a)
that focuses its probability mass on the highest reward universes quantified by
Q_{\lambda}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)
. Incoming percepts
e
then further exclude universes that are incompatible with those percepts, grounding the posterior beliefs in reality. When
\rho
satisfies the grain of truth w.r.t. the ground-truth universe (which includes the embedded Bayesian agent itself),
Theorem

3.11
shows that this decision process that concentrates the posterior beliefs on high-reward universes converges on posterior beliefs that are indistinguishable from the ground-truth distribution. Hence, embedded Bayesian agents “end up living” in a high-reward universe. While the illustration considers deterministic universes and a one-step planner agent, a similar intuition holds for stochastic universes and
k
-step planning, with
k
possibly being infinite.

We established that the predictive distribution converges to the ground-truth distribution. Can we also make statements about whether embedded Bayesian agents converge to an optimal policy? This will be the focus of
Section

4
. As a prerequisite, we first take a closer look at the beliefs of the embedded Bayesian agents.

3.6
Structural similarities: coupled beliefs through common causation

Both decoupled and embedded Bayesian agents are ‘rational agents’ that (i) update their beliefs and resulting predictive model through principled Bayesian updates and (ii) compute best responses through optimal planning. The main difference between the two types of Bayesian agents is which hypothesis class and corresponding prior beliefs they start from.

Decoupled Bayesian agents maintain beliefs over environments (cf. equation
4
) which they update with incoming percepts
e
. In case decoupled Bayesian agents also maintain a self model, as for example Self-AIXI (cf.
2.4
), the beliefs about the environment
\nu
and the agent’s own policy
\pi
are
decoupled
: The beliefs over environments are only updated with incoming percepts, and the beliefs over policies only with incoming actions, or equivalently,
w(\pi,\nu)=w(\pi)w(\nu)
.
In contrast, embedded Bayesian agents maintain beliefs over universes
\lambda
, that encompass both the agent’s policy
\pi
and the environment
\nu
. Its beliefs are in general
coupled
, meaning that the policy can contain information about the environment and vice versa. As a result, incoming actions not only provide information about the policy
\pi
, but can also contain information about the environment
\nu
, which possibly includes other agents. In the following, we first argue why coupled beliefs are desirable and which natural phenomena give rise to them. Then we formalize
structural similarities
as the notion that the policy
\pi
can contain information about the environment
\nu
. Finally, we show that incorporating structural similarities through coupled beliefs is important for making accurate predictions. This serves as a prelude for
Section

4
, where we show that coupled beliefs enable embedded Bayesian agents in multi-agent scenarios to converge to novel types of solution concepts different from Nash equilibria.

Structural similarities.

An embedded agent’s beliefs about its own policy and about the environment it is interacting with can be coupled, a phenomenon most clearly motivated by
structural similarities
. When a universe
\lambda
encompasses an ego-agent
\pi
and other agents within the environment use a copy of
\pi
as a policy, then the ego agent’s policy and actions contain information about the environment. This case of “identical copies” can be generalized towards situations where different agents share similar functional subroutines in their policies. Such structural similarities are not exceptional; they arise naturally in the world through various pathways, of which we highlight the following two
(
117
;
22
)
:
21
21

21

In the decision theory literature, structural similarities are investigated under the rubric of
Newcomb-like decision problems

(
77
;
65
;
33
;
51
;
2
)
.
(i) Different agents can have a
shared creation process
, such as when multiple AIs are instantiations of the same base model or when organisms share genes. (ii) Different agents can use a
convergent solution
to a similar task, i.e., develop analogous strategies to solve similar problems. Such structural similarities lead to coupled beliefs through the
common cause principle

(
90
)
: Policies and environments can share common causes, i.e., pathways towards structural similarities, while uncertainty over such causes leads to coupled beliefs.

By including hypotheses about structural similarities in its mixture model, an embedded agent can leverage the insight that “similar agents behave similarly in similar situations”. As a prelude to Section
4
, we illustrate the importance of this reasoning in the
Twin Prisoner’s Dilemma
, a one-shot game played against an exact copy of oneself.

Example 3.15

(The Twin Prisoner’s Dilemma
(
117
;
22
;
65
)
)
.

Imagine an AI agent is about to play a single round of the prisoner’s dilemma against an exact copy of itself.
22
22

22

The rules are standard: If both cooperate, they each receive a moderate reward (
r=2
); if both defect, they each receive a low reward (
r=1
); and if one defects while the other cooperates, the defector gets the highest reward (
r=3
) and the cooperator gets nothing (
r=0
).
The agent is given conclusive proof that its opponent is a perfect copy, running the same decision-making algorithm. From a purely selfish perspective, should the agent cooperate or defect?

While a standard decoupled agent would defect (the Nash Equilibrium), this thought experiment suggests that for an embedded agent that recognizes the perfect structural similarity with its opponent, it is rational to cooperate, as mutual cooperation leads to a higher personal reward compared to mutual defection. We will formalize this ability to rationalize cooperation by reasoning about structural dependencies in Section
4
.

Formalizing structural similarities.

For reasons of clarity, we assume that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
only contains
fully supported universes
as defined below. We refer the reader to
Appendix

E
where we consider the more general case without this assumption.

Definition 3.16

(Fully supported universe)
.

A universe
\lambda
is
fully supported
iff
\lambda(\text{\ae}_{*})>0
and
\lambda(\text{\ae}_{*}a)>0
for all
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Importantly, a fully supported universe has well-defined conditionals
\lambda(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
and
\lambda(e_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a_{t+1})
that uniquely specify a policy
\pi(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
and environment
\nu(e_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a_{t+1})
respectively. Hence, we can define an invertible mapping
f:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
converting a universe
\lambda
into a pair of policy and environment functions
(\pi,\nu)
. Hence, a prior
w(\lambda)
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
induces a joint prior
w(\pi,\nu)
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
with

w(\pi,\nu):=w(\nu^{\pi})\,.

Leveraging Shannon information theory, we define the
degree of structural similarity
23
23

23

Mathematically, the quantity
S(\lambda,w)=\log\frac{w(\lambda)}{w(\nu)w(\pi)}
is mainly a measure of the ”degree of coupledness”
\nu
and
\pi
w.r.t. the prior
w
. This coupling, in principle, could be arbitrary, and may not be due to beliefs about ”structural similarities”. For example, one may consider priors where environments are coupled to ”structurally dissimilar” policies. Nevertheless, we will still use the term ”degree of structural similarity” because the main focus of this paper is to consider priors that are coupled due to beliefs about structural similarities.

S
within a universe
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}
w.r.t. belief distribution
w(\lambda)
as the pointwise mutual information between
\pi
and
\nu
:

\displaystyle S(\lambda,w)

\displaystyle:=\log\frac{w(\lambda)}{w(\nu)w(\pi)}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{with}\penalty\ (\pi,\nu)=f(\lambda)\,,

(12)

\displaystyle w(\nu)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}w(\nu^{\pi^{\prime}})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ w(\pi):=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w({\nu^{\prime}}^{\pi})\,.

(13)

The average degree of structural similarity within
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
w.r.t.
w
is then defined as the Shannon mutual information
\mathcal{I}
between
\pi
and
\nu
, when
(\pi,\lambda)=f(\nu)
is distributed according to
w
:

\displaystyle S(w):=\mathbb{E}_{w(\lambda)}\left[S(\lambda,w)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{w(\pi,\nu)}\left[\log\frac{w(\pi,\nu)}{w(\pi)w(\nu)}\right]=\mathcal{I}_{w}(\pi;\nu)\,.

(14)

If the random variables
\pi
and
\nu
share a positive average degree of structural similarity, i.e.,
\mathcal{I}_{w}(\pi;\nu)>0
, then they can be efficiently compressed together. In
Section

5.4
, we will investigate this in more detail from the perspective of algorithmic information theory.

Coupled vs decoupled beliefs.

We call the beliefs
w(\lambda)

decoupled
iff
w(\lambda)=w(\pi)w(\nu)
with
(\pi,\nu)=f(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
. The beliefs
w(\lambda)
are
coupled
iff they are not decoupled. From these definitions, it is easy to see that the beliefs
w(\lambda)
are decoupled iff the degree of structural similarity is 0 for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
. Intuitively, one may say that the average degree of structural similarity measures "how much" the prior
w
is coupled.
Proposition

3.17
shows that embedded Bayesian agents with decoupled prior beliefs
w(\lambda)
behave identically to decoupled Bayesian agents, illustrating that structural similarities are at the core of what makes embedded Bayesian agents behave differently from decoupled Bayesian agents.

Proposition 3.17
.

Consider a hypothesis class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
over fully supported universes and the corresponding environment and policy classes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
. If the prior beliefs
w(\lambda)
are decoupled, then the conditionals of the mixture universe
\rho:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda
can be written in the following decoupled form:

\displaystyle\rho(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle:=\zeta(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}),\quad\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\xi(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\zeta(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}w_{\textrm{pol}}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\pi(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}),\quad w_{\textrm{pol}}(\pi\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a_{t+1}):=w_{\textrm{pol}}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\frac{\pi(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}{\zeta(a_{t+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}

\displaystyle\xi(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w_{\textrm{env}}(\nu\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}),\quad w_{\textrm{env}}(\nu\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}):=w_{\textrm{env}}(\nu\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\frac{\nu(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\xi(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}

with
w_{\textrm{pol}}(\pi\mid\varepsilon):=w(\pi)
and
w_{\textrm{env}}(\nu\mid\varepsilon):=w(\nu)
. Hence,
\rho
uses decoupled posterior beliefs
w_{\textrm{pol}}(\pi\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a_{t+1})
and
w_{\textrm{env}}(\nu\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
. As a result,

(i)

An embedded Bayes-optimal agent using the decoupled beliefs
w(\lambda)
to construct its mixture universe model
\rho
(cf. equation
8
) and implementing an embedded best response w.r.t.
\rho
(cf. equation
10
) is equivalent to a decoupled Bayesian agent with mixture environment
\xi
defined above, and implementing a decoupled best response w.r.t.
\xi
(cf. equation
5
).

(ii)

A
k
-step planner embedded Bayesian agent (cf.
Definition

3.9
) using the decoupled beliefs
w(\lambda)
to construct its mixture universe model
\rho
is equivalent to a
k
-step planner decoupled Bayesian agent with mixture environment
\xi
and mixture policy
\zeta
as defined above, and implementing a
k
-step planner policy

a_{t}\in\argmax_{a}Q_{\xi^{\zeta}}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t}),

with
Q_{\xi^{\zeta}}^{k}
as defined in equation
11
.

One can see that the policy prior
w_{\textrm{pol}}
and its corresponding mixture policy
\zeta
have no effect on the policy of Bayes-optimal agents, but they can still affect the policy of
k
-step planners.

Proof.

See
Section

C.1

∎

Predictions leveraging structural similarities.

Finally, let us investigate the consequences of incorporating structural similarities upon the predictions made by
\rho
. Assume that a history
\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}
is drawn according to some universe
\lambda
. The accumulated (average) prediction loss incurred by using the mixture universe
\rho
as a predictor can be written in terms of the KL divergence as

L_{n}(\rho,\lambda):=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[\mathrm{KL}\left(\lambda(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\mid\mid\rho(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\right)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}\left[\mathrm{KL}\left(\lambda(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\mid\mid\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\right)\right]\Big).

(15)

It is known
24
24

24

Check, e.g.,
44
.
that due to the telescopic property of the KL divergence, we have

L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)=\mathrm{KL}\left(\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\mid\mid\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\right)=\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\log\frac{\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}\,.

(16)

A celebrated result states that the accumulated prediction loss over all timesteps is bounded by
-\log w(\lambda)
:

Theorem 3.18

(Generalized Solomonoff bound
(
44
)
)
.

For all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
with
w(\lambda)>0
, and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the average accumulated prediction loss over all trajectories of length
n
can be bounded as follows:

L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)\leq-\log w(\lambda)<\infty\,.

Now let us compare an embedded Bayesian agent using a mixture universe
\rho
with beliefs
w(\lambda)
to make predictions, versus a decoupled Bayesian agent such as Self-AIXI using decoupled mixture environment
\xi
and mixture policy
\zeta
using prior beliefs
w(\nu)
and
w(\pi)
respectively, obtained from marginalizing
w(\lambda)
. Then
Theorem

3.18
shows that the total prediction loss of the embedded Bayesian agent is bounded by
-\log w(\lambda)
, versus the decoupled Bayesian agent which has a bound of
-\log w(\pi)w(\nu)
. The difference
25
25

25

We emphasize that the comparison here is with the particular decoupled Bayesian agent using the decoupled prior
w_{d}(\lambda)=w(\pi)w(\nu)
where
w(\pi)
and
w(\nu)
are the marginals of the (potentially coupled) prior
w(\lambda)
of the embedded Bayesian agent. Furthermore, the comparison is only relevant when we are comparing agents that use their self-model
\rho(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
in their planning (e.g., the embedded
k
-step planners or the decoupled Self-AIXI). If we consider the best response policy (making infinite-horizon planning) which ignores the self-model, then a more meaningful comparison would be to only consider the accumulated prediction loss over the percepts, i.e., only consider the second term of the sum in equation
15
.
Another important caveat to mention here is that in the comparison, we are fixing a universe
\lambda
and then comparing the prediction loss over trajectories that are sampled from
\lambda
. However, the most relevant prediction loss is the one that is incurred in the ground-truth universe, and changing the ground-truth policy would change the ground-truth universe. Therefore, a meaningful comparison between, let us say embedded 1-step planners
\pi_{1,\text{embedded}}
and decoupled Self-AIXI
\pi_{\text{Self-AIXI}}
, would be to fix some ground-truth environment
\mu
and then compare
L_{n}(\rho,\mu^{\pi_{1,\text{embedded}}})
with
L_{n}(\rho_{d},\mu^{\pi_{\text{Self-AIXI}}})
.

between these bounds is exactly equal to the degree of structural similarity
S(\lambda,w)
, suggesting that for universes exhibiting positive degrees of structural similarities, the predictive distribution of the mixture universe
\rho
may converge faster to the ground-truth distribution
\nu
compared to the decoupled predictive distributions of
\zeta
and
\xi
. This comes at the cost of a slower convergence of
\rho
for universes with a negative degree of structural similarities. This leads us to the following remark.

Remark 3.19

(Prospective prediction with structural similarity awareness.)
.

The tighter prediction bound from
Theorem

3.18
for universes with positive structural similarity highlights a key advantage for prospective prediction. Recognizing structural similarities provides a powerful method for predicting the behavior of other agents in novel situations never encountered before, which is particularly relevant for multi-agent systems where predictions are harder due to "non-stationarities" caused by the learning of all agents involved. This structural similarity-aware prediction, which relies on the reasoning that “similar agents behave similarly in similar situations”, allows an embedded agent to leverage its self-model to anticipate the actions of others, leading to more accurate predictions about the behavior of other agents.

As Shannon information depends on the used probability distribution, our
degree of structural similarities
for a universe
\lambda
depends on the belief prior
w
. Hence, the choice of prior determines how much the embedded Bayesian agents takes structural similarities into account. As noted earlier, structural similarities, such as the sharing of functional subroutines, are common in the world. It is therefore reasonable to incorporate these insights into the design of the prior, leading to coupled beliefs.
This argument can be further sharpened using Occam’s razor: Universes where agents share structural similarities are simpler to describe than universes where each agent is different. Hence, the former should be given a higher prior probability. In
Section

5.4
, we will formally ground this intuition using algorithmic information theory and show that the universal Solomonoff prior is always coupled.

Remark 3.20

(A large
S(\lambda,w)
does not necessarily imply a smaller prediction loss compared to the decoupled prior)
.

An important caveat of the above discussion is that the term
-\log w(\lambda)
of
Theorem

3.18
is only an upper bound which may be loose. Therefore, even if the degree of structural similarity is very large, we cannot really say that the prediction loss of the mixture distribution
\rho=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda
of the coupled prior
w(\lambda)
is necessarily much smaller compared to the prediction loss of the mixture distribution
\rho_{d}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w_{d}(\lambda)\lambda
of its decoupled counterpart
w_{d}(\lambda)=w(\pi)w(\nu)
. In fact, there are examples where
w
is "very coupled" in the sense that there are universes
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
for which
S(\lambda,w)
is very large, but nevertheless
\rho=\rho_{d}
and hence
L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)=L_{n}(\rho_{d},\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
(including those with large
S(\lambda,w)
), and hence for such examples
\rho
and
\rho_{d}
have the exact same prediction loss.

Even though
\rho
and
\rho_{d}
might be exactly equal, and hence the Bayes-optimal policy is the same for the corresponding embedded and decoupled Bayesian agents, the differences between the coupled and decoupled priors become important if one consider variants of the Bayes-optimal agents that do stronger forms of exploration such as Thompson sampling, as this would necessarily use the Bayesian beliefs to guide the exploration.
26
26

26

This is similar to the discussion at the end of
Remark

3.4
.

Given the caveat of the above remark, one wonders whether one can directly compare the predictions losses
L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)
and
L_{n}(\rho_{d},\lambda)
, without using the upper bounds
-\log(w(\lambda))
and
-\log(w_{d}(\lambda))
which may be loose. The next proposition provides such a comparison.

Proposition 3.21
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if we average over all universes
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
according to the prior
w(\lambda)
, then average prediction loss
L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)
cannot be worse than the average prediction loss
L_{n}(\rho_{d},\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)L_{n}(\rho_{d},\lambda)-\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)=\mathrm{KL}\left(\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\mid\mid\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\right)\geq 0\,.

Proof.

From equation
16
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}

\displaystyle w(\lambda)\left(L_{n}(\rho_{d},\lambda)-L_{n}(\rho,\lambda)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\left(\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\log\frac{\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}-\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\log\frac{\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\left(\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\log\frac{\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}\right)=\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\left(\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\right)\log\frac{\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{n}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{n}}\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\log\frac{\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}{\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})}=\mathrm{KL}\left(\rho_{d}(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\mid\mid\rho(\text{\ae}_{1:{n}})\right)\geq 0\,.

∎

4
Equilibrium behavior of embedded Bayesian agents

Having defined embedded Bayesian agents and their predictive mechanisms in the previous section, we now turn to a central question: What is the long-term strategic behavior of these agents when they interact? Understanding the notions of optimality in an embedded agency setup and the equilibrium behavior they converge to is important for characterizing their potential for cooperation or conflict. This section provides a comprehensive game-theoretic analysis of embedded Bayesian agents.

The results in this section are multifaceted, covering several distinct axes of variation. To guide the reader through our results, we first discuss and motivate these various axes and briefly situate our main results along them.

Decoupled vs. embedded agents.
A primary axis contrasts our work with the foundational literature. The seminal work of
52
;
53
;
54
uncovered in detail the convergence behavior of
decoupled
Bayesian agents, which we review in
Section

4.1
. In the subsequent sections, we aim to translate these results to the
embedded
Bayesian agents setup, where agents maintain beliefs over universes including themselves, instead of only environments. This requires us to develop new solution concepts that incorporate reasoning based on structural similarities, a feat unique to embedded Bayesian agents.

Subjective vs. objective solution concepts.
We distinguish between two types of solution concepts.
Subjective solution concepts
describe the behavior that rational learners, like Bayesian agents, converge to. This behavior is optimal with respect to their internal,
subjective beliefs
about the world. In contrast,
objective solution concepts
assess whether this converged behavior is optimal with respect to the
ground-truth
environment and the actual policies of the other agents. We develop both subjective and objective concepts for the embedded setting.

Repeated games vs. MAGRL.
We analyze agent behavior in two different settings. We first develop our new game-theoretic concepts in the classical
repeated games
setup (
Section

4.2
). This setting is simpler due to perfect monitoring of other agents’ actions and exact knowledge of the reward function. We then generalize these results to the more complex and realistic Multi-Agent General Reinforcement Learning (MAGRL) framework (
Section

4.3
), which requires us to handle partial observability and imperfect knowledge about the environment, leading to different equilibrium properties.

Exact vs.
\epsilon
-equilibria.
The beliefs of Bayesian agents (both decoupled and embedded) only converge to the ground-truth asymptotically. To characterize agent behavior before convergence is complete, we introduce
\epsilon
-variants of our solution concepts. These
\epsilon
-equilibria characterize behavior where the agent’s predictive model is allowed to be
\epsilon
-close to the ground-truth distribution, rather than identical to it.

We begin in
Section

4.1
with reviewing the results of Kalai and Lehrer on the equilibrium behavior of
decoupled
Bayesian agents. In
Section

4.2
, we pivot to the study of embedded Bayesian agents in the setting of repeated games, where we introduce our central contributions: the
subjective embedded equilibrium (SEE)
and its objective counterpart, the
embedded equilibrium (EE)
. These novel concepts take into account the coupled beliefs that arise from reasoning about structural similarities. We prove that embedded agents converge to playing
\epsilon
-SEEs. In
Section

4.3
, we generalize our findings to the richer Multi-Agent General Reinforcement Learning (MAGRL) framework, showing convergence to a
\epsilon
-correlated SEE while highlighting the challenges introduced by partial observability and unknown reward functions. Finally,
Section

4.4
analyzes more practical
k
-step planner agents and demonstrates their convergence to approximate versions of these equilibria. The tables below summarize the main solution concepts and convergence results presented in this section along the discussed axes.

Box 4.1: Abbreviations for the game-theoretic solution concepts

Abbreviation

Full Solution Concept

NE

Nash Equilibrium

CE

Correlated Equilibrium

SNE

Subjective Nash Equilibrium

SCE

Subjective Correlated Equilibrium

EE

Embedded Equilibrium

CEE

Subjective Embedded Equilibrium

SCEE

Box 4.3: Summary of Main Convergence Results

\epsilon
-SNE

(and
\epsilon
-NE)

\epsilon
-SEE

(and
\epsilon
-EE under add. conditions)

\epsilon
-SCE

Converge to
\epsilon
-SCEE

4.1
Background on equilibrium Behavior of Decoupled Bayesian Agents

Before analyzing the equilibrium behavior of embedded agents, we first set the stage by reviewing the foundational results of
52
;
53
;
54
, who studied the equilibrium behavior of
decoupled
Bayesian agents. Their work provides a crucial foundation, establishing convergence to
\epsilon
-Nash equilibria in the setting of repeated games with perfect monitoring.
We begin by formally defining this setting.

Definition 4.1

(Repeated Games with Perfect Monitoring)
.

A repeated game consists of
T
rounds of a single-stage game where each agent
i
simultaneously takes an action
a^{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{i}
and receives a reward
r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})
based on its own action and the actions chosen by the other agents
-i:=[N]\setminus\{i\}
in the current round.
27
27

27

Note that we use the notation
[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
The number of rounds
T
can be infinite. The agents have perfect recall of the history
\bar{a}_{1:t}=(\bar{a}_{1},...,\bar{a}_{t})\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{*}
, which consists of the previous actions of all agents. Each agent has a policy
\pi^{i}:\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\rightarrow\Delta\mathcal{A}^{i}
that makes a decision independently of the decisions of other agents in the current round. The agents have perfect knowledge of their own reward function
r^{i}(\bar{a})
, which is important when agents construct their behavioral policy
\pi^{i}
through, e.g., optimal planning. We define the percepts equal to the actions of the other agents:
e^{i}:=a^{-i}
. The agents can then use their percepts and own actions to compute their reward
r^{i}(\bar{a})
.

As in repeated games, the rewards are not provided by the environment but instead computed by the agents with reward function
r^{i}(\bar{a})
, and the percepts are equal to the actions of other agents, the multi-agent environment
\overline{\nu}
is a dummy environment interfacing the policies of all agents.

Within this framework of repeated games,
52
consider a specific case of decoupled Bayes-optimal agents that maintain a subjective belief model for each of its co-players, as detailed below.

Definition 4.2

(Decoupled Bayes-optimal agent in repeated games)
.

A decoupled Bayes-optimal agent in a repeated game with perfect monitoring is an agent
i
that maintains an independent subjective belief model for each co-player
j\neq i
, represented as a mixture model over possible co-player’s policies:
\xi_{j}^{i}(a^{j}|\bar{a}_{1:t})=\sum_{\pi^{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{j}}w_{j}^{i}(\pi^{j}|\bar{a}_{1:t})\pi^{j}(a^{j}|\bar{a}_{1:t})
. These individual models are combined into a single mixture environment model,
\xi^{i}
, under the assumption that co-players act independently:

\xi^{i}(e^{i}_{t}|\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})=\xi^{i}(a^{-i}_{t}|\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}):=\prod_{j\neq i}\xi_{j}^{i}(a_{t}^{j}|\bar{a}_{<t}).

The agent’s policy,
\pi^{i}
, is then a best response computed with respect to this subjective environment
\xi^{i}
(cf. equation
5
), using the agent’s ground-truth reward function
r^{i}(\bar{a})
to compute rewards from the predicted joint actions.

4.1.1
Subjective and objective solution concepts

The central question is: what is the long-term equilibrium behavior when such agents interact? To answer this, Kalai and Lehrer introduced the
subjective Nash equilibrium

(
52
;
53
;
54
)
. Below, we define the subjective Nash equilibrium in its general form that is also applicable to the MAGRL setting, and discuss afterwards how it simplifies in the repeated games setting.

Definition 4.3

(Subjective Nash Equilibrium)
.

A set of policies
(\pi^{i})_{i\in N}
and subjective environment models
(\xi^{i})_{i\in N}
constitutes a subjective Nash equilibrium in the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
if, for each agent
i
:

1.

Subjective Best Response
: The policy
\pi^{i}
is a decoupled best response (cf. equation
5
) with respect to the subjective environment model
\xi^{i}
.

2.

Uncontradicted Beliefs
: The distribution over histories induced by agent
i
’s beliefs and its own policy,
\left(\xi^{i}\right)^{\pi^{i}}
, is identical to the ground-truth personal distribution
\left(\mu^{i}\right)^{\pi^{i}}
induced by the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
and policies of all agents.

An
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium
relaxes the second condition, requiring only that the two distributions are
\epsilon
-close in total variation distance, i.e.,
D_{\infty}\left(\left(\xi^{i}\right)^{\pi^{i}},\left(\mu^{i}\right)^{\pi^{i}}\middle|\varepsilon\right)\leq\epsilon
.

For repeated games, the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
is a dummy interface between the policies of all agents, and the personal histories are equal to joint action sequences. Hence the ground-truth personal distribution is equivalent to the joint policies of all agents:

\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\bar{a}_{1:t})=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\pi^{i}(a_{k}^{j}\mid\bar{a}_{<k}).

This is a
subjective
solution concept because each agent computes a best response against its own belief model
\xi^{i}
, not necessarily against the ground-truth environment composed of the other agents’ true policies. The
uncontradicted beliefs
condition grounds these subjective beliefs in reality, but only
on the play-path
—that is, for histories with a non-zero probability of occurring. For counterfactual histories that have zero probability under
\pi^{i}
, the conditionals
\xi^{i}(a_{t}^{-i}|\bar{a}_{<t})
are not constrained by reality. These "off-the-play-path" beliefs can be incorrect yet are never falsified by observation. Nonetheless, they are crucial for the optimal planning routine, which must evaluate all counterfactual actions, including those with zero probability under the agent’s policy.

The corresponding
objective
solution concept is the well-known
Nash equilibrium
, where each agent’s policy must be a best response to the
actual
policies of the other agents
(
76
)
.

Definition 4.4

(Nash Equilibrium)
.

A set of policies
(\pi^{i})_{i\in N}
constitutes a Nash equilibrium in the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
if, for each agent
i
, the policy
\pi^{i}
is a best response w.r.t. the ground-truth personal environment
\mu^{i}
induced by the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
and policies of all agents:

\pi^{i}\in\argmax_{\pi}V_{(\mu^{i})^{\pi}}(\varepsilon).

An
\epsilon
-Nash equilibrium relaxes the best response condition, requiring that the value of each agent’s policy is
\epsilon
-close to the optimal value. Note that for
\epsilon
-Nash equilibria, the
\epsilon
-closeness condition is w.r.t. the best response computation, whereas for
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibria, the
\epsilon
-closeness condition is on the subjective beliefs, while still requiring a perfect best response w.r.t. those subjective beliefs.

A key result from Kalai and Lehrer is that for every subjective Nash equilibrium in a repeated game, there exists a Nash equilibrium that induces an identical distribution
\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}
over histories
(
53
, Proposition 1)
. Similarly, for every
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium, there is an
\epsilon
-Nash equilibrium whose history distribution
\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}
is
\epsilon
-close
(
53
, Theorem 1)
. These important results highlight that although the conditionals
\xi^{i}(a_{t}^{-i}|\bar{a}_{<t})
can be different from the ground-truth co-player policies for zero-probability histories
\bar{a}_{<t}
, potentially impacting the optimal planning routine to compute best responses, the resulting best-response policies lead to indistinguishable trajectory distributions
\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}
. Crucially, Kalai and Lehrer only prove this for the case of repeated games with perfect monitoring. As shown in later work by the same authors, this equivalence does
not
hold in the general multi-agent RL setup
(
54
)
.

4.1.2
Convergence of decoupled Bayesian agents in repeated games

We aim to demonstrate that for any
\epsilon>0
, a group of decoupled Bayesian agents play an
\epsilon
-subjective equilibrium after a sufficiently long period
t
. To formalize this, we must explicitly define the environment dynamics and agent’s policies for the new
tail game
that starts at time
t
and continues indefinitely. We define tail games in their general form applicable to MAGRL, and discuss afterwards how it simplifies in the case of repeated games we consider here.

Definition 4.5

(Tail games)
.

Let
\bar{\mu}
be a multi-agent environment, and
(\pi^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
the set of policies of the agents. Let us define the
tail multi-agent environment

\bar{\mu}_{\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}}
and
tail policies

\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
corresponding to the
tail game
starting at timestep
t
induced by joint history
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}
as

\bar{\mu}_{\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}}(\bar{e}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{t:t^{\prime}}\bar{a}_{t^{\prime}+1}):=\bar{\mu}(\bar{e}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}\overline{\text{\ae}}_{t:t^{\prime}}\bar{a}_{t^{\prime}+1})

\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}(a^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\text{\ae}^{i}_{t:t^{\prime}}):=\pi^{i}(a^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i}\text{\ae}_{t:t^{\prime}}^{i})

The corresponding measure over tail trajectories is defined as

\bar{\mu}_{\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}}^{\bar{\pi}}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{t:t^{\prime}}):=\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{t:t^{\prime}}\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t})
. Marginalizing out the co-player trajectories provides us with the tail personal environments

\mu_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
. Similarly, we define the tail mixture environments as

\xi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}(e^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\text{\ae}^{i}_{t:t^{\prime}}a^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1}):=\xi^{i}(e^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1}\mid\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i}\text{\ae}_{t:t^{\prime}}^{i}a^{i}_{t^{\prime}+1})

In repeated games, we assume the agents are given their reward functions, instead of including rewards in the percepts
e^{i}
. Furthermore, we assume perfect monitoring of the actions of others. Hence, personal histories are sequences of joint actions, and the tail personal measures
\big(\mu_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}\big)^{\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}}
are equivalent to the joint tail policies:

\big(\mu_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}\big)^{\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}}(\bar{a}_{t:t^{\prime}})=\prod_{k=t}^{t^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{j}(a^{j}_{k}\mid\bar{a}_{t:k-1})

Building on these concepts, Kalai and Lehrer’s central theorem demonstrates that decoupled Bayesian agents whose beliefs satisfy the grain-of-truth property converge to playing an
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium.

Theorem 4.6

(Convergence to
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium
(
52
)
)
.

Consider a repeated game with perfect monitoring played by decoupled Bayes-optimal agents. If each agent’s subjective model
\xi^{i}
satisfies the grain-of-truth property with respect to the ground-truth personal environment
(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a time
T(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T(\epsilon)
, with
\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-probability greater than 1-
\epsilon
over
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}
, the agents’ tail policies
\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
and tail mixture environments
\xi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
constitute an
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium of the tail game starting at time
t
.

Combined with the equivalence result between
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibria and
\epsilon
-Nash equilibria
(
54
)
, this implies that decoupled Bayes-optimal agents satisfying the grain-of-truth property converge to playing an
\epsilon
-Nash equilibrium. This foundational result establishes a strong link between Bayesian rationality and classical game-theoretic equilibria.

4.1.3
Convergence of Decoupled Bayesian Agents in Multi-Agent General RL

So far, we focused on repeated games with perfect monitoring.
54
broaden the scope to the Multi-Agent General Reinforcement Learning (MAGRL) setup, which accommodates partial observability and more complex environment dynamics. In this richer setting, the percepts
e^{i}
contain both the current reward
r^{i}
and observation
o^{i}
, where the observation can contain (partial) information about the actions of other agents, as well as other information from the environment. A decoupled Bayes-optimal agent in this setting still computes a best response to a subjective model
\xi^{i}
of its environment, but the nature of equilibria and convergence changes significantly. The definitions of the Nash equilibrium (cf. Definition
4.4
) and subjective Nash equilibrium (cf. Definition
4.3
) apply to the MAGRL case as well, but now with no restrictions on the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
, and with
\xi^{i}
a general mixture environment following
Eq.

4
instead of Definition
4.2
.

A crucial distinction from the repeated games setting is the presence of partial observability. Because agents no longer share a common history, their individual observation streams can lead to different but correlated personal histories. Hence, the tail policies
\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
are conditioned on different but correlated histories
\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}
, and thus the tail game starting at time
t
should take such correlated information into account. In contrast, in repeated games, all tail policies are conditioned on the identical history
\bar{a}_{<t}
, and therefore the resulting tail game does not incorporate private and correlated information that the agents are given.

In game theory, private, correlated information that agents are given before the start of a game is formally modeled through private messages
m^{i}
coming from a correlation device
(M,p)
. Here,
M=\prod_{i}M^{i}
is the joint space over individual messages
m^{i}
for each agent, and
p(\bar{m})
is the probability distribution over joint messages
\bar{m}=\{m^{i}\}_{i\in N}
. In our case, considering a tail game starting at time
t
, the messages are the personal histories
\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}
, and the distribution
p
is the ground-truth distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\bar{\pi}}
over joint histories
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}
.

The objective solution concept for games incorporating a correlation device is the correlated equilibrium
6
.

Definition 4.7

(Correlated Equilibria)
.

A correlation device
(M,p)
combined with a set of policies
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
with
\pi^{i}:M^{i}\times(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i})^{*}\to\Delta\mathcal{A}^{i}
form a
correlated equilibrium
in a correlated multi-agent environment
(\bar{\mu},M,p)
if each agent’s policy
\pi^{i}
is a best response w.r.t. the ground-truth personal correlated environment
\mu^{i}(e^{i}_{t}\mid\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i},m^{i})
for each message with
p(m^{i})>0
, with
\mu^{i}(e^{i}_{t}\mid\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i},m^{i})
obtained from appropriately marginalizing and conditioning the joint distribution induced by policies
(\pi^{j})_{j=1}^{N}
, environment
\bar{\mu}
and correlation device
(M,p)
:

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t},\bar{m})=p(\bar{m})\prod_{k=1}^{t}\left(\prod_{i\in N}\pi^{i}(a_{k}^{i}\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<k},m^{i})\right)\bar{\mu}(\bar{e}_{k}\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<k},\bar{a}_{k},\bar{m})

(17)

Bayesian agents compute best responses w.r.t. their subjective beliefs and not necessarily the ground-truth environment, hence we also need a subjective variant of the correlated equilibrium.

Definition 4.8

(Subjective Correlated Equilibria
(
54
)
)
.

A set of policies
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
with
\pi^{i}:M^{i}\times(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i})^{*}\to\Delta\mathcal{A}^{i}
and subjective mixture environments
\{\xi^{i}(e^{i}\mid m^{i},\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}},a^{i})\}_{i\in N}
is a
subjective correlated equilibrium
w.r.t. multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
and correlation device
(M,p)
if the following two conditions hold:

1.

Subjective best response.
Each agent’s policy
\pi^{i}
is a best response w.r.t.
\xi^{i}(e^{i}\mid m^{i},\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}},a^{i})
for each history and message with
p(m^{i})>0
.

2.

Uncontradicted beliefs.
The distribution over histories induced by the agent’s beliefs and policy is identical to the distribution induced by the policies of all agents combined with the ground-truth correlated multi-agent environment
(\overline{\mu},M,p)
:

\displaystyle\big(\xi^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}}\mid m^{i})=\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}}\mid m^{i})\quad\forall i\in N,\forall\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*},\forall m^{i}\in M^{i}:p(m^{i})>0

with
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
obtained from appropriately marginalizing and conditioning the joint distribution
\mu_{\bar{\mu}}^{\bar{\pi}}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t},\bar{m})
(equation
17
).

The
\epsilon
-subjective correlated equilibrium
relaxes the second condition: with probability greater than
1-\epsilon
, a message
\bar{m}
is chosen such that the subjective beliefs
\big(\xi^{i})^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}}\mid m^{i})
are
\epsilon
-close to the personal ground-truth distributions
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}}\mid m^{i})

(
54
)
.

With these concepts in place, we present the central result of
54
for the MAGRL setting: that decoupled Bayesian agents converge not to a subjective Nash, but to a subjective correlated equilibrium.

Theorem 4.9

(Convergence to
\epsilon
-Subjective Correlated Equilibrium
(
54
)
)
.

Consider a MAGRL environment played by decoupled Bayes-optimal agents. If each agent’s subjective model
\xi^{i}
satisfies the grain-of-truth property with respect to the ground-truth personal environment
\mu^{i}
, then for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a time
T
such that for all
t\geq T
, the agents’ tail policies
\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
and tail mixture environments
\xi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
constitute an
\epsilon
-subjective correlated equilibrium in the correlated tail game starting at time
t
with correlation device
(\overline{\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}}^{t-1},\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}})
.

Critically, the equivalence between subjective and objective equilibria
(
53
, Theorem 1)
, which holds in repeated games, breaks down in the general RL setup. In the MAGRL setting, a subjective equilibrium (Nash or correlated) is not generally equivalent to an objective one. The reason lies in the expanded role of the subjective model
\xi^{i}
. In MAGRL,
\xi^{i}
predicts not simply the other agents’ actions but percepts which also include the obtained rewards. An agent’s beliefs about rewards for counterfactual, off-the-play-path histories can be incorrect. For instance, an agent might hold a dogmatic belief that any deviation from its current policy
\pi^{i}
will result in a catastrophically low reward
(
54
;
59
)
. Since this belief is never contradicted by observation as the agent never deviates, this locks the agent into a suboptimal policy that is nonetheless a best response to its flawed subjective environment
\xi^{i}
. This prevents the agent from gathering the very data needed to correct its erroneous beliefs.

In summary, this subsection has charted the foundational results for decoupled Bayesian agents. We showed that in the simplified setting of repeated games, decoupled Bayesian agents under the grain-of-truth assumption converge to objective
\epsilon
-Nash equilibria. However, in the more general and realistic MAGRL setting, convergence is only guaranteed to an
\epsilon
-subjective correlated equilibrium, with no general guarantee of objective optimality due to the challenge of correcting off-path beliefs. With this background established, we will now follow a similar analytical path to study the equilibrium behavior of the
embedded
Bayesian agents introduced in this paper. This will require us to develop novel subjective and objective solution concepts to properly characterize their unique equilibrium properties.

4.2
Equilibrium behavior of embedded Bayesian agents on repeated games

We analyze the equilibrium behavior of embedded Bayesian agents, beginning with the setting of repeated games with perfect monitoring. In this context, an agent’s percepts consist of the other agents’ actions, and its rewards are determined by a known reward function. The core difference from the decoupled case is that embedded agents can maintain
coupled
beliefs over their own policy and the policies of others, a direct consequence of reasoning about structural similarities as discussed in Section 3.6. This allows an agent’s action
a_{t}^{i}
to be evidentially linked to the concurrent actions of other agents,
a_{t}^{-i}
, through the Bayesian belief update
w(\lambda\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t},a_{t}^{i})
. This seemingly subtle shift has important implications for the resulting equilibria.

We first define the specific agent model for this setting. We consider universes that are composed of a joint policy, where each agent acts independently conditioned on the history. The coupling between agents’ actions arises not from the universe’s causal structure, but from each agent’s subjective beliefs
w^{i}(\lambda)
over which universe it inhabits.

Definition 4.10

(Embedded Bayes-optimal agent in repeated games)
.

An embedded Bayes-optimal agent in a repeated game with perfect monitoring is an agent
i
that maintains a joint subjective belief model
\rho^{i}
over a class of universes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
. Each universe
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
is equivalent to a joint policy
\bar{\pi}_{\lambda}
that factorizes over independent agent policies:
\lambda(\bar{a}_{1:t})=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\prod_{i\in[N]}\pi^{i}_{\lambda}(a^{i}_{k}\mid\bar{a}_{<k})
. The agent’s beliefs are captured by the mixture universe
\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{1:t})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w^{i}(\lambda)\lambda(\bar{a}_{1:t})
, which yields the following conditional for the other agents’ actions:

\rho^{i}(a^{-i}_{t}|\bar{a}_{<t},a_{t}^{i}):=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w^{i}(\lambda\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a_{t}^{i})\prod_{j\neq i}\pi_{\lambda}^{j}(a^{j}_{t}\mid\bar{a}_{<t})\,.

The agent’s policy,
\pi^{i}
, is then an embedded best response computed with respect to this subjective mixture universe
\rho^{i}
(cf. equation
10
).

A crucial difference between the mixture universe of embedded Bayesian agents and the mixture environment of decoupled Bayesian agents in repeated games is that in the mixture universe
\rho^{i}(a^{-i}_{t}|\bar{a}_{<t},a_{t}^{i})
, there is a dependence of the other agents’ current actions
a^{-i}_{t}
on the ego-agent’s current action
a_{t}^{i}
. As each universe
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
adheres to a causal structure where there is no dependence between
a^{-i}_{t}
and
a^{i}_{t}
, this dependence in the mixture universe
\rho
is an
informational
dependence and not a
causal dependence
. This informational link arises due to the posterior belief update
w^{i}(\lambda\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a_{t}^{i})
based on the action
a^{i}
, which is used to predict the other agents’ actions
a^{-i}
.
In mixture environments
\xi
of decoupled Bayesian agents in the repeated games setting, there is no dependence between
a^{-i}_{t}
and
a^{i}_{t}
, because the mixture environment can only model causal dependencies of actions, in contrast to mixture universes which both can model causal and informational dependencies on actions (cf. Remark
3.4
).

4.2.1
Subjective solution concepts

The potential for dependencies of the other agents’ current actions
a^{-i}_{t}
on the ego-agent’s current action
a_{t}^{i}
in mixture universes
\rho
, necessitates a solution concept that can accommodate such coupled beliefs. Standard concepts like the Nash equilibrium assume independence. To address this, we introduce the
subjective embedded equilibrium
(SEE), a concept that combines the subjective Nash equilibrium with the idea of a
d
ependency equilibrium, introduced in
102
(cf.
Section

D.3
).

Differently from the case of subjective Nash equilibria, defining exact subjective embedded equilibria requires the solution of a technical challenge. For clarity, we therefore begin with the
\epsilon
-relaxed version, which characterizes the behavior of embedded Bayesian agents at finite times.

Definition 4.11

(
\epsilon
-Subjective Embedded Equilibrium)
.

A set of policies
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
and subjective mixture universes
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in N}
satisfying the grain-of-uncertainty property is an
\epsilon
-
subjective embedded equilibrium
in a repeated game if the following two conditions hold:

1.

Subjective Best Response.
Each agent’s policy
\pi_{i}
is an embedded best response with respect to its subjective mixture universe
\rho^{i}
.

2.

\epsilon
-Uncontradicted Beliefs.
The subjective beliefs
\rho^{i}
are
\epsilon
-close in total variation distance to the personal real-world distribution
(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}
induced by the ground-truth multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
and policies of all agents:
D_{\infty}(\rho^{i},(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}\mid\varepsilon)\leq\epsilon

In the repeated games setting, the multi-agent environment
\bar{\mu}
captures the reward functions of all involved agents, and the agents have perfect knowledge of their own reward function. As we assume perfect monitoring of the other agent’s actions, and the agent’s do not need to predict rewards, we have that the percepts are
e^{i}:=a^{-i}
and the agent’s personal histories are sequences of joint actions. Hence, the ground-truth personal distribution corresponds to the distribution induces by all agents:

(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}(\bar{a}_{1:t})=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\pi^{j}(a^{j}_{k}\mid\bar{a}_{<k})\,.

This leads to our main convergence result for embedded agents in repeated games: under the grain-of-truth assumption, embedded agents converge to playing an
\epsilon
-SEE.

Theorem 4.12

(Convergence to
\epsilon
-Subjective Embedded Equilibrium)
.

Let
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in N}
be Bayesian mixture universes satisfying the grain-of-uncertainty and grain-of-truth conditions w.r.t.
(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}
, and let
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
be the corresponding embedded best response policies in an infinitely repeated game with perfect monitoring. Then, for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a finite time
T(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T(\epsilon)
, with
\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-probability greater than
1-\epsilon
over
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}
, the agents’ tail policies
\pi^{i}_{\bar{a}_{<t}}
and tail mixture universes
\rho_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}
constitute an
\epsilon
-subjective embedded equilibrium of the tail game starting at time
t
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.2
.
∎

Example 4.13

(Convergence to a Cooperative
\epsilon
-SEE)
.

To illustrate how embedded Bayesian agents can converge to a cooperative
\epsilon
-SEE, we consider a two-player iterated Prisoner’s Dilemma with discount
\gamma=0
. The zero discount makes each round strategically equivalent to a one-shot game, while the history allows for belief updates. To construct the agent’s prior over universes, we first start from a prior over policies, which we combine into a prior over universes. Consider a countable class of deterministic policies
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and a prior
\tilde{w}(\pi)>0
for all
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
. We further assume that the class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
is large enough so that, for every
t
and every history
\bar{a}_{1:t}
of length
t
, there exists a pair of policies in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
which act according to
\bar{a}_{1:t}
for the first
t
rounds, and then either cooperate forever after or defect forever after.
28
28

28

In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
contains a policy which always cooperates and a policy which always defects.
This will ensure that the grain-of-uncertainty property is satisfied.

The agents’ prior over universes,
w^{i}(\lambda)
, is constructed as a combination of two hypotheses:

w^{i}(\lambda):=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\hat{w}^{i}(\pi^{i},\pi^{-i})\delta(\lambda^{\pi^{i}}_{\pi^{-i}}=\lambda)\,,

where

\hat{w}^{i}(\pi^{i},\pi^{-i}):=\alpha\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\delta(\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i})+(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\tilde{w}(\pi^{-i})\,.

Here,
\alpha\in[0,1]
is the agent’s prior belief in the "other agent is an identical copy" hypothesis (
\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i}
), while
1-\alpha
is the corresponding prior belief in the “other agent is an independent draw from
\tilde{w}
” hypothesis. This prior structure is motivated by Occam’s razor, as a universe with identical agents is algorithmically simpler to describe.

Since both embedded Bayes-optimal agents have the same priors, they implement the same policy. However, they don’t know up front that they are identical copies, and need to infer this from their interactions. Let us assume that the EBR policy breaks ties in a fixed canonical way.
29
29

29

E.g., if an agent finds itself in a situation where the
Q
value associated with cooperating is exactly the same as the one associated with defecting, then the agent defects by default. We could also make the default action to be cooperation.
This makes the EBR policy deterministic, and hence the same deterministic EBR policy is followed by both agents. Since the game is symmetric and since both agents follow the same deterministic policy, we can see that they will produce a trajectory in such a way that in each round the agents take the same action, i.e., they either both cooperate or both defect. Their actions may change from one round to another, but it will always be the case that they take the same action in the same round.

It is not yet clear whether the deterministic EBR policy belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
, but we will show that in the considered setup, the two embedded Bayesian agents will either always defect or converge to cooperating, which would then imply that the grain-of-truth property is satisfied because such policies are part of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
.

To show that both embedded Bayesian agents can converge to mutual cooperation, let us first define

m(\bar{a}_{1:t}):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi)\prod_{k=1}^{t}\pi(a^{1}_{k}\mid\bar{a}_{<k})\,,

which is the prior probability that a policy drawn from
\tilde{w}(\pi)
produces the actions within
\bar{a}_{1:t}
, when it is used as policy for agent 1.
30
30

30

In the considered setting both agents have an identical and deterministic ground-truth policy, hence we have that
a_{t}^{1}=a_{t}^{2}
for all timesteps
t
. Hence the definition of
m(\bar{a}_{1:t})
is invariant on whether we assume the policies are used by agent 1 or agent 2.
For every
k\geq 0
, define

m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}=m((D,D)^{k})\,,

where we adopt the convention that
m_{0}^{\textrm{defect}}=m((D,D)^{0})=m(\varepsilon)=1
, and

m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}m((D,D)^{k})\,.

m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}
is the prior probability that a policy drawn from
\tilde{w}(\pi)
always defects, in case the opponent defects as well.

If the prior belief in structural similarity is sufficiently strong (
\alpha>\frac{m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}
), then after some finite time
T
, the accumulated evidence that both agents are identical, i.e., the history of identical actions, will outweigh the alternative hypothesis that the two agents are different but happened to produce the same actions up until time
T
. As a consequence, after this time
T
, an agent’s choice to cooperate provides strong evidence that its opponent will do likewise, making cooperation the subjectively optimal action (cf.
Section

D.4
for a detailed explanation). In fact, we can characterize
T
as being the smallest
k
for which
\alpha>\frac{m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}
, and we can further show that the embedded Bayes-optimal agents defect up until time
T
after which they cooperate forever after. Since such policies belong to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
, we can see that the grain-of-truth property is satisfied. Refer to
Section

D.4
for further details.

As the agents’ mixture universes with priors
w^{i}
satisfy the grain-of-truth property, we have that for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a finite time
t(\epsilon)
for which the mixture universe is
\epsilon
-close to the ground-truth distribution (cf.
Theorem

3.11
). Hence, the agents converge to the
\epsilon
-subjective embedded equilibrium of mutual cooperation.

On the flip side, we can show that when the prior belief in structural similarity is too weak, i.e.,
\alpha\leq\frac{m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}
, then the embedded Bayesian agents always defect, which is also an
\epsilon
-subjective embedded equilibrium. Hence, the choice of prior (through
\alpha
) is an important variable determining the equilibrium behavior of the embedded Bayesian agents. When taking an Occam’s razor prior following the
minimum description length
line of thought, this would motivate a large
\alpha
, as universes containing two identical agents have roughly half the description length of a universe containing two different agents of similar complexity, and hence result in cooperative behavior among identical embedded Bayesian agents.

\diamondsuit

We now consider the exact, non-
\epsilon
version of the subjective embedded equilibrium (SEE) that characterizes the asymptotic limit of play, but we first must address a technical challenge. Agents’ policies may be deterministic. If an agent’s subjective model
\rho^{i}
converges to reflect this deterministic policy in the limit of time to infinity, it will assign zero probability to certain actions, violating the grain-of-uncertainty assumption. This is problematic because the embedded best response calculation requires well-defined conditionals
\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{1:t},a^{i})
for
all
actions
a^{i}
, including those that will have zero probability according to the resulting embedded best response policy. To resolve this, we introduce the concept of a
conditional completion
of a predictive model, which defines these necessary counterfactual beliefs as the limit of a sequence of models that
do
satisfy the grain-of-uncertainty property. Note that while this section is about the repeated games with perfect-monitoring setting, the following definition is stated in the more general MAGRL setting, for later reuse.

Definition 4.14

(Conditional Completion)
.

A conditional completion of a measure
\lambda(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})
consists of the measure
\lambda(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})
itself, along with a set of conditionals
\lambda(e\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t},a)
for all
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}\in\overline{\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}}^{*}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and
e\in\mathcal{E}
, derived as follows:

1.

There exists a sequence of measures
\{\lambda_{r}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})\}_{r\in\mathbb{N}}
, each satisfying the grain-of-uncertainty property, that converges to
\lambda
, i.e.,
\lim_{r\to\infty}\lambda_{r}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})=\lambda(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})
.

2.

The completed conditionals are defined by the limit:
31
31

31

We assume the series
\{\lambda_{r}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{1:t})\}_{r\in\mathbb{N}}
is suitably chosen such that this limit exists.

\lambda(e\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t},a):=\lim_{r\to\infty}\lambda_{r}(e\mid\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t},a)
.

In repeated games with perfect monitoring, where
e^{i}=a^{-i}
, the conditional completion specifies an agent’s beliefs about what its opponents would do in response to a counterfactual action, even if the agent assigns zero probability to taking that action itself. This allows us to formally define the SEE.

Definition 4.15

(Subjective Embedded Equilibrium)
.

A set of policies
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
and subjective predictive distributions
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in N}
, each with a specified conditional completion, constitutes a
subjective embedded equilibrium
if:

1.

Subjective Best Response.
Each agent’s policy
\pi_{i}
is an embedded best response with respect to
\rho^{i}
and its completion.

2.

Uncontradicted Beliefs.
The agent’s predictive distributions
\rho^{i}(\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i})
are identical to the ground-truth personal distribution
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}_{<t}^{i})
induced by the multi-agent environment
\overline{\mu}
and policies of all agents.

The SEE concept can rationalize behaviors unattainable under classical Nash equilibria, for example cooperation in the twin prisoner’s dilemma, as detailed below.

Example 4.16

(Cooperation in the Twin Prisoner’s Dilemma)
.

Consider two embedded agents playing a single-round Prisoner’s Dilemma with actions
C
and
D
, indicating ‘cooperating’ and ‘defecting’ respectively. Let their subjective models
\rho^{i}
be the limit of a sequence
\rho^{i}_{r}
where
\rho^{i}_{r}(\text{C},\text{C})=1-\epsilon_{r}
and
\rho^{i}_{r}(\text{D},\text{D})=\epsilon_{r}
, with
\epsilon_{r}\to 0
. Such an agent believes "I cooperate if and only if my opponent cooperates." Given this belief, cooperation is the rational best response. Since both agents cooperate, their belief that mutual cooperation occurs with probability 1 is not contradicted by reality. Thus, mutual cooperation is a subjective embedded equilibrium.

\diamondsuit

Let us revisit
Example

4.13
, and consider the tail game of length one of a single round of the prisoner’s dilemma after timestep
t
.
Example

4.13
showed that for large enough
\alpha
, both agents end up cooperating forever after some finite time
T
. Hence, the tail policies
\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
of both agents converge to pure cooperation, and their predictive models converge to the ground-truth distribution. As a consequence, in the limit of time to infinity, the agents not only converge to an
\epsilon
-SEE, but also to the SEE covered in
Example

4.16
.

The subjective Nash equilibrium is a special case of the SEE: when an agent’s beliefs are decoupled, an SEE is also a subjective Nash equilibrium.

Proposition 4.17
.

If a set of policies
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
and subjective models
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
form a subjective embedded equilibrium where the completed conditionals are decoupled, i.e.,
\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{1:t},a^{i})=\prod_{j\neq i}\rho^{i}(a^{j}\mid\bar{a}_{1:t})
, then this also constitutes a subjective Nash equilibrium. Consequently, there exists a Nash equilibrium that induces the same distribution over histories.

Proof.

See
Section

C.3

∎

The above result also holds for the
\epsilon
-variants (cf.
Section

C.3
).

This proposition leads to an important corollary: When embedded agents are endowed with decoupled beliefs, they are behaviorally equivalent to decoupled Bayes-optimal agents (cf.
Proposition

3.17
) and hence their long-term behavior recovers the classical results of Kalai and Lehrer (cf.
Theorem

4.6
). This serves as a crucial sanity check, demonstrating that our framework correctly subsumes the decoupled case.

Corollary 4.18

(Convergence of embedded Bayes-optimal agents with decoupled beliefs to
\epsilon
-Nash)
.

Consider an infinitely repeated game with perfect monitoring, and embedded Bayes-optimal agents starting from decoupled prior beliefs
w(\lambda)
, i.e., there exists a probability measure
\tilde{w}^{i}\in\Delta(\prod_{j}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{j})
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
it holds that

w^{i}(\lambda)=\sum_{\overline{\pi}\in\prod_{j}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{j}}\tilde{w}^{i}(\overline{\pi})\delta\left(\lambda=(\nu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}\right),\quad\text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{w}^{i}(\overline{\pi})=\prod_{j\in N}\tilde{w}^{i}(\pi^{j})\,.

with
\delta(\lambda=(\nu^{i})^{\pi^{i}})
the indicator function specifying whether
\lambda
is equal to the personal universe
(\nu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}
of agent
i
originating from marginalizing the joint universe
\bar{\nu}^{\bar{\pi}}
induced by
\overline{\pi}
. If their beliefs satisfy the grain-of-truth and grain-of-uncertainty properties, the agents converge to playing an
\epsilon
-subjective embedded equilibrium which, by
Proposition

4.17
, is also an
\epsilon
-subjective Nash equilibrium.

53
shows equivalence between
\epsilon
-subjective Nash and
\epsilon
-Nash equilibria. Therefore, for any
\epsilon>0
, their exists a time
T(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T(\epsilon)
, with
\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-probability greater than
1-\epsilon
over
\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}
, the trajectory distribution
\bar{\mu}_{\overline{\text{\ae}}_{<t}}^{\overline{\pi}}
induced by the tail policies of the agents are
\epsilon
-close to an
\epsilon
-Nash equilibrium in the tail game starting from time
t
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.4

∎

4.2.2
Objective solution concepts

In the preceding section, we developed the subjective embedded equilibrium to characterize the convergence behavior of embedded agents. However, just as the subjective Nash equilibrium has its objective counterpart in the classical Nash equilibrium, it is natural to seek an objective solution concept for the embedded setting. For decoupled agents, defining an objective best response is straightforward: An agent optimizes its policy against a fixed, external environment composed of the other agents’ true policies. In the embedded framework, this distinction blurs. An agent is part of the universe it inhabits, so a change to its own policy is a change to the universe itself. Due to potential structural similarities with other agents, modifying the "agent part" of the universe could fundamentally alter the "environment part", making the notion of an objective best response more subtle.

As a warmup to formalize the embedded equilibrium, let us first revisit the Nash equilibrium from the embedded agent point of view using universes instead of environments. We start from the ground-truth personal universe
\upsilon^{i}=\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
induced by the policies of all agents. A Nash equilibrium then requires that each policy
\pi^{i}
is a best response against the true policies of the other agents. In the repeated games setting, this best response is computed by planning using the conditionals
\upsilon^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
. However, for histories containing actions
a^{i}
that have zero probability under the agent’s policy
\pi^{i}
(i.e., off the play-path), the conditional
\upsilon^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
is undefined. The Nash equilibrium concept resolves this by implicitly making the
decoupled agency assumption
: the policies of other agents do not depend on the agent’s own policy. It therefore uses the conditionals from the personal environment,
\mu^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})=\prod_{j\neq i}\pi^{j}(a^{j}\mid\bar{a}_{<t})
, where the action
a^{i}
has no effect on the independently chosen, concurrent actions of the other agents.

The embedded equilibrium (EE), which we will introduce formally in a moment, does not make this decoupled agency assumption, but instead allows for structural dependencies. When the planning procedure contemplates an action
a^{i}
that has zero probability under
\pi^{i}
, it means that this action is incompatible with the currently considered universe
\upsilon
. Instead of assuming that the ego-policy
\pi^{i}
can be changed without changing the rest of the universe, the EE takes structural dependencies into account, which may be imposed by an ‘external designer’ or a natural process. For example, an AI researcher might conduct an experiment with a ‘self-play’ constraint where all agents must share the same policy parameters, or replicators in nature might interact with kin sharing a large part of their genes.

We formalize this by using conditionals
q(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
originating from an externally provided
dependency distribution

q
whenever the universe conditionals
\upsilon^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
are undefined. This allows for the policies of other agents sharing structural similarities to change when one deviates from one’s own policy. This corresponds to a specific conditional completion of the ground-truth universe
\upsilon
, using the dependency distribution
q
.

Explicitly constructing this conditional completion provides further insight. The dependency distribution
q
incorporates the structural relations. It can be constructed as follows: the external designer or natural process defines a class of "allowable universes,"
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\textrm{allowed}}
, containing only those possible universes where the appropriate structural relations hold. Then it introduces a prior
w(\lambda)
over these universes, inducing a mixture distribution
q:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\textrm{allowed}}}w(\lambda)\lambda
. The conditionals
q(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
then encode, for a given history and action, the likely continuations of the trajectories in allowable universes that are compatible with that history and action.

The conditional completion of the ground-truth universe
\upsilon
can now be constructed as the limit of a sequence
(\upsilon_{r})_{r}
:

\upsilon_{r}:=(1-\epsilon_{r})\upsilon+\epsilon_{r}q,\quad\text{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \lim_{r\to\infty}\epsilon_{r}=0\,,

leading to the following completed conditionals:

\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}):=\lim_{r\to\infty}\upsilon_{r}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})=\begin{cases}\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ \upsilon(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})>0\,,\\
q(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

where the term
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
on the left-hand side is the completed conditional, whereas the term
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
on the right-hand side (for the case
\upsilon(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})>0
) is the conditional from the original ground-truth measure
\upsilon
. As can be seen, the conditional
q(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
helps extend the definition of
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})
to the cases where
\upsilon(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})=0
.

This construction clarifies the reasoning of an agent in an EE. When contemplating an action
a^{i}
that is supported by its equilibrium policy
\pi^{i}
(on the play-path), the agent anticipates the future using the universe
\upsilon
representing the equilibrium policies of all agents involved. However, when contemplating in its planning procedure a deviation to an action with zero probability (off the play-path), the agent reasons that this action implies the world must be governed by a different universe than
\upsilon
. It therefore uses the dependency distribution
q
