f^{i}(\bar{a}_{*}):=\begin{cases}f^{i}_{l}(\bar{a}_{*})&\text{if }l(\bar{a}_{*})<l\\
\pi^{i}(\bar{a}_{*})&\text{if }l(\bar{a}_{*})\geq l\end{cases}

We verify the two conditions for
f
:
(i)
(\pi^{i})_{i}
play
\epsilon
-like
(f^{i})_{i}
: As
(\pi^{i}_{l})_{i}
play
\epsilon
-like
(f_{l}^{i})_{i}
in the
l
-fold game, and
f^{i}
and
\pi^{i}
are identical for all histories
\bar{a}_{*}
with
l(\bar{a}_{*})\geq l
, the induced distributions
\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
and
\overline{\mu}^{\bar{f}}
are
\epsilon
-close.
(ii)
(f^{i})_{i}
is an
\epsilon
-EE: We check that
f^{i}
is an
\epsilon
-best response. Since
(f_{l}^{i})_{i}
is an
\epsilon/2
-EE, any deviation in the first
l
periods yields a payoff gain of at most
\epsilon/2
during those periods. The maximum possible gain from any action taken after period
l
is bounded by
\epsilon/2
. Therefore, the total possible gain from any deviation from
f^{i}
is at most
\epsilon/2+\epsilon/2=\epsilon
. Thus,
(f^{i})_{i}
is an
\epsilon
-EE, with dependency distribution
q
having conditionals equal to the conditional completion of the common mixture
\rho
.
∎

We now state and prove the main theorem.

Theorem C.9

(Convergence to
\epsilon
-Embedded Equilibrium)
.

Let
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in N}
be Bayesian mixture universes satisfying the grain-of-uncertainty and grain-of-truth conditions, and let
\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in N}
be the corresponding embedded best response policies in an infinitely repeated game with perfect monitoring. If the mixtures
\{\rho^{i}\}_{i\in N}
of all the players are the same mixture, i.e.,
\rho^{i}=\rho^{j}

\forall i,j\in N
, then, for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a finite time
T(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T(\epsilon)
, with probability greater than
1-\epsilon
, the tail distribution
\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}}_{\bar{a}_{<t}}
induced by the tail policies
\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}
is
\epsilon
-close to the distribution induced by some policies constituting an
\epsilon
-embedded equilibrium in the tail game starting at time
t
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given.
From Lemma
C.8
, there exists an
\bar{\eta}>0
(which depends on
\epsilon
) such that any
\eta
-SEE with
\eta\leq\bar{\eta}
plays
\epsilon
-like an
\epsilon
-EE.
Let
\eta^{\prime}=\min(\epsilon,\bar{\eta})
.
As the mixture universes
\rho^{i}
satisfy grain of truth and grain of uncertainty, and as all agents share the same mixture, it follows by Theorem
4.12
(Convergence to
\epsilon
-SEE) that for
\eta^{\prime}>0
, there exists a finite time
T(\eta^{\prime})
such that for all
t\geq T(\eta^{\prime})
, with probability
\geq 1-\eta^{\prime}
, the agents’ tail play
(\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i},\rho_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i})
constitutes an
\eta^{\prime}
-SEE, where all agents have an equal tail mixture
\rho_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i}=\rho_{\bar{a}_{<t}}
.
Since
\eta^{\prime}\leq\bar{\eta}
, Lemma
C.8
applies to the tail play
(\pi_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i},\rho_{\bar{a}_{<t}}^{i})
. Therefore, the tail distribution
\bar{\mu}^{\bar{\pi}_{\bar{a}_{<t}}}_{\bar{a}_{<t}}
is
\epsilon
-close to the distribution induced by some policies
(f^{i})_{i}
constituting an
\epsilon
-EE for the tail game.
Furthermore, since
\eta^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
, the probability of this event,
1-\eta^{\prime}
, is
\geq 1-\epsilon
. This completes the proof.
∎

C.7
Proof of
Theorem

4.28

(i) As
\rho^{i}
satisfies the grain-of-uncertainty assumption, its conditionals are always well-defined and hence its conditional completion is unique, and as a result there exists an embedded best response w.r.t.
\rho^{i}
that is equal to
\pi^{i}
. (ii) From
Theorem

3.11
, it follows from the grain-of-truth property that the tail mixture universes
\rho_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
converge to the tail ground-truth distribution
\big(\mu_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}\big)^{\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}}
as
t\to\infty

\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-almost-surely. It follows that for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a finite time
T(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T(\epsilon)
, with probability greater than
1-\epsilon
, we have that
D_{\infty}(\rho^{i},\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\leq\epsilon
. (iii) The tail policies and tail environment are conditioned on the personal histories
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
, which can differ for each agent due to partial observability; hence these histories serve as a correlation device
((\overline{\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}})^{t-1},\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}})
for the subsequent tail policies, leading to an
\epsilon
-subjective correlated equilibrium instead of an
\epsilon
-subjective embedded equilibrium.

C.8
Proof of
Proposition

4.29

We follow an approach inspired by the
dogmatic beliefs
introduced by
59
. We will construct for each agent
i
a mixture model
\rho^{i}
with completed conditionals that predicts eternal rewards of 0 after deviating at least once from the deterministic policy
\pi^{i}
, such that following the policy
\pi^{i}
is always an embedded best response according to equation
10
(in the worst-case,
\pi^{i}
also results in an expected future return of 0, but then
\pi^{i}
still qualifies as an embedded best response, as all alternatives also lead to an expected return of 0).

In the following, we assume without loss of generality that the minimum reward in
\mathcal{R}
is equal to 0. Let us define the finite set
\mathcal{E}^{i}_{0}
as the set of percepts encoding a reward of 0:

\mathcal{E}^{i}_{0}:=\{e^{i}\in\mathcal{E}^{i}:r^{i}(e^{i})=0\}.

Let us define the function
\mathrm{dev}:\big(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i}\big)^{*}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{i}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}
that computes the first timestep
t
where the action
a^{i}_{t}
in history
\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}
deviates from the action prescribed by the deterministic policy
\pi^{i}
. If
\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}
does not deviate from
\pi^{i}
, then we let
\mathrm{dev}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i},\pi^{i}):=l(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i})+1
.
Now let us introduce the following two measures over
\big(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i}\big)^{*}\cup\big(\big(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i}\big)^{*}\times\mathcal{A}^{i}\big)
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{t=1}^{\mathrm{dev}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i},\pi^{i})-1}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}^{i}||\mathcal{E}^{i}|}\prod_{t=\mathrm{dev}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i},\pi^{i})}^{l(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i})}\frac{\delta(e^{i}_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{i})}{|\mathcal{A}^{i}||\mathcal{E}^{i}_{0}|}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}a^{i})

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}^{i}|}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textrm{random}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}^{i})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left(|\mathcal{A}^{i}||\mathcal{E}^{i}|\right)^{t}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textrm{random}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}^{i}a^{i})

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{\textrm{random}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}^{i})\frac{1}{|\mathcal{A}^{i}|}\,,

with
\delta(e^{i}_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{i})
the indicator function indicating whether
e^{i}_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{i}
. Hence, for histories
\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}
where there is at least 1 action deviating from the one prescribed by
\pi^{i}
,
\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}
only assigns non-zero probability to histories that have 0 reward after deviating for the first time from
\pi^{i}
.

Take
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}(\text{\ae}_{*}^{i})
as the personal trajectory distribution resulting from marginalizing out the
\text{\ae}_{*}^{-i}
in the ground-truth joint-trajectory distribution
\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}(\overline{\text{\ae}}_{*})
. Now we introduce the following infinite sequence of
\{\rho^{i}_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{N}}
to obtain the conditional completions of
\rho^{i}

\rho_{r}^{i}:=(1-\epsilon_{r}-\epsilon_{r}^{2})\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}+\epsilon_{r}\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}+\epsilon_{r}^{2}\lambda_{\textrm{random}}\,,

with
\lim_{r\to\infty}\epsilon_{r}=0
. It is easy to see that
\rho_{r}^{i}
satisfies the grain-of-uncertainty condition and hence in the limit defines a valid conditional completion. Furthermore, we have that
\lim_{r\to\infty}\rho_{r}^{i}=\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
, and hence the resulting
\rho^{i}
satisfies the
uncontradicted beliefs
condition for the subjective embedded equilibrium (
Definition

4.15
). It remains to be shown that
\pi^{i}
is an embedded best response w.r.t.
\rho^{i}
for all agents
i
.

Consider a history
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
with
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})>0
, i.e., all the actions follow
\pi^{i}
. Now consider that at time
t
, the action
a^{i}_{t}
deviates from the deterministic policy
\pi^{i}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})
. In this case,
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})=0
and hence the predictive distribution
\rho_{r}^{i}
is equal to

\rho_{r}^{i}(e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})=\frac{\epsilon_{r}\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i}e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t})+\epsilon_{r}^{2}\lambda_{\textrm{random}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i}e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t})}{\epsilon_{r}\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})+\epsilon_{r}^{2}\lambda_{\textrm{random}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})},

and hence

\lim_{r\to\infty}\rho_{r}^{i}(e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})=\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}(e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})\,,

for any future trajectory
e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}
. Notice that
\lambda_{\textrm{dogmatic}}(e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})
only has non-zero probability for future trajectories
e_{t}^{i}\text{\ae}^{i}_{>t}
that have a total sum of rewards of 0. Hence, after deviating once from
\pi^{i}
, the value of any policy is 0, including the optimal policy. Hence, following the deterministic policy
\pi^{i}
is always better or equal to following any other policy, as following another policy leads to the minimum value of 0. Hence
\pi^{i}
is an embedded best response w.r.t.
\rho^{i}=\lim_{r\to\infty}\rho^{i}_{r}
, and hence
(\pi^{i},\rho^{i})_{i}
is an SEE. We can repeat the same proof technique for constructing a SNE
(\pi^{i},\xi^{i})_{i}
, taking
\xi^{i}(e^{i}\mid\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}a^{i})=\rho^{i}(e^{i}\mid\text{\ae}_{*}^{i}a^{i})
for the above constructed
\rho^{i}
. This concludes the proof.

C.9
Proof of
Theorem

4.31

We need a few lemmas, which we state in the single-agent setting for simplicity.

Lemma C.10
.

For all
k\geq 1
, we have
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq Q_{\rho}^{k+1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
.

Proof.

This is an immediate corollary from equation
11
that we prove by induction on
k\geq 1
:

•

For
k=1
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=Q_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\sum_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\rho(a_{t+1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})Q_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a)\right]

\displaystyle=Q_{\rho}^{2}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t}),

hence the lemma is correct for
k=1
.

•

Let
k>1
, and assume that the lemma is true for
k-1
. We have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a)\right]

\displaystyle=Q_{\rho}^{k+1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the induction hypothesis.

It follows by induction that the lemma holds for all
k\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma C.11
.

If
\pi
is a
k
-step planning policy w.r.t. the mixture universe
\rho
, then for every history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
we have

V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\geq\max_{a}Q_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\geq V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

Proof.

In the light of
Lemma

C.10
, it suffices to show the first inequality.

From
Definition

3.9
we have

\pi(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\,.

Therefore,

\sum_{a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\,.

(35)

Now from
Lemma

C.10
and equation
11
we have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\leq Q_{\rho}^{k+1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a)\right]

(36)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\sum_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{t})+\gamma\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t}a_{t+1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})

where
(\ast)
follows from applying equation
35
to the history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}
instead of
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, and
(\dagger)
follows from the definition of
\rho^{\pi}
.

We will now show by induction on
\ell\geq 1
that

\displaystyle\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

(37)

\displaystyle+\gamma^{\ell}\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1}a_{t+\ell}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\,.

By combining equation
35
and equation
36
we get

\displaystyle\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\left((1-\gamma)\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{t})+\gamma\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t}a_{t+1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}}r(e_{t})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})+\gamma\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t},a_{t+1})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t}a_{t+1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t},a_{t+1})\,,

where
(\ddagger)
follows from the definition of
\rho^{\pi}
and the convention that
\text{\ae}_{t:t}=a_{t}e_{t}
. Therefore, equation
37
holds for
\ell=1
.

Now let
\ell>1
and assume that equation
37
holds for
\ell-1
. We have

\displaystyle\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma^{\ell-1}\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2},a_{t+\ell-1})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{\ell-1}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2}a_{t+\ell-1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2},a_{t+\ell-1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\diamond)}}{{\leq}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma^{\ell-1}\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2},a_{t+\ell-1})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{\ell-1}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2}a_{t+\ell-1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\Bigg(

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(1-\gamma)\sum_{e_{t+\ell-1}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t+\ell-1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2}a_{t+\ell-1})r(e_{t+\ell-1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\gamma\sum_{e_{t+\ell-1}\in\mathcal{E},a_{t+\ell}\in\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(e_{t+\ell-1}a_{t+\ell}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2}a_{t+\ell-1})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2}a_{t+\ell-1}e_{t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\Bigg)

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma^{\ell}\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1}a_{t+\ell}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\,,

where
(\diamond)
follows from applying equation
36
to
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-2},a_{t+\ell-1})
. We conclude that equation
37
holds for every
\ell\geq 1
. In particular,

\displaystyle\max_{a}Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma^{\ell}\sum_{(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}}\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1}a_{t+\ell}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}\sum_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i+1}}r(e_{t+i})\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{t:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,

where in the above equation,
(\ast)
follows from the fact that
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1},a_{t+\ell})\in[0,1]
and
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\gamma^{\ell}=0
, and
(\dagger)
follows from the definition of the value function (cf.
Definition

B.31
).
∎

We also need the following useful lemma from
64
:

Lemma C.12

(
64
, Lemma 4.17)
.

For any two environments
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
, and for any two policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, we have that

|V_{\nu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\nu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})|\leq D_{\infty}(\nu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\nu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\,.

Now we prove an analogous statement of
Theorem

4.31
for the single-agent setting.

Lemma C.13
.

Let
\pi
be the policy of an embedded Bayesian agent in environment
\mu
, implementing a
k
-step-planning policy using Bayesian mixture universe
\rho
, satisfying the grain of truth and grain-of-uncertainty conditions, as well as the conditions that
\rho
dominates
\rho^{\pi}
and
\rho^{\pi}
dominates
\mu^{\pi}
, and the following sensibly off-policy condition: There exists a positive
\alpha<1/\gamma-1
and a
T
such that for all
t\geq T
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}\sum_{e}\rho(e\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}a)[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)]\right]\leq(1+\alpha)\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)}\left[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)\right]\,.

Then it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty\,,

with
V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
the optimal value resulting from an embedded best-response w.r.t.
\rho
.

Proof.

We follow the approach of
12
, combining their Theorem 16 with Theorem 18, while generalizing the proof to
k
-step planning. We have

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}D_{\infty}(\rho^{\pi},\rho\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from
Lemma

C.11
as
\pi
is a
k
-step planner policy, and
(\dagger)
follows from
Lemma

C.12
. As from the stated assumptions we have that
\rho
dominates
\rho^{\pi}
, we have from the merging of opinions theorem
(
8
)
that
D_{\infty}(\rho,\rho^{\pi}\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\to 0
as
t\to\infty

\rho^{\pi}
-almost surely. Hence, for any
\beta>0
, there exists a
t_{1}
such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]

(38)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[D_{\infty}(\rho^{\pi},\rho\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\leq\beta\,,

where for
(\dagger)
we used the inequality
V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\geq\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)\geq V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
from
Lemma

C.11
.
Now let us proceed by showing that for self-models that are good models of the
k
-step planning policy, i.e., that satisfy the above inequality, we have that the resulting value is close to the optimal value corresponding to infinite-horizon optimal planning w.r.t.
\rho
, when assuming that the
sensibly off-policy
condition holds. Let us define the optimality gap as

\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}):=V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\,,

with
V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
the optimal value resulting from infinite-horizon optimal planning w.r.t.
\rho
.
Take
t\geq\max(T,t_{1})
with
T
defined in the Lemma statement, and
t_{1}
defined for equation
38
. Then we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}Q^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)+\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}Q^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}Q^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-\max_{a}Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)\right]+\beta

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}(Q^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)-Q_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a))\right]+\beta

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\max_{a}\gamma\mathbb{E}_{\rho(e\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}},a)}\left[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)\right]\right]+\beta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{\leq}}(1+\alpha)\gamma\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)}\left[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)-V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)\right]+\beta

\displaystyle=(1+\alpha)\gamma\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)}\left[\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}ae)\right]+\beta\,,

where in
(\dagger)
we used equation
38
and in
(\ddagger)
the sensibly off-policy condition. As the above holds for any
t\geq\max(T,t_{1})
, we can recursively apply it and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\leq\beta\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}[(1+\alpha)\gamma]^{l}=\frac{\beta}{1-(1+\alpha)\gamma}\,,

where we used the assumption that
\alpha<1/\gamma-1
.

As
\rho^{\pi}
dominates
\mu^{\pi}
(cf. the stated assumptions), we have for some finite constant
C
that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\leq\frac{1}{C}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[\Delta(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\leq\frac{\beta}{C(1-(1+\alpha)\gamma)}\,.

Finally, as
V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\geq V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\geq V_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
, and as the above holds for any
\beta>0
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})}\left[V^{*}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})-V_{\rho^{\pi}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\right]\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad t\to\infty\,.

∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

4.31
.

Proof.

(i) Using
Lemma

C.13
, we have that under the stated conditions, the agent’s
k
-step planner policies converge to infinite-horizon optimal planning w.r.t.
\rho^{i}
in
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
-mean. As convergence in mean implies convergence in probability for bounded random variables, we have that for all
\delta>0
, the probability that the policy
\pi^{i}
is a
\delta
-embedded best response w.r.t. subjective environment
\rho^{i}
for the history
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
, converges to 1:

\mathbb{P}_{(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}}\left[V^{*}_{\rho^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})-V_{{(\rho^{i})}^{\pi^{i}}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\leq\delta\right]\to 1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{as}\penalty\ \penalty\ t\to\infty\,,

with
\mathbb{P}_{(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}}
the probability distribution of
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
under
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
. (ii) As
\rho^{i}
dominates
\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}
, we have from
Theorem

3.11
that the tail mixtures
\rho_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
converge to the tail ground-truth distribution
\big(\mu_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}\big)^{\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}}
as
t\to\infty

\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-almost-surely. Almost-sure convergence implies convergence in probability, hence we have that

\mathbb{P}_{(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}}\left[D_{\infty}(\rho^{i},\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\leq\epsilon\right]\to 1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{as}\penalty\ \penalty\ t\to\infty.

(iii) The policies and environment are conditioned on the history, which can differ for each agent due to partial observability; hence these histories serve as a correlation device
(\overline{\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}}^{t-1},\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}})
for the subsequent tail policies.
∎

C.10
Proof of
Proposition

4.33

We first prove that the policy of an approximate embedded Bayesian agent with growing planning horizons and improving accuracy has a value that converges to the optimal value with respect to the subjective environment induced by the mixture universe:

Proposition C.14
.

Let
\pi
be the policy of an embedded
(k_{t},\epsilon_{t})
-approximate Bayes-optimal agent with respect to a mixture universe model
\rho
. If
\lim_{t\to\infty}k_{t}=\infty
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\epsilon_{t}=0
, it holds that

\lim_{t\to\infty}V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\lim_{t\to\infty}V^{*}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

Proof.

First notice that for every action
a_{t}
, we have

Q_{\rho}^{k_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-\gamma^{k_{t}}\,.

The reason is that the non-optimality of a planner with a horizon of
k_{t}
steps will only come from the contribution of the discounted rewards after
k_{t}
steps, which would add up to at most

(1-\gamma)\sum_{t^{\prime}\geq t+k_{t}}\gamma^{t^{\prime}-t}=(1-\gamma)\sum_{t^{\prime}\geq k_{t}}\gamma^{t^{\prime}}=\gamma^{k_{t}}\,.

Therefore, by defining
\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}=\epsilon_{t}+\gamma^{k_{t}}
, we get that

\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad Q^{k_{t}}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{k_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})-\epsilon_{t}\geq\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})-\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}=V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}\,.

In particular, we also have

\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad Q^{*}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq Q^{k_{t}}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}\,.

Now let us define
\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})-V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})
. We have:

\displaystyle\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})-V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\mathbb{E}_{a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})-Q_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}+Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-Q_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{a_{t}\sim\pi(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{e_{t}\sim\rho(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}\left[V_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})-V_{\rho^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{a_{t}e_{t}\sim\rho^{\pi}(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})\right]\,.

We can then show by induction on
\ell\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t+t^{\prime}}\gamma^{t^{\prime}}+\gamma^{\ell}\mathbb{E}_{\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1}\sim\rho^{\pi}(\cdot|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\left[\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+\ell-1})\right]\,,

and by taking
\ell\to\infty
, we get

\displaystyle\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t+t^{\prime}}\gamma^{t^{\prime}}\leq\left(\max_{t^{\prime}\geq 0}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t^{\prime}+t}\right)\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\max_{t^{\prime}\geq 0}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t^{\prime}+t}\,.

Since
\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}=0
, we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}\max_{t^{\prime}\geq 0}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t^{\prime}+t}=0
, and hence

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\Delta({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=0\,.

∎

Now we are ready to prove
Proposition

4.33
:
(i) As
\rho^{i}
satisfies the grain-of-uncertainty assumption, its conditionals are always well-defined and hence its conditional completion is unique. The policy
\pi^{i}
is a
(k_{t},\epsilon_{t})
-approximate embedded best response w.r.t. this unique conditional completion. (ii) From
Theorem

3.11
, it follows from the grain-of-truth property that the tail mixtures
\rho_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}
converges to the tail ground-truth distribution
\big(\mu_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}\big)^{\pi_{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}^{i}}
as
t\to\infty

\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}}
-almost-surely.
It follows that for each
\epsilon>0
, there exists a finite time
t_{1}(\epsilon)
such that for all
t\geq t_{1}(\epsilon)
, with probability greater than
1-\epsilon
, we have that
D_{\infty}(\rho^{i},\big(\mu^{i}\big)^{\pi^{i}}\mid\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:t})\leq\epsilon
. (iii) The policies and environment are conditioned on the history, which can differ for each agent due to partial observability; hence these histories serve as a correlation device
(\overline{\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}}^{t},\overline{\mu}^{\overline{\pi}})
for the subsequent tail policies. (iv) From Proposition
Proposition

C.14
we can see that the value of the policies
\pi^{i}
converge to the optimal value with respect to the mixture
\rho^{i}
, which means that for every
\delta>0
, there exists a finite time
t_{2}(\delta)
such that for all
t\geq t_{2}(\delta)
,
\pi^{i}
is a
\delta
-best response with respect to
\rho^{i}
. Taking
T=\max(t_{1},t_{2})
concludes the proof.

C.11
Proof of
Lemma

5.15

We start by showing the lemma for the case
l(h^{\prime})=1
, i.e.,
h^{\prime}=x\in\mathcal{B}
.

For a target precision
\epsilon>0
, we can do the following procedure in order to estimate
\rho_{\tau}^{w}(x|h)
:

•

Start with
\mathrm{high}\leftarrow 1
and
\mathrm{low}\leftarrow 0
, and then repeat the following as long as
\mathrm{high}-\mathrm{low}>\epsilon
:

–

Compute
\mathrm{mid}\leftarrow\frac{\mathrm{low}+\mathrm{high}}{2}
.

–

Send the request
\langle x,\mathrm{mid},h\rangle
to the oracle.

–

If we get 0, then we must have
\rho_{\tau}^{w}(x|h)\leq\mathrm{mid}
, so we set
\mathrm{high}\leftarrow\mathrm{mid}
.

–

If we get 1, then we must have
\rho_{\tau}^{w}(x|h)\geq\mathrm{mid}
, so we set
\mathrm{low}\leftarrow\mathrm{mid}
.

•

Since the inequality
\mathrm{low}\leq\rho_{\tau}^{w}(x|h)\leq\mathrm{high}
is an invariant of the loop, and since
|\mathrm{high}-\mathrm{low}|
decays exponentially with the number of iterations, we will exit the loop after
\lceil-\log_{2}\epsilon\rceil
iterations and the value of
\mathrm{low}
(resp.
\mathrm{high}
) would be an
\epsilon
-accurate estimate of
\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x|h)
from below (resp. from above).

Now we turn to the case where
t^{\prime}:=l(h^{\prime})>1
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\epsilon<1
as otherwise we can replace
\epsilon
by
\min\{\epsilon,1/2\}
.

Let us write
h^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{t^{\prime}}^{\prime})
so that

\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\,.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}:=\epsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon,t^{\prime})>0
be a number that computably depends only on
\epsilon
and
t^{\prime}
which we will choose later. From the above procedure, we can get for every
1\leq i\leq t^{\prime}
, an approximation
\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})
of
\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})
such that

\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})+\epsilon^{\prime}\,.

We can get this using at most
\lceil-\log_{2}\epsilon^{\prime}\rceil
oracle requests for every
1\leq i\leq t^{\prime}
, and hence we make at most
t^{\prime}\lceil-\log_{2}\epsilon^{\prime}\rceil
oracle requests in total, which computably depends only on
\epsilon
and
t^{\prime}
.

We claim that if
\epsilon^{\prime}
is sufficiently small, then

\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h):=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})

yields the desired
\epsilon
-approximation of
\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)
. Indeed:

•

If
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
and if there exists some
1\leq i_{0}\leq t^{\prime}
such that
\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i_{0}}|hh^{\prime}_{<i_{0}})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, then

0\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i_{0}}|hh^{\prime}_{<i_{0}})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\epsilon\,,

and

0\leq\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i_{0}}|hh^{\prime}_{<i_{0}})\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i_{0}}|hh^{\prime}_{<i_{0}})+\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon\,,

which imply that

|\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)-\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)|\leq\epsilon\,.

•

If
\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i_{0}}|hh^{\prime}_{<i_{0}})>\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for all
1\leq i\leq t^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\left(1+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})}\right)\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\left(1+\frac{2\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\exp\left(\frac{2\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)\,,\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,t^{\prime}\}\,,

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
1+z\leq\exp(z)
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x^{\prime}_{i}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})=\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\tilde{\rho}^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\rho^{w}_{\tau}(x_{i}^{\prime}|hh^{\prime}_{<i})\exp\left(\frac{2\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)\exp\left(\frac{2t^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)=\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)\left(1+\exp\left(\frac{2t^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{w}_{\tau}(h^{\prime}|h)+\left(\exp\left(\frac{2t^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)-1\right)\,,

and hence it suffices to choose
\epsilon^{\prime}
so that
\exp\left(\frac{2t^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)-1=\epsilon
.

By choosing
\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\epsilon\ln(1+\epsilon)}{2t^{\prime}}
, we get both
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
and
\exp\left(\frac{2t^{\prime}\epsilon^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\right)-1=\epsilon
, which completes the proof of
Lemma

5.15
.

C.12
Proof of
Theorem

5.16

Let us first analyze, abstractly, the
w
-reflective universal inductor oracle (
w
-RUI-oracle) whose existence we want to show. More precisely, let us analyze the relationship between the
w
-RUI-oracle and its corresponding
w
-reflective universal inductor (
w
-RUI): The inductor reacts, i.e., is a consequence of, the oracle that we use. Hence, we need to show the existence of an oracle such that its answers to queries reflect what this inductor – that depends on the oracle – would compute. Thus, in some sense we need some kind of fixpoint in a space containing oracles and inductors. This is what we will do. Let us start by defining appropriate spaces.
Let

\Omega_{\textrm{q}}=[0,1]^{\mathcal{B}\times([0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q})\times\mathcal{B}^{*}}\,,

and

\Omega_{\textrm{e}}=[0,1]^{\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}}\,

and note the following:

•

Each element
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{\textrm{q}}
corresponds to a function
\mathrm{query}:\mathcal{B}\times([0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q})\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
which in turn gives rise to a probabilistic oracle
\tau_{\mathrm{query}}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
as follows: Given
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
encoding a triplet
(b,p,h)\in\mathcal{B}\times(\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1])\times\mathcal{B}^{*}
, i.e.,
x=\langle b,p,h\rangle
, we let
\tau_{\mathrm{query}}(x)=\mathrm{query}(x)
.
96
96

96

Note that in
Definition

5.11
, we impose that the encoding
\langle b,p,h\rangle
is complete, i.e., that all bitstrings
x\in\mathcal{B}^{\ast}
are a valid encoding of a triplet
(b,p,h)

•

Similarly, each element
\mathrm{eval}\in\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
corresponds to a function
\mathrm{eval}:\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
which can be interpreted as a characterization of an inductor/predictor. More precisely, for every history
h\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
and every
b\in\mathcal{B}
,
\mathrm{eval}(b,h)
can be seen as the probability of predicting that the next token is
b
given that the history of observations so far is
h
.

We will work with the space
X=\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
and define a mapping
\phi
that maps each element of
X
to a
subset
of
X
. The mapping
\phi
will be defined in a way that reflects the properties of oracles and inductors. Once we have done this we can apply a classical fixed point theorem to deduce the existence of an
x^{*}\in X
that satisfies
x^{*}\in\phi(x^{*})
. This will correspond to a
w
-RUI-oracle.

So, let us now define

\phi:\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}\to\mathrm{Pow}(\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}})\,,

from
\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
to its own power set (i.e., the set of subsets of
\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
), such that

(\mathrm{query}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}^{\prime})\in\phi(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})

if and only if

(i)

For every
(b,p,h)\in\mathcal{B}\times([0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q})\times\mathcal{B}^{*}
, we have

\mathrm{eval}(b,h)>p\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathrm{query}^{\prime}(b,p,h)=1\,,

and

\mathrm{eval}(b,h)<p\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathrm{query}^{\prime}(b,p,h)=0\,.

(ii)

For every
(b,h)\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}
, we have

\mathrm{eval}^{\prime}(b,h)=\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(b|h)=\frac{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}w_{M}\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(hb)}{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}w_{M}\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)}\,,

where
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}
is the space of all restricted abstract probabilistic oracle machines and
w\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}
is the lower semicomputable universal prior for which we would like to show that a
w
-RUI-oracle exists.

Let us first check that a fixed point of
\phi
, i.e., a pair
(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})\in\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
satisfying
(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})\in\phi(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})
, indeed gives rise to a RUI-oracle
\tau_{\mathrm{query}}
and its corresponding reflective universal inductor
\mathrm{eval}=\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}
. This actually follows directly from the definition of the
w
-RUI-oracle in Definition
5.11
and the above definition of
\phi
.

In order to prove the existence of the desired fixed point we will employ an infinite dimensional version of the classical Kakutani’s fixed point theorem:

Theorem C.15

(
29
)
.

Let
X=[0,1]^{T}
be the set of functions from
T
to
[0,1]
, where
T
is a countable set, and let
\phi:X\rightarrow\mathrm{Pow}(X)
be a set-valued function for which the following holds:

(1)

For all
x\in X
the set
\phi(x)
is nonempty and convex.

(2)

The graph of
\phi
(i.e., the set
\{(x,y)\in X^{2}:y\in\phi(x)\}
) is closed, i.e., for every sequence
(x_{n},y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for which
y_{n}\in\phi(x_{n})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=x
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}y_{n}=y
(where the convergence is taken to be the pointwise convergence
97
97

97

Recall that for a sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
X=[0,1]^{T}
,
(x_{n})_{n}
converges pointwise to
x\in X
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}(t)=x(t)
for every
t\in T
.
in
[0,1]^{T}
) then
y\in\phi(x)
.

Then there exists a fixed point
x^{*}
of
\phi
.

We already argued above that in our case
X=\Omega_{\textrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\textrm{e}}
, which can be rewritten as
[0,1]^{T}
with

T=\big(\mathcal{B}\times(\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1])\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\big)\cup\big(\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\big)\,,

which satisfies the required countability requirement in
Theorem

C.15
. We also easily check that our definition of
\phi
implies that
\phi(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})
is always non-empty and convex. So Condition (1) is trivially satisfied.

It remains to show that the graph of
\phi
is closed. In our notations we can rewrite the closedness condition (as defined in
Theorem

C.15
) as follows. Suppose we have

(\mathrm{query}_{n},\mathrm{eval}_{n},\mathrm{query}_{n}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}_{n}^{\prime})\in\Omega_{\mathrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{e}}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{q}}\times\Omega_{\mathrm{e}}

such that

(\mathrm{query}_{n}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}_{n}^{\prime})\in\phi(\mathrm{query}_{n},\mathrm{eval}_{n})\,,\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\,,

and assume that

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathrm{query}_{n},\mathrm{eval}_{n},\mathrm{query}_{n}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}_{n}^{\prime})=(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval},\mathrm{query}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}^{\prime})\,.

We need to show that

(\mathrm{query}^{\prime},\mathrm{eval}^{\prime})\in\phi(\mathrm{query},\mathrm{eval})\,,

i.e., we need to show that these functions satisfy Conditions (i) and (ii) from above. Showing Condition (i) is straightforward: Let
(b,p,h)\in\mathcal{B}\times([0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q})\times\mathcal{B}^{*}
and assume first that
\mathrm{eval}(b,h)>p
. As
\mathrm{eval}_{n}
converges to
\mathrm{eval}
pointwise, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{eval}_{n}(b,h)=\mathrm{eval}(b,h)>p
, and hence there must exist
n_{0}>0
such that for all
n>n_{0}
, we have
\mathrm{eval}_{n}(b,h)>p
. As
(\mathrm{query}^{\prime}_{n},\mathrm{eval}^{\prime}_{n})\in\phi(\mathrm{query}_{n},\mathrm{eval}_{n})
, it follows that
\mathrm{query}^{\prime}_{n}(b,p,h)=1
, for all
n>n_{0}
, implying that
\mathrm{query}^{\prime}(b,p,h)=1
as well, as desired. The case
\mathrm{eval}(b,h)<p
follows similarly. If
\mathrm{eval}(b,h)=p
, there is no constraint on
\mathrm{query}^{\prime}(b,p,h)
that we need to check, so (i) is trivially satisfied in this case as well.

In order to show that Condition (ii) is satisfied, it suffices to show that for every
h\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
and every
b\in\mathcal{B}
, we have

\mathrm{query}\mapsto\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(b|h)=\frac{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}w_{M}\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(hb)}{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}w_{M}\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)}

is continuous (as a function of
\mathrm{query}
), where continuity is defined using the pointwise convergence
98
98

98

Equivalently, we endow
\Omega_{\textrm{q}}=[0,1]^{\mathcal{B}\times([0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q})\times\mathcal{B}^{*}}
with the product topology and define the continuity of
\mathrm{query}\mapsto\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(b|h)
based on this topology.
as follows: We require that if
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{query}_{n}=\mathrm{query}
pointwise, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}_{n}}}(b|h)=\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(b|h)
.

Define, for all
h\in{\cal B}^{*}
,

F_{h}(\mathrm{query}):=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}w_{M}\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h).

Then
\rho^{w}_{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(b|h)=F_{hb}(\mathrm{query})/F_{h}(\mathrm{query})
. As
both
h
and
hb
are members of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
, it thus suffices to prove that
\mathrm{query}\mapsto F_{h}(\mathrm{query})
is continuous for every
h\in{\cal B}^{*}
, as the quotient of two non-zero continuous functions is also continuous. (Here we used that
F_{h}(\mathrm{query})
is always non-zero, which can be seen by considering a restricted abstract probabilistic oracle machine
M_{h}
which hard codes
h
in its definition and writes it with probability one.)

To show that
\mathrm{query}\mapsto F_{h}(\mathrm{query})
is continuous, we first observe that it actually suffices to show that
\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
is continuous (as a function of
\mathrm{query}
) for arbitrary
h
and
M
. To see this, recall the following fact from analysis (that we will use repeatedly throughout the remainder of the proof): If
(a_{n})
is a sequence of non-negative real numbers such that
\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}
is bounded (by one or any other constant), then for every
{\epsilon}>0
there exists
n_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{n>n_{\epsilon}}a_{n}\leq{\epsilon}
. This fact can be used to show that if
(f_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of continuous functions from an arbitrary topological space
X
to
[0,1]
then
\sum_{n}a_{n}f_{n}
is also continuous.
99
99

99

We essentially use the following argument: For every
x_{0}\in X
and every
{\epsilon}>0
we use the continuity of the functions
(f_{n})_{n\leq n_{\epsilon}}
to find a neighborhood of
x_{0}
for which
\sum_{n\leq n_{\epsilon}}a_{n}f_{n}(x)
is at most
{\epsilon}
away from
\sum_{n\leq n_{\epsilon}}a_{n}f_{n}(x_{0})
for every
x
in that neighborhood. This means that
\sum_{n}a_{n}f_{n}(x)
is at most
3{\epsilon}
away from
\sum_{n}a_{n}f_{n}(x_{0})
.
Now since
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}
is countable and
\sum_{M}w_{M}\leq 1
we can now see that if
\mathrm{query}\mapsto\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
is continuous for every
h\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, then
\mathrm{query}\mapsto F_{h}(\mathrm{query})
is also continuous.

Thus all that remains to be seen is that for arbitrary but fixed
h\in{\cal B}^{*}
and
M\in{\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}}
we have that
\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
is continuous (as a function in
\mathrm{query}
).
Towards this end, recall that we may view the execution of
M
as a, potentially infinite, rooted tree as follows. The root of the tree corresponds to the initial state and each edge corresponds to the transition from one internal state to the next. If
M
never queries the oracle nor uses the randomness tape, we get a path. If it does, we get a tree. In this tree each node in which we query the oracle (resp. the randomness tape) has two successors, one for getting
0
as an answer and one for getting
1
. We assign the probability of getting this answer as a label to the corresponding edge. For completeness, we label all deterministic edges with
1
. As we are only interested in states with
h
on the output tape, we may remove all nodes from the tree that do not contain
h
or a prefix of
h
on the output tape (recall that the output tape is written from left to right, thus once it contains a non-prefix of
h
, it will never contain a string starting with
h
). Similarly, we remove all nodes that are (strict) descendants of some node with
exactly

h
on the output tape. Finally, we remove all nodes for which no node in its subtree contains
h
on its output tape.

Observe that, by construction, the remaining tree satisfies the following:

•

All leaves contain (exactly)
h
on the output tape.

•

All nodes that have
h
on the output tape are leaves.

•

The probability that
M
reaches a given leaf is given by the product of the labels on the path from the root to this leaf.

Observe also that the oracle
\tau
only influences the labels of the edges of this tree, but not the structure of the tree. We call the resulting tree
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
and we denote its leaves as
\mathcal{L}_{M,h}
. We then have

\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{L}_{M,h}}w_{M,\mathrm{query}}(v)\,,

where
w_{M,\mathrm{query}}(v)
denotes the product of the labels along the path from the root to
v
in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
if we use the oracle
\tau_{\mathrm{\mathrm{query}}}
.

Recall that we need to show that
\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}
is continuous (as a function of
\mathrm{query}
). Fix some sequence
\left(\mathrm{query}_{n}\right)_{n}
that converges to some arbitrary
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{q}}
.

As a first step, observe that whenever
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
is finite the statement is trivially true, as then
\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}
depends only on finitely many different values,
100
100

100

Note that for every
v\in\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
, the mapping
\mathrm{query}\mapsto w_{M,\mathrm{query}}(v)
is a polynomial function of finitely many values
\{\mathrm{query}(x):\text{ }x\in Q_{v}\}
, where
Q_{v}
is the set of oracle requests
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
that were made on the computational path from the root node to
v
. Therefore, the mapping
\mathrm{query}\mapsto w_{M,\mathrm{query}}(v)
is a continuous function in
\mathrm{query}
, and hence the sum of the weights of finitely many nodes is also continuous in
\mathrm{query}
.
can trivially be bounded uniformly.
To gain some intuition why the generalization to infinite trees is non-trivial, and in particular why we need the slightly awkward looking bound on the number of oracle calls in
Definition

5.6
, consider the following machine (which does not restrict the number of oracle calls): It starts by sending a request
x_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
to the oracle, if the answer is
1
, it writes
h
and stops. Otherwise, it questions the oracle again with another request
x_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\setminus\{x_{1}\}
and writes
h
if the answer is
1
. Otherwise, it questions the oracle again with yet another request
x_{3}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
, etc.
101
101

101

We assume that the mapping
n\mapsto x_{n}
is computable. For example, we can let
x_{n}
be the binary representation of
n
.
Let
p_{i}:=\tau_{\mathrm{query}}(x_{i})
denote the probability that the
i
th call to the oracle results in a
1
. Then, by construction, we have that the probability that
M
writes
h
and stops is given by
\lambda_{M}:=\sum_{i\geq 1}p_{i}\cdot\prod_{1\leq j<i}(1-p_{j})
. Now consider different versions on how to set the
p_{i}
. Fix some
c>0
arbitrarily and define
p_{i}^{(n)}=c/n
if
i\leq n
and
p_{i}^{(n)}=0
otherwise. Clearly,
p_{i}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle n\to\infty$}}0
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, independently of
c
. However,
\lambda_{M}^{(n)}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{c}{n}\cdot(1-c/n)^{i}=1-(1-c/n)^{n}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.41112pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle n\to\infty$}}1-e^{-c}
. That is, different values of
c
give us different halting probabilities, even though we converge locally to the same value on all edges of the tree.

Next, let us try to understand what the bound in
Definition

5.6
actually means. For that let us first consider the special case
h=b
for some
b\in{\cal B}
, i.e., we have only one stage. As our
M
is randomized, the machine can still use an arbitrarily large number of calls to the oracle, but they will have to come in a somewhat structured way. To gain some intuition let us consider the following example. Assume the machine queries the randomness tape until it sees
1
, and then decides on
4^{i}
as a bound if it got a
1
in the
i
th trial. The important point (and difference to our counterexample from above) is that the machine has to decide on the bound
before
its first call to the oracle.
102
102

102

As a side remark note that in our current example we do not even have a bounded
expected
number of calls to the oracle, i.e., we do
not
need a bounded expected number of oracle calls per stage.
So let us see now how we can use this fact. As a first step we color all nodes in
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}

orange
iff they correspond to the first oracle call (on the path from the root to some leaf). Let
{\cal O}
be the set of orange nodes in
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
and, similarly to the leafs, denote for each
v\in{\cal O}
by
w_{v}
the product of the labels on the path from the root to
v
. Note that neither the set
{\cal O}
nor the weights
w_{v}
of orange nodes depends on the query function.
Note also that regardless of which query function
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{q}
we use in the remainder of the tree, we always have for all
v\in{\cal O}
that the sum of the weights of all leaves in the subtree of
v
can never be larger than
w_{v}
. We can use this fact to approximate
\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
as follows. Suppose we only sum up the weights of all orange nodes that have depth at most
d
(here
depth
of a vertex
v
denotes the number of edges of the (unique) path from
v
to the root of the tree). Clearly, there are only a finite number of such nodes. Also, if we make
d
larger, we get a better and better approximation of the sum of the weights of all orange nodes. Thus, for every
{\epsilon}>0
there exists a
d_{\epsilon}
such that the sum of the weights of all orange nodes of depth larger than
d_{\epsilon}
is at most
{\epsilon}
. As a consequence we get: The sum of the weights of all leaves that are in a subtree of orange nodes of depth larger than
d
is at most
{\epsilon}
. With this we have taken care of all but a finite number of orange nodes. Denote this finite set by
{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}
.

While, by construction, the number of nodes in
{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}
is finite, their subtrees can still contain an infinite number of leaves. To also reduce this number to a finite number, we repeat the trick from above. To do so, we need to handle one additional issue: While the weight of an orange node, by the definition of "orange", is independent of
\tau_{\mathrm{query}}
, this is not the case for the leaves. Consider the oracle
unif
that just returns
0/1
uniformly at random. Observe that for any other function
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{q}
, the weight of any leaf
v^{\prime}
in the subtree of some orange vertex
v
satisfies

w_{\mathrm{query}}(v^{\prime})\leq 2^{N_{v}}\cdot w_{{\text{unif}}}(v^{\prime})
, where
N_{v}
is the bound on the number of oracle calls in the subtree of
v
(recall that this bound exists according to
Definition

5.6
, cf. also our discussion above). With this observation at hand we can now proceed as follows.

Fix some orange node
v\in{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}
and consider the tree with respect to
unif
. Then the sum of the weights of all leaves in the subtree of
v
that have (total) depth at most
d_{\epsilon}+d
will form, for larger and larger
d
, an ever more precise approximation of the sum over all leaves in the subtree of
v
. Thus, there exists a
d_{v}
such that the sum over all leaves of depth larger than
d_{v}
is at most
{\epsilon}/(2^{N_{v}}|{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}|)
. Note that this implies that if we change the query function from
unif
to an arbitrary
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{q}
, we still know that the sum over all leaves of depth larger than
d_{v}
is at most
{\epsilon}/|{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}|
. Now set

d_{T}:=\max_{v\in{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}}d_{v}
and observe that the factor
|{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}|
allows us to conclude that regardless of which oracle
\mathrm{query}\in\Omega_{q}
we consider, the sum of the weights of all leaves of depth larger than
d_{T}
is at most
2{\epsilon}
(one
{\epsilon}
for the leaves in subtrees of orange nodes outside of
{\cal O}_{d_{\epsilon}}
and one for those inside).

As a final step observe that the sum of all leaves of depth at most
d_{T}
contains only finitely many different queries to the oracle (as we have a finite tree). Thus, if
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{query}_{n}=\mathrm{query}
pointwise, then for every
{\epsilon}>0
there exists an
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
the sums of the weights of the leaves up to depth
d_{T}
with respect to
\mathrm{query}_{n}
and
\mathrm{query}
differ by at most
{\epsilon}
. In summary, we have thus shown: For every
{\epsilon}>0
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have

|\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}_{n}}}(h)-\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)|\leq 3{\epsilon}\,.

This concludes the proof that
\mathrm{query}\mapsto\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
is continuous for
h=b\in\mathcal{B}
.

To handle the general case
h=b_{1}\ldots b_{\ell}
we will apply the above argument recursively
\ell
times. Each stage will contribute a term of
2{\epsilon}
for ignoring leaves of stage
i
that are too deep. There are two important points that allow us to argue like that. Firstly, the fact that the output tape may only be written from left to right, ensures that in order to reach a state with
h
on the tape, we have to pass first through a node with
b_{1}
on it, then with
b_{1}b_{2}
on it, etc. Secondly, we recall that the sum of all weights of leaves in the subtree of some vertex can never be larger than the weight of the root of this subtree. Note that this implies that if, for example, we consider all leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
that are in subtrees of leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}}
that are contained in one of the two "
{\epsilon}
-parts", the total sum of such leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,h}
cannot be larger than
2{\epsilon}
. In other words, we may well ignore all such leaves, as they have already been taken care of. Rephrased again, this means that for the second stage we only have to consider leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}b_{2}}
which are descendants of leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}}
which are at depth at most
d_{T_{1}}=d_{T}(\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}})
. And here comes the trick. For any of these leaves, say
v\in\mathcal{L}_{M,b_{1}}
(of depth at most
d_{T_{1}}
), we can design a new machine
M_{v}
that, effectively, just starts in the state of
M
corresponding to
v
. Thus, we can repeat all of our arguments from above (for the case
h=b
) and conclude that there exists a depth
d_{v}
such that the sum of the weights of all leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}b_{2}}
that are contained in the subtree of
v
and have depth larger than
d_{v}
is bounded by
{\epsilon}/K_{1}
, where
K_{1}
is the number of leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}}
of depth at most
d_{T_{1}}
. Clearly, this implies that for

d_{T_{2}}:=\max d_{v}
we have that the sum of the weights of all leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}b_{2}}
of depth larger than
d_{T_{2}}
is bounded by
4{\epsilon}
. Proceeding in the same way for all stages we thus get: There exists a
d_{T_{\ell}}
such that the sum of weights of all leaves in
\mathcal{T}_{M,b_{1}\ldots b_{\ell}}
of depth larger than
d_{T_{\ell}}
is bounded by
2\ell{\epsilon}
. Similarly as for the case
h=b
above this thus allows us to conclude: If
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{query}_{n}=\mathrm{query}
pointwise, then for every
{\epsilon}>0
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have

|\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}_{n}}}(h)-\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)|\leq(2\ell+1){\epsilon},

which shows that
\mathrm{query}\mapsto\lambda_{M}^{\tau_{\mathrm{query}}}(h)
is continuous, as desired.

C.13
Proof that equation
21
implies equation
18

First, we show by induction on
t\geq 0
that

w(M|h_{1:t})=w(M)\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{1:t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{1:t})}\,.

(39)

•

For
t=0
, we have
h_{1:t}=\varepsilon
by convention. Therefore, it follows from the third equality of equation
21
that
w(M|h_{1:t})=w(M|\varepsilon)=w(M)
. Now since
\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(\varepsilon)=\bar{\lambda}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}^{\tau}(\varepsilon)=1
, we can see that equation
39
holds for
t=0
.

•

For
t>0
, if equation
39
holds for
t-1
, then

w(M|h_{<t})=w(M)\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})}\,,

and hence from the second equality of equation
21
we get

w(M|h_{1:t})=w(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}=w(M)\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}=w(M)\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{1:t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{1:t})}\,.

We conclude that equation
39
holds for all
t\geq 0
. Now we have

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{1:t})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{k}\mid h_{<k})

\displaystyle=\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{t}\mid h_{<t})

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})\lambda^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{t}\mid h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\geq}}\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w(M)\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{<t})}\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}\mid h_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w(M)\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{1:t})\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the first inequality of equation
21
and
(\dagger)
follows from equation
39
.

C.14
Proof of
Lemma

5.27

Proof.

As the conditional semimeasure
\sigma:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{B}
is
\tau
-lower-semicomputable, there exists two
\tau
-POMs
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
, which upon input
h\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
output an infinite sequence of prefix-free encoded rational numbers
\langle q^{0}_{k}\rangle
and
\langle q^{1}_{k}\rangle
, respectively, for which
\lim_{k\to\infty}q^{b}_{k}=\sigma(h)(b)
with
b\in\{0,1\}
. Let us use the notation
\phi_{0}(h)
and
\phi_{1}(h)
to indicate the iterator functions streaming the infinite sequences
(\langle q_{k}^{b}\rangle)_{k}
for
b=0
and
b=1
respectively. We closely follow the proof of Theorem 6 of
114
(which is similar to the proof technique of
67
) with one minor modification to handle the stochasticity resulting from the probabilistic oracle
\tau
.

The main challenge that a probabilistic oracle introduces for the traditional lower-semicomputable functions
\phi(x,k)
(cf.
Definition

2.8
) is that
\tau
-POMs in general have a stochastic output, making it difficult to enforce that the output upon input
(x,k+1)
is greater or equal to the output upon input
(x,k)
, as those outputs are associated with independent runs of the
\tau
-POM on different inputs and hence can stochastically fluctuate independently of each other. For the proof technique of
114
to work, it is crucial that the intervals
\phi(x,k+1)-\phi(x,k)
are all non-negative.

We solve this challenge by our specialized definition of
\tau
-LSC that instead of making independent calls to a function
f(x,k)
with increasing values of
k
, we have a single
\tau
-POM that streams an infinite sequence of
(q_{k})
. Even though this infinite sequence can be stochastic due to the uncontrollable stochasticity of the probabilistic oracle, the
\tau
-POM can easily enforce that
q_{k+1}\geq q_{k}
, as the
\tau
-POM has access to
q_{k}
when computing
q_{k+1}
, in contrast to the case where
q_{k}
and
q_{k+1}
would be computed by independent calls to some stochastic function
f(x,k)
.

Now we can apply the main proof technique of
114
, which informally goes as follows. First we partition the interval
[0,1]
into a list of subintervals of width
\Delta_{k}^{b}:=q^{b}_{k}-q^{b}_{k-1}
labeled by the corresponding bit
b
. Then we sample a random real from
[0,1]
, check which subinterval it is part of and output the label associated with that subinterval. As we have that
\lim_{k\to\infty}q^{b}_{k}=\sigma(h)(b)
, and taking
q^{b}_{0}:=0
, we have that the sum of subintervals
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{k}^{b}=\sigma(h)(b)
, and hence random sampling a real from
[0,1]
and checking which interval it belongs to and outputting the associated label
b
results in sampling from
\sigma(h)
. Note that since
\sigma(h)\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{B}
is a semiprobability distribution, it might be the case that the sampled real does not belong to any of the subintervals. In this case, the
\tau
-POM
M
does not halt. We can see that the output distribution
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(b\mid h)
of
M
indeed matches the semiprobability
\sigma(h)(b)
.

Algorithm
3
formalizes this construction. The algorithm descriptions follow those of
114
, with the minimal change of using
\phi_{0}(h)
and
\phi_{1}(h)
–the iterator functions streaming the infinite sequences
(\langle q_{k}^{b}\rangle)_{k}
for
b=0
and
b=1
respectively.

Input:

A list of intervals P, a bit
b\in\mathcal{B}
, and a rational number
\Delta\in\mathbb{Q}

Effect:

A subinterval of label
b
and length
\Delta
is added to P

if

P is empty

then

left
\leftarrow
0

else

left
\leftarrow
P[-1].right
end if

P.append(label:
b
, right: left +
\Delta
)

Algorithm 1

append

Input:

A list of intervals P, and a bitstring
\omega_{1:k}
of length
k
interpreted as a number in
\{i2^{-k}:0\leq i\leq 2^{k}-1\}

Effect:

If
[\omega_{1:k},\omega_{1:k}+2^{-k}]
is a subset of a subinterval within P, return its label
b

left
\leftarrow
0

for

(
b
, right)
\in
P

do

if

left
\leq\omega_{1:k}
and
\omega_{1:k}+2^{-k}\leq
right

then

return
b

end if

left
\leftarrow
right

end for

Algorithm 2

check

Input:

(
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
, h)

Require:

random bitstream
\omega

Output:
b\sim\sigma(h)

Let P be an empty list

\psi_{0}\leftarrow 0

\psi_{1}\leftarrow 0

for

(q^{0}_{k},q^{1}_{k})
in
\textrm{zip}(\phi_{0}(h),\phi_{1}(h))

do

for

b\in\{0,1\}

do

\Delta_{b}\leftarrow q_{k}^{b}-\psi_{b}

\psi_{b}\leftarrow q_{k}^{b}

append(P,
b,\Delta_{b}
)

end for

check(P,
\omega_{1:k}
)

end for

Algorithm 3

sample

As noted by
114
,
\omega_{1:k}
in this construction is only specified to precision
2^{-k}
. Hence, we need check(P,
\omega_{1:k}
) to only output True when the entire interval
[\omega_{1:k},\omega_{1:k}+2^{-k}]
is a subset of some subinterval within P.
∎

C.15
Proof of
Theorem

5.28

Proof.

We will recursively define weight functions

and APOMs

so that they satisfy equation

1.

\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}

\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(b|h)>0
for all

and all

.

2.

The function

\tau
-LSC and hence there exists a machine

that “lower-semi-computes”

in the sense of

5.24
.

for every

and every

satisfying

.

equation

w_{n}
and
M_{n}
for histories up to length
n
:

\displaystyle\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{n}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\,,\quad

\displaystyle\forall t\in[n]

(40)

\displaystyle w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})

\displaystyle=w_{n}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\forall t\in[n]

The fifth and sixth desiderata trivially hold for

\displaystyle\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}^{\prime}}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}_{M_{n-1}}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\quad&\text{if }t<n\,,\\
\\
\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\,,\quad&\text{if }t\geq n\,,\\

(41)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle=\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})=\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}^{\prime}}(b|h_{<t})\,,

\displaystyle w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}w_{n-1}(M|h_{1:t})\quad&\text{if }t<n\\
w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}\quad&\text{if }t\geq n\,,\end{cases}

where we use the convention that

. In order to see why we can indeed construct the machines

is

-LSC.

Combining the above two observations, we can see that the function

\langle b,h_{<t}\rangle\mapsto\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}_{M_{n-1}}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\quad&\text{if }t<n\,,\\
\\
\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\,,\quad&\text{if }t\geq n\,,\\
\end{cases}

-sampleable as well. Therefore, the machine

can be constructed. Furthermore, having access to
\langle M_{n-1}\rangle
and
\langle M_{w_{n-1}}\rangle
, we can computably
103

103

That is, there exists a Turing machine that can compute

and

.
construct the description
\langle M_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
of

.

Now since
\bar{\lambda}_{M_{n}^{\prime}}^{\tau}
is
\tau

that satisfies

. In the following, we show that actually all the desiderata are satisfied for

1.

By construction, we made

, and hence

is a measure. To prove that it is a positive measure, note that for every

, we have

Hence, it suffices to show that

and all

, we must have

We conclude that the first desideratum is satisfied.

2.

From

Let

.

We conclude that the third desideratum is satisfied.

4.

In the following, we show that equation

is satisfied:

where
(\ast)
follows from the induction hypothesis.

, we have from equation

that

\displaystyle\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})=\bar{\lambda}_{M_{n}^{\prime}}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\geq\lambda_{M_{n}^{\prime}}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{n}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\,,

.

Therefore, the first condition of equation

is satisfied.

–

Let

and let

so that

. We have:

*

If
t<n
, then it follows from equation

that

\displaystyle w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n-1}(M|h_{1:t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n-1}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}=w_{n}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}\,,

follows from the induction hypothesis.

If
