, then from equation
41
, we have that

\displaystyle w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n-1}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}=w_{n}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}\,,

where the last equality is true because
l(h_{<t})=t-1<n
.

Therefore, the second condition of equation
40
is satisfied.

–

We have
w_{n}(M|\varepsilon)=w_{n-1}(M|\varepsilon)
because
l(\varepsilon)=0<n
, and we have
w_{n-1}(M|\varepsilon)=w(M)
from the induction hypothesis. Hence, the third condition of equation
40
is satisfied.

We conclude that the fourth desideratum is satisfied.

5.

Let
0\leq t\leq n^{\prime}\leq n
.

–

If
n^{\prime}=n
, then we obviously have

w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n^{\prime}}(M|h_{1:t})\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n^{\prime}}}(b|h_{<t})\,.

–

If
n^{\prime}<n
, then
n^{\prime}\leq n-1
. From the induction hypothesis, we know that

w_{n-1}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n^{\prime}}(M|h_{1:t})\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n-1}}(b|h_{<t})=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n^{\prime}}}(b|h_{<t})\,.

On the other hand, since
t\leq n^{\prime}<n
, we know from equation
41
that

w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n-1}(M|h_{1:t})\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})=\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{n-1}}(b|h_{<t})=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n-1}}(b|h_{<t})\,.

Therefore,

w_{n}(M|h_{1:t})=w_{n^{\prime}}(M|h_{1:t})\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n}}(b|h_{<t})=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n^{\prime}}}(b|h_{<t})\,.

We conclude that the fifth desideratum is satisfied.

6.

We have already shown that from
\langle M_{n-1}\rangle
and
\langle M_{w_{n-1}}\rangle
, we can computably construct
\langle M_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
, from which we can computably construct
\langle M_{n}\rangle
. We also showed that from
\langle M_{w_{n-1}}\rangle
and
\langle M_{n}\rangle
, we can computably construct
\langle M_{w_{n}}\rangle
. Therefore, the sixth desideratum is satisfied.

We conclude that
(w_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(M_{n})_{n\geq 0}
satisfy the six desiderata.

We now construct the machine
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
based on the recursive definition of the machines
(M_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(M_{w_{n}})_{n\geq 0}
. The machine
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
will take an input history
h
, compute the index
n=l(h)
, construct the machine
M_{n+1}
by recursively applying the process from desideratum 6, and then sample from
M_{n+1}
’s distribution for the history
h
to produce the next bit
b=h_{n+1}
.

Input:

Input history
h\in\mathcal{B}^{*}

Output:

Bit
b

n\leftarrow l(h)

\langle M_{\text{current}}\rangle\leftarrow\langle M_{0}\rangle

//
Initialize with hardcoded description of
M_{0}

\langle M_{w,\text{current}}\rangle\leftarrow\langle M_{w_{0}}\rangle

//
Initialize with hardcoded description of
M_{w_{0}}

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

n+1

do

(\langle M_{\text{current}}\rangle,\langle M_{w,\text{current}}\rangle)\leftarrow\text{Construct}(\langle M_{\text{current}}\rangle,\langle M_{w,\text{current}}\rangle)

//
Run the computational procedure from desideratum 6

end for

//
At this point,
\langle M_{\text{current}}\rangle=\langle M_{n+1}\rangle

b\leftarrow M_{\text{current}}^{\tau}(h)\;

//
Simulate
M_{n+1}^{\tau}
on input
h
and sample one bit
b\sim\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{n+1}}(\cdot|h)

return

b

Algorithm 4

The APOM
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
satisfying equation
21

We define the APOM
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
as the machine described in Algorithm
4
. By its construction,
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
is a valid APOM. Its conditional output distribution on input
h
is, by definition, that of the machine
M_{l(h)+1}
:

\lambda^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(b|h):=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{l(h)+1}}(b|h)\,.

We also define the global weight function
w(M|h)
based on the length of the history:

w(M|h):=w_{l(h)}(M|h)\,.

We now show that this machine
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
and this weight function
w
together satisfy the three recursive equations in equation
21
:

1.

We verify the first condition of equation
21
. Let
h_{<t}
be a history. We have

\displaystyle\lambda^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{l(h_{<t})+1}}(b|h_{<t})=\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{t}}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{t}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w_{t-1}(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}w(M|h_{<t})\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(b|h_{<t})\,,

where the equalities
(*)
follow from the definitions of
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
and
w(M|h)
,
(\dagger)
from the fact that
M_{t}
satisfies equation
40
, and
(\ddagger)
from the fifth desideratum. Thus, Condition 1 of equation
21
holds.

2.

We verify the second condition of equation
21
. Let
h_{1:t}
be a history. We have

\displaystyle w(M|h_{1:t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}w_{t}(M|h_{1:t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}w_{t-1}(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\bar{\lambda}^{\tau}_{M_{t}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}w(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\lambda^{\tau}_{M_{l(h_{<t})+1}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}w(M|h_{<t})\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{M}^{\tau}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}{\lambda^{\tau}_{M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}}(h_{t}|h_{<t})}\,,

where the equalities
(*)
follow from the definitions of
w(M|h)
and
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
, and
(\dagger)
from the recursive definition in equation
41
. Thus, Condition 2 of equation
21
holds.

3.

Finally, we verify the third condition:

\displaystyle w(M|\varepsilon)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}w_{l(\varepsilon)}(M|\varepsilon)=w_{0}(M|\varepsilon)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}w(M)\,,

where
(*)
follows from the definition of
w
and
(\dagger)
follows from the definition of
w_{0}
. Thus, Condition 3 of equation
21
holds.

Since an APOM
M^{w}_{\mathrm{RO}}
and a weight function
w
exist that satisfy all three conditions of equation
21
, and we have shown in
Section

C.13
that this implies equation
18
, the theorem is proven.

∎

C.16
Proof of
Theorem

5.29

Proof.

Minor variation upon
63
and
114
.

First, let us show that the
k
-step
Q
values of equation
11
are
\tau
-estimable. This can be proved by induction on
k
.

For one-step
Q
values (
k=1
), notice from equation
11
that for
\rho=\rho^{RO}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)}\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},\text{\ae}_{t+1}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+(1-\gamma)\gamma r(e_{t+1})+\gamma^{2}V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1})\right]\,.

Similarly, for every
T\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{T}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\left[(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{T}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})+\gamma^{T+1}V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T})\right]\,.

Now since
0\leq V_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae_{t}a_{t+1}e_{t+1}\ldots a_{t+T}e_{t+T})\leq 1
, we can see that for every
T\geq 1
,

Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
can be lower-bounded and upper-bounded as

\underline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\leq Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\leq\overline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\,,

where

\displaystyle\underline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{T}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\sum_{i=0}^{T}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})\,,

\displaystyle\overline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E},\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{T}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\left[(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{T}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})+\gamma^{T+1}\right]\,.

Now since
\rho
is
\tau
-estimable, we can see that both
\underline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
and
\overline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
are
\tau
-estimable. Furthermore, since

\lim_{T\to\infty}\overline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)-\underline{Q}_{\rho,T}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)=\lim_{T\to\infty}\gamma^{T+1}=0\,,

we can see that
Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
is
\tau
-estimable.

Now for every
k>1
, assume that
Q_{\rho}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
is
\tau
-estimable, we can see from equation
11
that

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho(e\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)}\left[(1-\gamma)r(e)+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae,a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e}\rho(e\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\left[(1-\gamma)r(e)+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae,a^{\prime})\right]\,,

which implies that
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
is
\tau
-estimable as well. We conclude that for every
k\geq 1
,
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
is
\tau
-estimable, and hence also
\tau
-LSC and
\tau
-USC.

Now since optimal-planning is at least as good as
k
-step planning, we have

Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\geq Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
. Furthermore, since optimal planning can bring at most additional expected reward of
\gamma^{k}
on top of the returns guaranteed by
k
-steps planning, we can see that
Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\leq Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)+\gamma^{k}
. Therefore, for every
k\geq 1
, we have

Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\leq Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)\leq Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)+\gamma^{k}\,,

which shows that
Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
is also
\tau
-estimable.

In the remainder of the proof, we show that E-AIXI
RO
is implementable on a POM with access to reflective oracle
\tau
. The proof for
k
-E-AIXI
RO
is similar and hence we omit it.

We need to generalize
63
to incorporate action spaces with
|\mathcal{A}|>2
. Assume that we have observed history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, and we would like to compute
\argmax_{a\in\cal A}Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
. How can we do this using a
\tau
-POM that takes
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
as input?

Since we have a complete prefix-free encoding of
\mathcal{A}
, we can construct a binary tree to navigate the prefix-free encoding, with each leaf corresponding to a valid encoding of an action within
\mathcal{A}
, and the internal nodes representing the common prefix of its descendant leaves. The root node corresponds to the empty string. Choosing an action
a\in\mathcal{A}
corresponds to starting from the root node and then descending the tree until reaching a leaf. Each move represents deciding one bit of the code of the action to be taken.

Let us fix a history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, and define for each node
n
in the tree a value
v_{n}
as follows:

•

Each leaf node has the value
Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
with
a
the action encoded by the leaf, which we know to be
\tau
-estimable from the discussion above.

•

Each parent node has as value the maximum of the values of its two children nodes. Hence its value is the maximum of the values of its descendant leaves, which are all
\tau
-estimable. Therefore, the value of each node is also
\tau
-estimable.

We can interpret taking the action
\argmax_{a\in\cal A}Q_{\rho}^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
as starting on the root node and then descending the tree by recursively going to the child node having the higher value (with ties broken arbitrarily), until reaching a leaf.

Now if we are at a node
n
which is not a leaf, we will have two children
n_{0},n_{1}
, where
n_{0}
(resp.
n_{1}
) corresponds to appending the bit 0 (resp. 1) to the bit-string of the node
n
. We would like to determine the child node with the maximum value. Notice that
v_{n_{1}}\geq v_{n_{0}}
if and only if
(v_{n_{1}}-v_{n_{0}}+1)/2\geq 1/2
. Now since the values
v_{n_{0}}
and
v_{n_{1}}
are
\tau
-estimable, the quantity
(v_{n_{1}}-v_{n_{0}}+1)/2\in[0,1]
is
\tau
-estimable as well, and hence also
\tau
-sampleable, by
Lemma

5.27
. Therefore, there exists a
\tau
-POM
M_{n}^{\tau}
with output distribution satisfying
\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(1\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=(v_{n_{1}}-v_{n_{0}}+1)/2
. In summary, we managed to construct for each non-leaf node
n
, a machine
M_{n}
such that
\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(1\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq 1/2
if and only if
v_{n_{1}}\geq v_{n_{0}}
. In particular, we have:

•

O^{\tau}(\langle M_{n},{\text{\ae}_{<t}},\frac{1}{2}\rangle)=1
implies that
\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(1\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq 1/2
and hence
v_{n_{1}}\geq v_{n_{0}}
.

•

O^{\tau}(\langle M_{n},{\text{\ae}_{<t}},\frac{1}{2}\rangle)=0
implies that
\lambda_{M_{n}}^{\tau}(1\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\leq 1/2
and hence
v_{n_{1}}\leq v_{n_{0}}
.

Therefore, the bit that is returned by
O^{\tau}(\langle M_{n},{\text{\ae}_{<t}},\frac{1}{2}\rangle)
corresponds to going to the child node with the higher value (with ties broken arbitrarily), which is what we want.

We can now leverage this to construct a single machine
M^{*}
implementing an optimal policy as follows: On input
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, we first set
n
to the root node, and then as long as
n
is not a leaf, we repeat the following:

1.

b\leftarrow O^{\tau}(\langle M_{n},{\text{\ae}_{<t}},\frac{1}{2}\rangle)
.

2.

n\leftarrow n_{b}
.

Once we exit the loop, we get to a leaf node
n
corresponding to an action
a\in\mathcal{A}
with code
\langle a\rangle
, which we would then write on the output tape. This action corresponds to an optimal action maximizing
Q^{*}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a)
.

Now since (
k
-)E-AIXI
RO
agents are implementable on a POM with access to
\tau
, we can see that if such agents are combined with a multi-agent environment
\overline{\nu}
that is implementable on a POM with access to
\tau
, then the resulting POM induces a universe in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\tau-\mathrm{RO}}
.
∎

C.17
Proof of
Theorem

5.31

Let
\rho=\rho^{\mathrm{RUI}}=\rho_{\tau-\mathrm{RUI}}^{w}
. We claim that it is sufficient for our purposes to show that there exists an rPOM with access to a
w
-RUI-oracle
\tau
which takes as input a binary string
\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a,k,\epsilon\rangle
encoding
(\text{\ae}_{*},a,k,\epsilon)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathbb{N}\times(\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1))
, it computes an approximation
\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}(\text{\ae}_{*},a)
which is
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
-away from
Q^{k}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{*},a)
. We can then define the
\pi
policy as the one that returns

\argmax_{a^{\prime}}\tilde{Q}^{k_{t},\epsilon_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})\,,

where ties are broken arbitrarily. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})>0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\tilde{Q}^{k_{t},\epsilon_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\tilde{Q}^{k_{t},\epsilon_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad Q^{k_{t}}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})+\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{2}\geq\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{k_{t}}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})-\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad Q^{k_{t}}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\rho}^{k_{t}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a^{\prime})-\epsilon_{t}\,,

as required by
Definition

4.32
.

Now we turn to showing that we can get an approximation
\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
of
Q^{k}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
using a rPOM with access to the
w
-RUI-oracle
\tau
, i.e., a
\tau
-POM computation that makes a computably bounded number of requests to the
w
-RUI-oracle
\tau
.

We first show that we can do this for
k=1
. Note that analogously to equation
33
, we have

Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=Q_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=t}^{t^{\prime}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{i})\,.

(42)

Let
t_{\epsilon}>0
be a positive integer that is large enough so that
(1-\gamma)\sum_{i>t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i}<\frac{\epsilon}{4}
, e.g.,
t_{\epsilon}=\left\lceil\frac{\log(\epsilon/4)}{\log\gamma}\right\rceil
. In this case, we have
(1-\gamma)\sum_{i>t+t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i-t}<\frac{\epsilon}{4}
for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
. We claim that it is sufficient to obtain for every
a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
and every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t-1}
an approximation
\tilde{Q}^{1,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
which is at most
\frac{\epsilon}{4}
-away from

(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=t}^{t+t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{i})\,.

(43)

Indeed, if we have this, then we get for all
a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}^{1,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-Q_{\rho}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})|

\displaystyle=\left|\tilde{Q}^{1,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-\lim_{t^{\prime}\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=t}^{t^{\prime}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{i})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\tilde{Q}^{1,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=t}^{t+t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{i})\right|+(1-\gamma)\sum_{i>t+t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i-t}

\displaystyle<\frac{\epsilon}{4}+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,.

Now we show that equation
43
can indeed be approximated up to an additive error of at most
\frac{\epsilon}{4}
using a
\tau
-rPOM.

Using
Lemma

5.15
, we can get an approximation
\tilde{\rho}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
of
\rho(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
for every
(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}
and every
i\in\{t,\ldots,t+t_{\epsilon}\}
, such that

|\tilde{\rho}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})-{\rho}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})|\leq\tilde{\epsilon}:=\frac{\epsilon}{4(t_{\epsilon}+1)|\mathcal{E}|(|\mathcal{A}|\times|\mathcal{E}|)^{t_{\epsilon}}}\,,

and the number
N_{\epsilon}
of oracle requests that is needed depends (computably) only on
\epsilon
.
104
104

104

We can choose
\displaystyle N_{\epsilon}=\max_{1\leq i\leq t_{\epsilon}+1}\max_{(e_{1},\text{\ae}_{2:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-1}}N_{\epsilon,\langle e_{1}\text{\ae}_{2:i}\rangle}
, where
N_{\epsilon,h^{\prime}}
(for
h^{\prime}:=\langle e_{1}\text{\ae}_{2:i}\rangle\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
) is as in
Lemma

5.15
.
By defining

\tilde{Q}^{1,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t}):=(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=t}^{t+t_{\epsilon}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}\tilde{\rho}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})r(e_{i})\,,

we get our desired approximation. Furthermore, the total number of oracle requests in the above procedure is at most
\tilde{N}_{1,\epsilon}:=(t_{\epsilon}+1)|\mathcal{E}|(|\mathcal{A}|\times|\mathcal{E}|)^{t_{\epsilon}}N_{\epsilon}
, which computably depends only on
|\mathcal{A}|
,
|\mathcal{E}|
and
\epsilon
.

Now we turn to showing our claim that
Q^{k}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
can be approximated using a
\tau
-rPOM. We have just shown how to do the approximation for
k=1
. Now
since from equation
11
we have

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})}\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right]\,,

we can use a recursive procedure to approximate
Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}\tilde{Q}^{k-1,\epsilon/2}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right]\,,

where

\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
can be obtained using
Lemma

5.15
in such a way that

|\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-{\rho}(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})|\leq\tilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}:=\frac{\epsilon}{4|\mathcal{E}|}\,,

and the number
N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
of oracle requests that is needed depends (computably) only on
\epsilon
and
|\mathcal{E}|
. The total number of oracle requests
\tilde{N}_{k,\epsilon}
to compute
\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
is equal to

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{k,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{E}|N_{\epsilon}^{\prime}+|\mathcal{E}||\mathcal{A}|\tilde{N}_{k-1,\epsilon/2}=|\mathcal{E}|\sum_{i=0}^{k-2}|\mathcal{E}|^{i}|\mathcal{A}|^{i}N_{\epsilon/2^{i}}^{\prime}+|\mathcal{E}|^{k-1}|\mathcal{A}|^{k-1}\tilde{N}_{1,\epsilon/2^{k-1}}\,,

which computably depends only on
|\mathcal{A}|
,
|\mathcal{E}|
,
\epsilon
and
k
.

It remains to show that
\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
as defined above indeed yields the desired approximation. Now assuming the inductive hypothesis that
|\tilde{Q}^{k-1,\epsilon}(\text{\ae}_{*},a)-Q^{k-1}_{\rho}(\text{\ae}_{*},a)|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for all
(\text{\ae}_{*},a,\epsilon)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\times(\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1])
, we have

\left|\tilde{Q}^{k-1,\epsilon/2}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})-Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\tilde{Q}^{k,\epsilon}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle-Q_{\rho}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}|\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})-\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})|\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\tilde{Q}^{k-1,\epsilon/2}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\left|\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}}\tilde{Q}^{k-1,\epsilon/2}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})-\gamma\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{k-1}_{\rho}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{\epsilon}{4|\mathcal{E}|}+\sum_{e_{t}\in\mathcal{E}}\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\gamma\frac{\epsilon}{4}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,.

C.18
Proof of
Theorem

5.33
:
k
-step planners do not always converge to infinite-horizon optimal planning

We start by studying decoupled variants of AIXI that perform
k
-step planning (e.g., Self-AIXI
(
12
)
for which
k=1
). We give a counterexample showing that such agents do not always converge to infinite-horizon planning, hence disproving the sensibly-off policy assumption. We then generalize the counterexample to the coupled case and show that
k
-E-AIXI agents do not always converge to the embedded best response w.r.t. their mixture universe model
\rho
.

C.18.1
Counter-example for
k
-step planning decoupled Bayesian agents such as Self-AIXI

We demonstrate that Self-AIXI agents and their variants performing
k
-step planning do not always converge to the best response w.r.t. their mixture environment model
\xi
. This violation occurs because we can adversarially choose the reference universal machine for defining the Kolmogorov complexity to construct some ‘dogmatic belief’ over the agent’s own future behavior. As a result, the agent fails to converge to the optimal infinite-horizon policy, becoming trapped by a dogmatic and pessimistic self-model.

We will be interested in
k
-step planning decoupled Bayesian agents w.r.t. the mixture environment
\xi
and mixture policy
\zeta
which are induced by a Solomonoff prior over the class of environments (resp. policies) which are implementable by APOMs with access to an arbitrary probabilistic oracle
\tau
.

The overall argument is as follows:

•

For every
k\geq 1
, we design a specific environment
\mu_{k}
and a specific policy
\pi_{\mathrm{bad}}
such that
k
-step planning w.r.t.
\mu_{k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{bad}}}
is not optimal for the environment
\mu_{k}
.

•

Then we argue that we can design
U
such that
\zeta_{U}\approx\pi_{\mathrm{bad}}
and
\xi_{U}\approx\mu_{k}
, and hence
\xi_{U}^{\zeta_{U}}\approx\mu_{k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{bad}}}
. This means that
k
-step planning with respect to
\xi_{U}^{\zeta_{U}}
would not be optimal if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{k}
(because
k
-step planning w.r.t.
\mu_{k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{bad}}}
is not optimal for
\mu_{k}
). On the other hand, infinite-horizon planning w.r.t.
\xi_{U}\approx\mu_{k}
would be optimal if
\mu_{k}
is the ground-truth environment.

Let us define a specific ground-truth environment,
\mu_{R,k}
, for an integer
k\geq 1
, and a parameter
R\in(0,1)
, as follows:

•

Action Space
:
\mathcal{A}=\{\text{up},\text{down}\}
.

•

Observation Space
: A trivial space
\mathcal{O}=\{o_{0}\}
.

•

Reward Space
:
\mathcal{R}:=\{0,R,1\}
.

•

Percept Space
:
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{R}
.

•

Reward Function
: The reward at time
t
depends on the history of actions
a_{1:t}
:

r_{t}(a_{1:t})=\begin{cases}R&\text{if }a_{i}=\text{up for all }1\leq i\leq t\\
1&\text{if }t\geq k+1\text{ and }a_{t-k:t}=(\text{down},\dots,\text{down})\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(44)

•

Discount Factor
:
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

The agent faces a choice: receive a consistent, smaller reward
R
by always playing ‘up’, or endure
k
steps of zero reward to unlock a higher, perpetual reward of 1 by consistently playing ‘down’. If
R
is too low then one can show that the optimal policy with respect to
\mu_{R,k}
is the policy
\pi_{\mathrm{down}}
which always plays ‘down’. On the other hand, if
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
is the policy that always plays ‘up’, then a
k
-step planner w.r.t.
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
will never play the
\mathrm{down}
action because the self-model, which is used by the
k
-step-planner to reason about its future behavior after
k
steps, predicts that it will revert back to the
\mathrm{up}
action after
k
-steps forever after, and all these actions would have a reward of zero.

In fact, we will show a generalization of the aforementioned facts: If
\nu
is an environment that is sufficiently close to
\mu_{R,k}
, then the optimal policy with respect to
\nu
is to always play ‘down’. Similarly, if
\pi
is a policy that is also sufficiently close to
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
, then the
k
-step planner w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
will always play the sub-optimal action ‘up’ when the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
.

In order to establish this, we first develop a few helpful terminology and facts regarding the approximations of
\mu_{R,k}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
, respectively.

Definition C.16
.

Let
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{approx}}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and let
\epsilon>0
. We say that a policy
\tilde{\pi}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{approx}}
if for every
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{approx}}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

|\tilde{\pi}(a|\text{\ae}_{*})-\pi(a|\text{\ae}_{*})|\leq\epsilon\,.

Similarly, we say that an environment
\tilde{\nu}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}

\epsilon
-approximates
\nu
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{approx}}
if for every
(\text{\ae}_{*},a,e)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{approx}}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E}
, we have

|\tilde{\nu}(e|\text{\ae}_{*},a)-\nu(e|\text{\ae}_{*},a)|\leq\epsilon\,.

The following lemma establishes a few useful bounds on the probabilities of policies and environments which
{\epsilon}
-approximate other policies and environments.

Lemma C.17
.

If
\tilde{\pi}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}

\epsilon_{\mathrm{p}}
-approximates another policy
\pi:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{approx}}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and if
\tilde{\nu}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}

\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}}
-approximates another environment
\nu:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mathrm{e},\mathrm{approx}}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
, then for every
t>0
, every
i\geq 0
, and every
\text{\ae}_{1:{t+i}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t+i}
for which
{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{p,\mathrm{approx}}
and
{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{e,\mathrm{approx}}
, we have

\tilde{\nu}^{\tilde{\pi}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\geq(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{p}})^{i}(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}})^{i+1}\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,,

and

\tilde{\nu}^{\tilde{\pi}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\leq(1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{p}})^{i}(1+\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}})^{i+1}\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}^{\tilde{\pi}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{i}\tilde{\pi}(a_{t+j}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+j}})\prod_{j=0}^{i}\tilde{\nu}(e_{t+j}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+j}}a_{t+j})

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{j=1}^{i}(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}})\tilde{\pi}(a_{t+j}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+j}})\prod_{j=0}^{i}(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}})\tilde{\nu}(e_{t+j}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+j}}a_{t+j})

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{p}})^{i}(1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{e}})^{i+1}\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,,

and the upper bound is shown similarly.
∎

We define the set
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
to be the set of all histories which are compatible with
\mu_{R,k}
, i.e.,

\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}=\{\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}:e_{i}=(o_{0},r_{i}(a_{1:i}))\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq l(\text{\ae}_{*})\}\,.

Lemma C.18
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let
\nu
be an environment that
\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
. For every
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
, we have

•

The optimal
Q^{*}
value in the environment
\nu
after playing ‘down’ can be bounded from below as follows

Q^{*}_{\nu}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{down})\geq\frac{\gamma^{k}(1-\epsilon)^{k+1}(1-\gamma)}{1-\gamma+\gamma\epsilon}\,.

•

The optimal
Q^{*}
value in the environment
\nu
after playing ‘up’ can be bounded from above as follows

Q^{*}_{\nu}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{up})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\left\{(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}\right\}\,.

In particular, if
R<\gamma^{k}
, then there exists a small enough
\epsilon
such that

Q^{*}_{\nu}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{up})<Q^{*}_{\nu}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{down})\,,

and hence the optimal planner w.r.t.
\nu
would always play the ‘
\mathrm{down}
’ action if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
, as all produced histories would belong to
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
for which the bounds on
Q^{*}
apply.

Proof.

Let
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
and let
t=l(\text{\ae}_{*})+1
so that
\text{\ae}_{*}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}
.
Let
a_{\geq t}=(\mathrm{down})^{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}
be an infinite sequence of ‘down’ actions, and then recursively define
e_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}
as
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
for every
i\geq 0
, where the reward function
r_{t+i}
is given in equation
44
. Notice that
\mu_{R,k}(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})=1
, and hence

\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})\geq 1-{\epsilon}
for all
i\geq 0
. Let
\pi_{\mathrm{down}}
be the policy that always takes the ‘down’ action. We have

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{down})

\displaystyle=Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq Q_{\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\lim_{m\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:t\leq i\leq m\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t}^{\prime},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}r(e_{i}^{\prime})\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}^{\prime}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq k\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq k\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq k\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i}\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{\geq}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geq k\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i}(1-\epsilon)^{i+1}=\frac{\gamma^{k}(1-\epsilon)^{k+1}(1-\gamma)}{1-\gamma+\gamma\epsilon}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from equation
33
,
(\dagger)
follows from the fact that
a_{t+i-k:t+i}
is a substring of
a_{\geq t}
and hence it is all ‘down’, and
(\ddagger)
follows from
Lemma

C.17
and the fact that
\pi_{\mathrm{down}}
0-approximates itself, and hence

\nu^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\geq(1-\epsilon)^{i+1}(1-0)^{i}\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{down}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=(1-\epsilon)^{i+1}\,.

Now we turn to proving the upper bound on
Q^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}(\text{\ae}_{*},\mathrm{up})
for
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
. Again,
let
t=l(\text{\ae}_{*})+1
so that
\text{\ae}_{*}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}
. Let
i\geq 0
and assume that
\text{\ae}_{t:t+i-1}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i}
is an extension of
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
in such a way that
{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+i-1}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Note that for
i=0
, we use the convention that
\text{\ae}_{t:t-1}=\varepsilon
and
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{0}=\{\varepsilon\}
. Let
a_{t+i}\in\mathcal{A}
and let
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
so that
\text{\ae}_{1:{t+i}}={\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. We have

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t+i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\nu(e_{t+i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})((1-\gamma)r(e_{t+i}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i}^{\prime},a_{t+i+1}))

\displaystyle=\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})((1-\gamma)r(e_{t+i})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i},a_{t+i+1}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t+i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t+i}^{\prime}\neq e_{t+i}\end{subarray}}\nu(e_{t+i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})((1-\gamma)r(e_{t+i}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i}^{\prime},a_{t+i+1}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon+(1-\gamma)r(e_{t+i})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i},a_{t+i+1})

\displaystyle=\epsilon+(1-\gamma)r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i},a_{t+i+1})

(45)

where
(\ast)
follows from the following facts:

•

\nu

{\epsilon}
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Now since
{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, we have

|\mu_{R,k}(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})-\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})|\leq\epsilon\,.

•

Since
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
is compatible with the deterministic environment
\mu_{R,k}
, we have
\mu_{R,k}(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})=1
, and hence
|1-\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})|\leq\epsilon

which means that

1-\epsilon\leq\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})\leq 1\,.

•

(1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime},a_{t+1})\leq 1
and hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t+i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t+i}^{\prime}\neq e_{t+i}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\nu(e_{t+i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})((1-\gamma)r(e_{t+i}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i}^{\prime},a_{t+i+1}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t+i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t+i}^{\prime}\neq e_{t+i}\end{subarray}}\nu(e_{t+i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})\leq 1-\nu(e_{t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i})\leq\epsilon\,.

By defining,
\alpha=1-\gamma
, equation
45
simplifies to

Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}},a_{t+i})\leq\epsilon+\alpha r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+i+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}a_{t+i}e_{t+i},a_{t+i+1}))\,.

(46)

We will show by induction on
\ell\geq 1
that for every
t\geq 0
and every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, we have

Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}+\max_{a_{t+1:t+\ell}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}}\left\{\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma^{\ell}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell}}a_{t+\ell},e_{t+\ell})\right\}\,,

(47)

where for
t\leq i\leq t+\ell
, we define
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
and we use this in
{\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell}}
. For
\ell=1
, the inequality follows immediately from equation
46
.

Now let
\ell>1
and assume that equation
47
holds for
\ell-1
, i.e.,
for every
t\geq 0
and every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, we have

Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}+\max_{a_{t+1:t+\ell-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell-1}}\left\{\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma^{\ell-1}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell-1}}a_{t+\ell-1},e_{t+\ell-1})\right\}\,,

(48)

By combining this with equation
46
, we get

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}(

\displaystyle{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\epsilon+\alpha r_{t}(a_{1:t})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1}))

\displaystyle=\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\{\alpha r_{t}(a_{1:t})+\gamma Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+1}},a_{t+1}))\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigg\{\alpha r_{t}(a_{1:t})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma\left(\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}+\max_{a_{t+2:t+\ell}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell-1}}\left\{\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-2}\gamma^{i}r_{t+1+i}(a_{1:t+1+i})+\gamma^{\ell-1}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell}}a_{t+\ell},e_{t+\ell})\right\}\right)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}+\max_{a_{t+1:t+\ell}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}}\left\{\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma^{\ell}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell}}a_{t+\ell},e_{t+\ell})\right\}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from equation
46
and
(\dagger)
follows from equation
48
applied to
Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+1}},a_{t+1})
. We conclude that equation
47
holds for all
\ell\geq 1
. By taking
\ell\to\infty
, we get

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}+\sup_{a_{>t}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}}\left\{\alpha\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\gamma^{\ell}Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t+\ell}}a_{t+\ell},e_{t+\ell})\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\alpha\sup_{a_{>t}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\alpha\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}:\\
a_{t}=\mathrm{up}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})\,.

Now for every
\ell>0
, let

\mathcal{S}_{\ell}=\{a_{>0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}:a_{i}=\mathrm{up}\text{ for }i\leq\ell,\text{ and }a_{\ell+1}=\mathrm{down}\}\,,

i.e.,
\cal{S}_{\ell}
is the set of all infinite sequences of actions which start with
\ell
‘up’ actions followed by a ‘down’ action. Furthermore let

\mathcal{S}_{\infty}=\{(\mathrm{up})^{\infty}\}\,,

be the (singleton) set containing the single infinite sequence of up actions. Clearly,

\{a_{1:\infty}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}:a_{1}=\mathrm{up}\}=\bigcup_{1\leq\ell\leq\infty}\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\,,

where ‘
1\leq\ell\leq\infty
’ is a shorthand for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}=\{1,2,\ldots\}\cup\{\infty\}
. Hence, we can rewrite the upper bound on
Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})
as follows:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\alpha\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}:\\
a_{t}=\mathrm{up}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\alpha\sup_{1\leq\ell\leq\infty}\sup_{a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})\,.

Now notice that:

•

If
\ell=\infty
, then

\sup_{a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{<t}a_{t:t+i})\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}R=\frac{R}{1-\gamma}\,,

where the upper bound follows because the reward corresponding to an up action can be at most
R
.

•

If
1\leq\ell<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{<t}a_{t:t+i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\gamma^{i}R+\sum_{i=\ell+k}^{\infty}\gamma^{i}=\frac{(1-\gamma^{\ell})R}{1-\gamma}+\frac{\gamma^{\ell+k}}{1-\gamma}\,,

where the upper bound
(\ast)
follows because for
a_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
we have:

–

The actions
(a_{t^{\prime}})_{t\leq t^{\prime}<t+\ell}
are all up and hence they all get a reward of at most
R
.

–

The action
a_{t+\ell}
is down. Now since
a_{t+\ell-1}=\mathrm{up}
, all the actions
(a_{t^{\prime}})_{t+\ell\leq t^{\prime}<t+\ell+k-1}
will get a reward of 0.

–

The actions
(a_{t^{\prime}})_{t^{\prime}\geq t+k}
can each get a reward of at most 1.

We conclude that

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\frac{\alpha}{1-\gamma}\max\left\{R,\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\left\{(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\left\{(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+\sup_{\ell\geq 1}\left\{(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}\right\}\,,

where
(\dagger)
holds because we assumed that
{\epsilon}
is sufficiently small so that
\gamma<1
and hence

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}=R\,.

It remains to show that if
R<\gamma^{k}
, then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})\leq Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})
. For this, notice that if
R<\gamma^{k}
, then
R<\gamma^{k}
as well and hence

(1-\gamma^{\ell})R+\gamma^{\ell+k}=R+\gamma^{\ell}(\gamma^{k}-R)\leq R+\gamma(\gamma^{k}-R)\,,

and hence

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}+R+\gamma(\gamma^{k}-R)\,.

In particular

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})-Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\gamma^{k}(1-\epsilon)^{k+1}(1-\gamma)}{1-\gamma+\gamma\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}-R-\gamma(\gamma^{k}-R)\,.

By taking the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\gamma^{k}(1-\epsilon)^{k+1}(1-\gamma)}{1-\gamma+\gamma\epsilon}-\frac{\epsilon}{1-\gamma}-R-\gamma(\gamma^{k}-R)=\gamma^{k}-R-\gamma(\gamma^{k}-R)=(\gamma^{k}-R)(1-\gamma)>0\,.

We conclude that for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, we have
Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})>Q^{*}_{\nu}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})
for every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. In particular, if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
, then an optimal planner w.r.t.
\nu
always takes the ‘down’ action.
∎

Now we will argue that if
\nu

\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, and if
\pi

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on a suitable subset of
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, then the
k
-step optimal planner w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
will always play the suboptimal ‘up’ action if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
.

As a warm-up, let us consider the case where
\nu=\mu_{R,k}
and
\pi=\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
, and let us analyze the
k
-step planner w.r.t.
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
. Assume that the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
and assume that we observed a history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
of
t-1
‘up’ actions. What will the
k
-step planner do? By definition, it will choose the action
a_{t}
which maximizes
Q^{k}_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
. Now because
\mu_{R,k}
is deterministic, we can write the expression of
Q^{k}_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
as

Q^{k}_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\gamma^{i}r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})+\gamma^{k}V_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+k-1})\,,

where in
\text{\ae}_{t:t+k-1}
, we let
e_{t^{\prime}}=(o_{0},r_{t^{\prime}}(a_{1:t^{\prime}}))
for
t\leq t^{\prime}<t+k
. Now notice that:

•

If
a_{t}=\mathrm{down}
, then
r_{t^{\prime}}(a_{1:t^{\prime}})=0
for all
t\leq t^{\prime}<t+k
because a ‘down’ action will make the reward of any subsequent ‘up’ action to be 0, and furthermore, the reward of a ‘down’ action is only worth it when there is a streak of at least
k
‘down’ actions. Furthermore,
V_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{\ae}_{t:t+k-1})
would also be equal to zero because
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
only samples up ‘actions’ which would have zero rewards since they were preceded by
a_{t}=\mathrm{down}
.
105
105

105

It is worth noting, that while
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=0
if
a_{t}=0
, the conditional
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}(\cdot|\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
is still well defined (cf.
Remark

B.30
). Therefore,
Q^{k}_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
is well-defined every for
a_{t}=\mathrm{down}
.

•

If
a_{t}=\mathrm{up}
, then

Q^{k}_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\geq Q_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=R>0\,.

Therefore the
k
-step planner w.r.t.
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
will always play the suboptimal ‘up’ action. The main reason for the failure of the
k
-step planner is w.r.t.
\mu_{R,k}^{\mu_{\mathrm{up}}}
is that its self-model is a bad policy that is incapable of “continuing on doing the right thing". Because of this, the
k
-step planner is forced to play sub-optimally, as it does not “trust its future self" on doing the right thing.

Now we turn to showing a similar result for general
\nu
and
\pi
that
{\epsilon}
-approximate
\mu_{R,k}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}
be the set of action sequences which contains a streak of ‘up’ actions followed by a streak of exactly
k
‘down’ actions followed by a streak of ‘up’ actions, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}=\bigcup_{t\geq k}\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq t-k}\{\mathrm{up}\}^{i}\times\{\mathrm{down}\}^{k}\times\{\mathrm{up}\}^{t-k-i}\,,

and let

\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}=\{{\text{\ae}_{<t}}:t>0,\,{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}\text{ and }a_{<t}\in\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}\}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}\,.

Furthermore, let
\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}
be the set of action sequences which contain only ‘up’ actions, and let

\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}=\{{\text{\ae}_{<t}}:t>0,\,{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}\text{ and }a_{<t}\in\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}\}\subseteq\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}\,.

Lemma C.19
.

Let
\epsilon>0
, let
\nu
be an environment that
\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, and let
\pi
be a policy that
\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}\cup\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. For every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
we have

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})\geq\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\epsilon)R}{1-\gamma(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\,,

and

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})\leq k\epsilon+\gamma^{k}(\gamma+2\epsilon)\,.

In particular, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
and
R>\gamma^{k+1}
, we have

Q^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})<Q^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})\,,

and hence the
k
-step planner w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
will always play the ‘up’ action if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
.

Proof.

Let us first prove the lower bound on
Q^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})
. Let
a_{\geq t}=(\mathrm{up})^{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}^{\infty}
be an infinite sequence of ‘up’ actions, and then recursively define
e_{\geq t}\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}
as
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
for every
i\geq 0
, where the reward function
r_{t+i}
is given in equation
44
. Clearly
{\text{\ae}_{<t+i}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
for all
i\geq 0
. Furthermore, since
a_{1:t+i}
consists only of up actions, we have
r(e_{t+i})=r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})=R
for all
i\geq 0
. Notice that

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\geq}}Q^{1}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})=Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\lim_{m\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:t\leq i\leq m\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i-t}\sum_{(e_{t}^{\prime},\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}\times\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{i-t}}r(e_{i})\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}^{\prime}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{m\to\infty}(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:t\leq i\leq m\end{subarray}}\gamma^{i-t}r(e_{i})\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{i\geq 0}\gamma^{i}R\nu^{\pi}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{\geq}}(1-\gamma)\sum_{i\geq 0}\gamma^{i}R(1-\epsilon)^{2i+1}\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}(e_{t}\text{\ae}_{t+1:t+i}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{i\geq 0}\gamma^{i}R(1-\epsilon)^{2i+1}=\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\epsilon)R}{1-\gamma(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from
Lemma

C.10
,
(\dagger)
follows from equation
33
,
(\ddagger)
follows from
Lemma

C.17
.

Now we turn to proving an upper bound on
Q^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})
. For this, let us define
a_{t}=\mathrm{down}
. We will show by induction on
k\geq 1
that

Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq k\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}}\left\{(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})+\gamma^{k}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\right\}\,,

(49)

where we recursively define
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
for
0\leq i<k
and we use those in
{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}
. We again use the convention that for
k=1
, we have
\mathcal{A}^{k-1}=\mathcal{A}^{0}=\{\varepsilon\}
and
a_{t+1:t+k-1}=a_{t+1:t}=\varepsilon
.

We prove equation
49
by induction on
k
. For
k=1
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{{\nu^{\pi}}}^{1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=Q_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left((1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t}^{\prime}\neq e_{t}\end{subarray}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left((1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t}^{\prime}\neq e_{t}\end{subarray}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\right)+\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon+\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t})\right]\,,

where in
(\ast)
we used the fact that
(1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime})\leq 1
for all
e_{t}^{\prime}\in\cal E
, and that
{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\leq 1
, and in
(\dagger)
we used the fact that
{\nu^{\pi}}
is a semimeasure, and hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t}^{\prime}\neq e_{t}\end{subarray}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\leq 1-{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=1-\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})-\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\leq\epsilon\,.

Therefore, equation
49
holds for
k=1
.

Now let
k>1
and assume that equation
49
holds for
k-1
, i.e., for every
t>0
and every
({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t-1}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle Q^{k-1}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq(k-1)\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-2}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-2}}\left\{(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{k-2}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})+\gamma^{k-1}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k-1}})\right\}\,,

where
e_{t+i}
for
0\leq i<k-1
are recursively defined as
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
for
0\leq i<k-1
and we use those in
{\text{\ae}_{<t+k-1}}
.

Then, it follows from equation
11
that

\displaystyle Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime},a_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t}^{\prime}\neq e_{t}\end{subarray}}{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t}^{\prime})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}^{\prime},a_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\left[(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\,,

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
1-\epsilon\leq{\nu^{\pi}}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\leq 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq\epsilon+(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k-1}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t},a_{t+1})\,.

By using the induction hypothesis on the history
{\text{\ae}_{<t+1}}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e_{t}
, we get

\displaystyle Q_{\nu^{\pi}}^{k}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+(1-\gamma)r(e_{t})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\gamma\max_{a_{t+1}\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{(k-1)\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+2:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-2}}\left\{(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{k-2}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+1+i})+\gamma^{k-1}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=k\epsilon+\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}}\left\{(1-\gamma)\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\gamma^{i}r(e_{t+i})+\gamma^{k}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\right\}\,,

where
e_{t+i}
for
0\leq i<k
are again recursively defined as
e_{t+i}=(o_{0},r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i}))
and we use those in
{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}
. It follows by induction that equation
49
holds for every
k\geq 1
. Now for every
a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}
and every
1\leq i\leq k-1
, we have

•

If
a_{t+i}=\mathrm{up}
, then
r(e_{t+i})=r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})=0
because
a_{t+i}
is preceded by the
\mathrm{down}
action.

•

If
a_{t+i}=\mathrm{down}
, then
r(e_{t+i})=r_{t+i}(a_{1:t+i})=0
because
a_{<t}
is all-up, and hence
a_{t+i-k:t+i}
is not all down.

We conclude that

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\leq k\epsilon+\gamma^{k}\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\,.

Now we turn to proving an upper bound on
V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})
, which can be written as

\displaystyle V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})\,,

Let
a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{E}
and let
e_{t+k}=(o_{0},r_{t+k}(a_{1:t}))
. We have

\displaystyle Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{t+k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\nu(e_{t+k}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})((1-\gamma)r(e_{t+k})+\gamma V_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k}e_{t+k}))

\displaystyle\leq\gamma+(1-\gamma)\sum_{e_{t+k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\nu(e_{t+k}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})r(e_{t+k})

\displaystyle=\gamma+(1-\gamma)\nu(e_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})r(e_{t+k})+(1-\gamma)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t+k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t+k}^{\prime}\neq e_{t+k}\end{subarray}}\nu(e_{t+k}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})r(e_{t+k})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma+r(e_{t+k})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{t+k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\\
e_{t+k}^{\prime}\neq e_{t+k}\end{subarray}}\nu(e_{t+k}^{\prime}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})\leq\gamma+r(e_{t+k})+1-\nu(e_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\gamma+r(e_{t+k})+\epsilon\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the fact that
\nu

{\epsilon}
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Now notice that

•

If
a_{t+1:t+k-1}
contains an ‘up’ action, then
r(e_{t+k})=r_{t+k}(a_{1:t+k})=0
regardless of whether
a_{t+k}
is up or down. Therefore,

\displaystyle V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})(\gamma+r(e_{t+k})+\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})(\gamma+\epsilon)=\gamma+\epsilon\,.

•

If
a_{t+1:t+k-1}
is all-down, then
a_{t:t+k-1}
is all-down, and hence
a_{<t+k}\in\mathcal{A}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}
. Now since
\pi

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}
, we get

\displaystyle V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})

\displaystyle=\pi(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}\mathrm{up})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}:\\
a_{t+k}\neq\mathrm{up}\end{subarray}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t+k})

\displaystyle\leq\pi(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}\mathrm{up})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{t+k}\in\mathcal{A}:\\
a_{t+k}\neq\mathrm{up}\end{subarray}}\pi(a_{t+k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle\leq Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}\mathrm{up})+1-\pi(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle=Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}\mathrm{up})+\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})-\pi(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})

\displaystyle\leq Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}\mathrm{up})+\epsilon\,.

Now fix
a_{t+k}=\mathrm{up}
. We get

V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\leq Q_{\nu^{\pi}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}}a_{t})+\epsilon\leq\gamma+r(e_{t+k})+2\epsilon\,.

Since
a_{t}=\mathrm{down}
, we have
r(e_{t+k})=0
, and hence

V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\leq\gamma+2\epsilon\,.

We conclude that

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})\leq k\epsilon+\gamma^{k}\max_{a_{t+1:t+k-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-1}}V_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t+k}})\leq k\epsilon+\gamma^{k}(\gamma+2\epsilon)\,.

Now since

\displaystyle Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})-Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})\geq\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\epsilon)R}{1-\gamma(1-\epsilon)^{2}}-k\epsilon-\gamma^{k}(\gamma+2\epsilon)\,,

and since

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\epsilon)R}{1-\gamma(1-\epsilon)^{2}}-k\epsilon-\gamma^{k}(\gamma+2\epsilon)=R-\gamma^{k+1}\,,

we conclude that if
R>\gamma^{k+1}
, then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{up})>Q^{k}_{{\nu^{\pi}}}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},\mathrm{down})
for every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
consisting of only up actions. For such
\epsilon
, the
k
-step-planner w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
will always take the ‘up’ action if the ground-truth environment is
\mu_{R,k}
.
∎

Corollary C.20
.

Let
\epsilon>0
, let
\nu
be an environment that
\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, and let
\pi
be a policy that
\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}\cup\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Define the following two policies:

•

Let
\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\pi}}
be the embedded best response w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
, which is the same as the optimal policy
\pi^{*}_{\nu}
w.r.t.
\nu
.
106
106

106

Note that the conditionals of
\nu^{\pi}
are always well defined (cf.
Remark

B.30
) and we can define the embedded best response w.r.t. those conditionals. This boils down to optimal planning w.r.t.
\nu
.

•

Let
\pi^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}
be the
k
-step optimal planner w.r.t.
\nu^{\pi}
.

If
\gamma^{k+1}<R<\gamma^{k}
, and if
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, then if
\mu_{R,k}
is the ground-truth environment, the
\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\pi}}=\pi^{*}_{\nu}
always plays the ‘down’ action while the
\pi^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}
policy always plays the ‘up’ action. Furthermore, there exists a gap between the returns of
\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\pi}}
and
\pi^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=R\,,\quad\mu_{R,k}^{\pi^{k}_{\nu^{\pi}}}\text{-almost surely},

and

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V_{\mu_{R,k}^{\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\pi}}}}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=1\,,\quad\mu_{R,k}^{\pi^{*}_{\nu^{\pi}}}\text{-almost surely}.

Proof.

This is an immediate corollary of
Lemma

C.18
and
Lemma

C.19
.
∎

Now we turn to applying
Corollary

C.20
to the mixture policy and the mixture environment that are induced by the Solomonoff prior.

Let us first establish some useful notation, and a few helpful facts. For every APOM
M
, let
\mathbb{P}[M^{\tau}(x)=y]
be the probability that on input
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, the
\tau
-POM
M
halts with
y\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
written on the output tape. Then, we can define the policy
\pi_{M}^{\tau}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}
and environment
\nu_{M}^{\tau}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}
as follows:

\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=\mathbb{P}[M^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)=\langle a\rangle]\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{M}^{\tau}(e|\text{\ae}_{*},a)=\mathbb{P}[M^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle)=\langle e\rangle]\,.

We say that a policy
\pi
is
\tau
-POM-implementable if there exists
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
such that
\pi_{M}^{\tau}=\pi
. Similarly, we say that an environment
\nu
is
\tau
-POM-implementable if there exists
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
such that
\nu_{M}^{\tau}=\nu
. We denote the class of all
\tau
-POM-implementable policies as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and the class of all
\tau
-POM-implementable environments as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

Now for a universal monotone Turing machine
U
, define the mixtures
\zeta_{U,\tau}
and
\xi_{U,\tau}
as follows:

\zeta_{U,\tau}=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}\,,

and

\xi_{U,\tau}=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\nu_{M}^{\tau}\,.

By defining

w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\pi_{M}^{\tau}=\pi\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,,\quad\forall\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}\,,

and

w_{\mathrm{env}}^{U,\tau}(\nu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\nu_{M}^{\tau}=\nu\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,,\quad\forall\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}\,,

we get

\zeta_{U,\tau}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)\pi\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{U,\tau}=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{env}}^{U,\tau}(\nu)\nu\,.

Lemma C.21
.

Let
\tau
be an arbitrary probabilistic oracle. For every
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, there exists an APOM
M_{\pi,\nu}
such that the
\tau
-POM
M_{\pi,\nu}^{\tau}
implements both
\pi
and
\nu
, i.e.,
\pi_{M_{\pi,\nu}}^{\tau}=\pi
and
\nu_{M_{\pi,\nu}}^{\tau}=\nu
.

Proof.

Let
M_{p}
and
M_{e}
be two APOMs such that
\pi_{M_{p}}=\pi
and
\nu_{M_{e}}=\nu
. Then we can design an APOM
M_{\pi,\nu}
such that on input
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
it does the following:

•

If
x=\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
for some
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
, then it simulates
M_{p}
on input
x
and writes the output of the simulated
M_{p}
on its output tape.

•

If
x=\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle
for some
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}
, then it simulates
M_{e}
on input
x
and writes the output of the simulated
M_{e}
on its output tape.

•

Otherwise, it loops for ever.

Obviously, we have
\pi_{M_{\pi,\nu}}^{\tau}=\pi_{M_{p}}^{\tau}=\pi
and
\nu_{M_{\pi,\nu}}^{\tau}=\nu_{M_{e}}^{\tau}=\nu
.
∎

The following lemma shows that for every sufficiently big finite set
\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}^{*}
of binary strings, and every injective computable function
F:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
that keeps
\mathcal{S}_{0}
invariant, i.e.,
F(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{S}
, we can always design a universal monotone Turing machine
U
such that (i)
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is very likely under the Solomonoff prior, and (ii) for every (potentially infinite) subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{*}
of binary strings which is closed under
F
, i.e.,
F(\mathcal{S})=\{F(x):x\in\mathcal{S}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, the set
\mathcal{S}\cap F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})=\mathcal{S}\setminus(\mathcal{S}\setminus F(\mathcal{B}^{*}))
is almost as likely as
\mathcal{S}
according to the induced Solomonoff distribution.

Lemma C.22
.

Let
{\epsilon}>0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{0}
be an arbitrary finite set of binary strings containing at least
\lceil\log(2/\epsilon)\rceil
elements. Let
F:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
be a an arbitrary computable function for which
F(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. There exists a universal monotone Turing machine
U
such that

1.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{0}
has at least
1-\epsilon
probability according to the Solomonoff distribution:

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq 1-\epsilon\,.

2.

For every subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{*}
that is closed under
F
, i.e.,

F(\mathcal{S})=\{F(x):x\in\mathcal{S}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\,,

and every weight function
w:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
that is non-decreasing under
F
, i.e.,
w(F(x))\geq w(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, we have

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\cap F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

In particular,

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\cap F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

Proof.

Let
\ell=\lceil\log(2/\epsilon)\rceil
, and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}
be the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let us fix some arbitrary monotone Turing machine
T_{x_{i}}
which writes
x_{i}
on its output tape (without reading any bit from its input tape) and then halts.

For every monotone Turing machine
T
, define the monotone Turing machine
S_{F}(T)
which simulates
T
until the simulated machine
T
halts:

•

Prior to the halting of the simulated machine
T
, the real machine
S_{F}(T)
does not write any bit on its output tape. We only store the value of the (simulated) output tape of the simulated
T
on the working tape of
S_{F}(T)
.

•

When the simulated
T
attempts to read a bit from its input tape, we read the bit from the actual input tape of
S_{F}(T)
.

•

If the simulated
T
does not halt, then
S_{F}(T)
loops forever without writing any bit on its output tape (even if the simulated
T
writes some bits on its simulated output tape).

•

If the simulated
T
halts, the machine
S_{F}(T)
computes
F(x)
, where
x
is the output of the simulated
T
, and then writes
F(x)
on the output tape.

Clearly,
S_{F}(T)(p)=F(T(p))
for every
p\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
. Furthermore, we can computably get a description
\langle S_{F}(T)\rangle
of
S_{F}(T)
from a description
\langle T\rangle
of
T
.

Now let
T_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,T_{n}^{\prime},\ldots
be a computable enumeration of all monotone Turing machines, i.e., the mapping
n\mapsto\langle T_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
is computable.

We define another enumeration
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},\ldots
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n}=\begin{cases}T_{x_{n}}\quad&\text{if }1\leq n\leq\ell\,,\\
T^{\prime}_{\frac{n-\ell}{\ell}}\quad&\text{if }n>\ell\text{ and }\ell\text{ divides }n\,,\\
S_{F}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil\frac{n-\ell}{\ell}\rceil})\quad&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Clearly, the mapping
n\mapsto\langle T_{n}\rangle
is also computable.

Let
c:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
be the complete prefix-free encoding of positive natural numbers where
c(n)
is the bitstring having
n-1
ones followed by a zero.

We can then define the machine
U
so that
U(c(n)p)=T_{n}(p)
as follows:

•

The machine
U
starts reading the input tape bit by bit until it reads the first zero.

•

Let
n
be the number of bits that were read. The machine then computes the representation
\langle T_{n}\rangle
of the
n
-th machine
T_{n}
in the enumeration and then start simulating
T_{n}
.

This machine
U
is a universal monotone Turing machine by construction (as it can simulate any machine in the enumeration
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
which covers all monotone Turing machine).

For every
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we have
U(c(i))=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=T_{x_{i}}(\varepsilon)=x_{i}
, and hence
K_{U}(x_{i})\leq l(c(i))=i
. Therefore,

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-K_{U}(x_{i})}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-i}=1-2^{-\ell}\geq 1-\epsilon\,.

Now let
\mathcal{S}
be some arbitrary set which is closed under
F
, i.e.,
F(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{S}
. Let
x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Since
F(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime}
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we must have
x\notin\mathcal{S}_{0}
and hence for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we must have
x\neq x_{i}=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=U(c(i))
. Let
p_{x}
be an arbitrary bit-string for which
U(p_{x})=x
. From the structure of
U
, we know that there must exist
n>0
such that
p_{x}=c(n)p^{\prime}_{x}
for some
p^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
. Hence,
x=U(p_{x})=U(c(n)p^{\prime}_{x})=T_{n}(p^{\prime}_{x})
. Since
x\neq x_{i}=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=U(c(i))
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we must have
n>\ell
.

If
\ell
does not divide
n
, then
T_{n}=S_{F}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil(n-\ell)/\ell\rceil})
and hence

x=T_{n}(p^{\prime}_{x})=S_{F}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil(n-\ell)/\ell\rceil})(p^{\prime}_{x})=F(T^{\prime}_{\lceil(n-\ell)/\ell\rceil}(p^{\prime}_{x}))\,,

which is a contradiction because
x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Therefore,
\ell
must divide
n
. In particular,

x=T_{n}(p^{\prime}_{x})=T^{\prime}_{(n-\ell)/\ell}(p^{\prime}_{x})\,.

Now if we define
\tilde{p}_{x}=c(n-\ell+1)p^{\prime}_{x}
, we get

\displaystyle U(\tilde{p}_{x})

\displaystyle=U(c(n-\ell+1)p^{\prime}_{x})=T_{n-\ell+1}(p^{\prime}_{x})=S_{F}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil(n-2\ell+1)/\ell\rceil})(p^{\prime}_{x})

\displaystyle=S_{F}(T^{\prime}_{(n-\ell)/\ell})(p^{\prime}_{x})=F(T^{\prime}_{(n-\ell)/\ell}(p^{\prime}_{x}))=F(x)\,.

Therefore, for every bit-string
p_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
for which
U(p_{x})=x
, there exists a shorter bit-string
\tilde{p}_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
such that
U(\tilde{p}_{x})=F(x)
and
l(\tilde{p}_{x})=l(p_{x})-\ell+1
, hence,

K_{U}(F(x))\leq K_{U}(x)-\ell+1\,.

Thus, for every weight function
w:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
that is non-decreasing under
F
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(F(x))-\ell+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(F(x))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(F(x))2^{-K_{U}(F(x))}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{y\in F(\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*}))}w(y)2^{-K_{U}(y)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\diamond)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{y\in\mathcal{S}}w(y)2^{-K_{U}(y)}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from our choice of
\ell
,
(\dagger)
follows from the fact that
w
is non-decreasing under
F
,
(\ddagger)
follows from the injectivity of
F
, and
(\diamond)
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is closed under
F
, and hence
F(\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*}))\subseteq F(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{S}
.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\cap F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus(\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*}))}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}\setminus F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

∎

The following lemma leverages
Lemma

C.22
and shows how we can design a universal monotone Turing machine
U
for which
\zeta_{U,\tau}

{\epsilon}
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
and
\xi_{U,\tau}

{\epsilon}
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
.

Lemma C.23
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a monotone Turing machine
U
such that
\xi_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, and
\zeta_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}\cup\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.

Proof.

Let

, and let

and

, as in

. Let

. For example, for every

. What matters is that
\pi_{M_{i}}=\pi_{\mathrm{down}}
and

for all

. Let

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\left\{\langle M_{i}\rangle:1\leq i\leq\ell\right\}\,.

Now for every APOM

, define the machine

as follows:

Input:

x=\langle{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\rangle
or

Output:

Action
a_{t}
or Percept
e_{t}

Parse the input

using the complete prefix-free encodings of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{E}

if

Let

\mathrm{streak}\text{\_}\mathrm{found}\leftarrow\text{false}

Algorithm 5

The "reverting" APOM
S_{k}(M)

Notice that
\nu_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}=\mu_{R,k}
for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
. We assume that the “boiler-plate" structure of

is done in a way that ensures that
S_{k}(M)\neq M_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, e.g., we can just add some useless loop that does not exist in any of the machines
(M_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
.

Now define the function
F:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
as

F(x)=\begin{cases}\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle\quad&\text{if }x=\langle M\rangle\text{ for some }M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}\}\,,\\
x\quad&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(50)

Clearly,
F
is computable and injective. Let
U
be the universal monotone Turing machine of
