C.22
applied to
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{\langle M_{1}\rangle,\ldots,\langle M_{\ell}\rangle\}
,
F
and
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
. We have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M_{i}\rangle)}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

(51)

Now let

\mathcal{S}:=\left\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}\}\right\}=\left\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\}\,,

where

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}\}\,.

Clearly,
\mathcal{S}
is closed
107
107

107

Recall that we designed the wrapper
S_{k}
in a way that ensures that
S_{k}(M)\neq M_{i}
for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
and every
1\leq i\leq\ell
.
under
F
, i.e.,
F(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{S}
. Furthermore, if
x\notin\mathcal{S}
then
F(x)=x\notin\mathcal{S}
, hence

\mathcal{S}\cap F^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})=F(\mathcal{S})\,,

and from

we have

\sum_{x\in F(\mathcal{S})}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}w(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)},

for any weight function
w:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
that is non-decreasing under
F
. We can reformulate this by saying that for any weight function

that is non-decreasing under
S_{k}
, i.e., it satisfies
w(S_{k}(M))\geq w(M)
for every

, we have

\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}w(S_{k}(M))2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}w(M)2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,,

(52)

Given these properties of the chosen universal monotone Turing machine
U
, we proceed to prove the lemma.

We notice first that since
\pi_{M_{i}}=\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
and
\nu_{M_{i}}=\mu_{R,K}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{env}}^{U,\tau}(\mu_{R,k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\nu_{M}^{\tau}=\mu_{R,k}\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M_{i}\rangle)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\geq}}1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from equation
51

and similarly we can show that

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi_{\mathrm{up}})\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,.

We will show by induction on
t\geq 0
that for every
\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t}\cap\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
, we have

w(\mu_{R,k}|\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,.

For
t=0
, we have
w(\mu_{R,k}|\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=w(\mu_{R,k}|\varepsilon)=w(\mu_{R,k})\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
. Now let
t>0
, and assume that the claim is true for
t-1
. We have from

that

w(\mu_{R,k}|\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=w(\mu_{R,k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\frac{\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\xi_{U,\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\geq}}w(\mu_{R,k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\geq}}1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,,

where

follows from the fact that
\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=1
for
\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
and
(\dagger)
follows from the induction hypothesis
108
108

108

Note that
\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
implies that
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
as well.
. Hence the claim is true for all
t\geq 0
.

Now fix
t>0
and let
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t-1}\cap\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
. For every
a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
and every

, we have from

3.17
that

\xi_{U,\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}}w(\nu|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,,

hence

\displaystyle|\xi_{U,\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})-\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq|w(\mu_{R,k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-1|\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})+\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}\setminus\{\mu_{R,k}\}}w(\nu|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle\leq|w(\mu_{R,k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-1|+\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}\setminus\{\mu_{R,k}\}}w(\nu|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\leq 2(1-w(\mu_{R,k}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}))

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon\,.

We conclude that
\xi_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.

Similarly, one can show by induction that for histories
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
containing only up actions, that
w(\pi_{\mathrm{up}}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi_{\mathrm{up}})\geq 1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, and hence that for any
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
and

we have that
\zeta_{U,\tau}

-approximates

on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
:

|\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})|\leq\epsilon\,.

Now we turn to showing that
\zeta_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Note that the shortest history in
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
has length
k
. Let
t>k
and let
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t-1}\cap\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Since the shortest history in
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
has length
k
, we must have
t\geq k>0
. Let
a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
and for every
0<T\leq t
, let

\displaystyle\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T}\mid\mid e_{<T}):=\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{T}\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t^{\prime}}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}})\,,

so that

\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\frac{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})}{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})}\,.

(53)

From
Proposition

3.17
, we have

\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})\pi(a_{T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})\,,

where the posterior
w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})
is updated recursively as

w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})=\frac{w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T-1}})\pi(a_{T-1}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T-1}})}{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{T-1}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T-1}})}\,.

By recursive application (and starting from
w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid\varepsilon)=w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)
), this gives:

w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})=\frac{w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{T-1}\pi(a_{t^{\prime}}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}})}{\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{T-1}\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t^{\prime}}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}})}=\frac{w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)\pi(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})}{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})}\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T}\mid\mid e_{<T})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})\cdot\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})\pi(a_{T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}\frac{w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)\pi(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})}{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:T-1}\mid\mid e_{<T-1})}\pi(a_{T}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<T}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{pol}}^{U,\tau}(\pi)\pi(a_{1:T}\mid\mid e_{<T})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\pi_{M}^{\tau}=\pi\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi(a_{1:T}\mid\mid e_{<T})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:T}\mid\mid e_{<T})\,.

Combining this with equation
53
, we get

\displaystyle\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})}{\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})}=\frac{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})}{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\frac{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})}{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})}\,,

(54)

where in
(\ast)
we used the fact that for
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we have
M=M_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq\ell
and hence
\pi_{M}^{\tau}=\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
, which means that
\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})=\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})=0
because
a_{<t}
contains a substring of
k
down actions.

Now assume that
a_{t}=\text{up}
, and define

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S},S_{k}}=\left\{S_{k}(M):M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\,,

and notice that for every
T>t
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S},S_{k}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M^{\prime}\rangle)}\pi_{M^{\prime}}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})\,.

where in
(a)
we used the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S},S_{k}}
, in
(b)
we used the fact that
\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=1
because
a_{t}=\mathrm{up}
and
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
and hence contains the sequence
(\mathrm{down})^{k}
as a substring (which means that
S_{k}(M)
would deterministically return ‘up’ on input
{\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}}
).

By defining the weight function
w:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\to[0,1]
as
w(M)=\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})
for all
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}w(S_{k}(M))\,.

(55)

We will show that
w
is non-decreasing under
S_{k}
, i.e,
w(S_{k}(M))\geq w(M)
for all
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Let
0\leq t^{\prime}<t-k
be such that
a_{t^{\prime}:t^{\prime}+k-1}=(\mathrm{down})^{k}
, which exists because
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. We have

\displaystyle w(M)

\displaystyle=\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})=\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})=\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})=w(S_{k}(M))\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the following two facts:

•

If
i<t^{\prime}+k-1
, then
S_{k}(M)
produces the output of
M
on input
\text{\ae}_{1:{i}}
because
a_{<i}
does not contain a streak of
k
‘down’ actions. Therefore,
\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})=\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})
.

•

If
i\geq t^{\prime}+k-1
, then
a_{<i}
contains a streak of
k
downs and
a_{i}
must be the up action,
109
109

109

This follows from the structure of histories in
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.
hence
\pi_{S_{k}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})=1\geq\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{i}|\text{\ae}_{1:{i}})
.

We conclude that
w
is non-decreasing under
w
, and hence it follows from equation
55
and equation
52
that

\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{1:t}\mid\mid e_{<t})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{k}(M)\rangle)}w(S_{k}(M))

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}w(M)

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\pi_{M}^{\tau}(a_{<t}\mid\mid e_{<t-1})\,.

Substituting this back into the expression of
\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
in equation
54
, we get

\zeta_{U,\tau}(\mathrm{up}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\zeta_{U,\tau}(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq 1-\epsilon=\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(\mathrm{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\epsilon\,.

Now for
a\neq\mathrm{up}
, we have

\zeta_{U,\tau}(a\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\leq 1-\zeta_{U,\tau}(\mathrm{up}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\leq\epsilon=\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(a|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})+\epsilon\,.

In summary, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have

|\zeta_{U,\tau}(a\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(a\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})|\leq\epsilon\,.

Therefore,
\zeta_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.
∎

Putting everything together, we get the following theorem:

Theorem C.24
.

Consider a
k
-AIXI
τ
agent, i.e., a decoupled Bayesian agent employing
k
-step planning for a fixed integer
k\geq 1
, with a decoupled mixture universe model
\rho=\xi_{U,\tau}^{\zeta_{U,\tau}}
constructed from a mixture policy
\zeta_{U,\tau}
and a mixture environment
\xi_{U,\tau}
which are themselves constructed from a Solomonoff prior over the
\tau
-POM-computable policies and
\tau
-POM-computable environments, respectively. Then, there exists a computable ground-truth environment
\mu
and a reference universal monotone Turing machine
U
for defining the Kolmogorov complexity
K_{U}
in the Solomonoff prior, such that the agent interacting with
\mu
does not converge to the infinite-horizon optimal planner w.r.t.
\rho=\xi_{U,\tau}^{\zeta_{U,\tau}}
, despite the fact that this setup satisfies the grain-of-truth property (in case, e.g., of a reflective oracle). In particular, Self-AIXI does not always converge to AIXI.

Proof.

Fix
R
so that
\gamma^{k+1}<R<\gamma^{k}
. Let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small as indicated in
Corollary

C.20
and then construct
U
as in

Lemma

C.23
.
∎

C.18.2
Counterexample for embedded Bayesian agents

For a universal monotone Turing machine
U
, define the mixture universe
\rho_{U,\tau}
as follows:

\rho_{U,\tau}=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}\,.

By defining

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\tau}=\{\lambda_{M}^{\tau}:\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\}\,,

and

w_{\mathrm{uni}}^{U,\tau}(\lambda)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\lambda\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,,\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\tau}\,,

we get

\rho_{U,\tau}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}^{\tau}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}^{U,\tau}(\lambda)\lambda\,.

We start by showing a stronger version of
Lemma

C.22
.

Lemma C.25
.

Let
{\epsilon}>0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{0}
be an arbitrary finite set of binary strings containing at least
\lceil\log(2/\epsilon)\rceil
elements. Let
F_{1}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
and
F_{2}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
be two arbitrary computable function for which
F_{1}(x)=F_{2}(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, and
F_{1}(F_{2}(x))=F_{2}(F_{1}(x))
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
. There exists a universal monotone Turing machine
U
such that

1.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{0}
has at least
1-\epsilon
probability according to the Solomonoff distribution:

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq 1-\epsilon\,.

2.

For every
m\in\{1,2\}
and every subset
\mathcal{S}_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{*}
that is closed under
F_{m}
, i.e.,

F_{m}(\mathcal{S}_{m})=\{F_{m}(x):x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{m}\,,

and every weight function
w_{m}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
that is non-decreasing under
F_{m}
, i.e.,
w_{m}(F_{m}(x))\geq w_{m}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, we have

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\cap F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

In particular,

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\cap F^{-1}_{m}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}2^{-K_{U}(x)}\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

Proof.

Let
\ell=\lceil\log(2/\epsilon)\rceil
, and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}
be the elements of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, let us fix some arbitrary monotone Turing machine
T_{x_{i}}
which writes
x_{i}
on its output tape (without reading any bit from its input tape) and then halts. Furthermore, define the function
F_{0}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
as

F_{0}(x)=F_{1}(F_{2}(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}F_{2}(F_{1}(x))\,,\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the assumption in the lemma.

For every monotone Turing machine
T
, and every
m^{\prime}\in\{0,1,2\}
define the monotone Turing machine
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
which simulates
T
until the simulated machine
T
halts:

•

Prior to the halting of the simulated machine
T
, the real machine
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
does not write any bit on its output tape. We only store the value of the (simulated) output tape of the simulated
T
on the working tape of
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
.

•

When the simulated
T
attempts to read a bit from its input tape, we read the bit from the actual input tape of
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
.

•

If the simulated
T
does not halt, then
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
loops forever without writing any bit on its output tape (even if the simulated
T
writes some bits on its simulated output tape).

•

If the simulated
T
halts, the machine
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
computes
{F_{m^{\prime}}}(x)
, where
x
is the output of the simulated
T
, and then writes
{F_{m^{\prime}}}(x)
on the output tape.

Clearly,
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)(p)={F_{m^{\prime}}}(T(p))
for every
p\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
. Furthermore, we can computably get a description
\langle S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)\rangle
of
S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T)
from a description
\langle T\rangle
of
T
.

Now let
T_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,T_{n}^{\prime},\ldots
be a computable enumeration of all monotone Turing machines, i.e., the mapping
n\mapsto\langle T_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
is computable.

We define another enumeration
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},\ldots
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{n}=\begin{cases}T_{x_{n}}\quad&\text{if }1\leq n\leq\ell\,,\\
T^{\prime}_{\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}}\quad&\text{if }n>\ell\text{ and }3\ell\text{ divides }n-\ell\,,\\
S_{F_{\left\lfloor\frac{n-\ell}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3}}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}\rceil})\quad&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Clearly, the mapping
n\mapsto\langle T_{n}\rangle
is also computable. The following figure illustrates how the machines
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are related to
(T_{x_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
and
(T_{n}^{\prime})_{n\geq 1}
:

T_{x_{1}}
\vdots
T_{x_{\ell}}
\ell
machines (for
\mathcal{S}_{0}
)
=T_{1}
=T_{\ell}
S_{F_{0}}(T^{\prime}_{1})
\ell-1
machines:
T_{\ell+1},\ldots,T_{2\ell-1}
S_{F_{1}}(T^{\prime}_{1})
\ell
machines:
T_{2\ell},\ldots,T_{3\ell-1}

S_{F_{2}}(T^{\prime}_{1})
\ell
machines:
T_{3\ell},\ldots,T_{4\ell-1}
T^{\prime}_{1}
1 machine:
T_{4\ell}
Block for
T^{\prime}_{1}
S_{F_{0}}(T^{\prime}_{2})
\ell-1
machines:
T_{4\ell+1},\ldots,T_{5\ell-1}
S_{F_{1}}(T^{\prime}_{2})
\ell
machines:
T_{5\ell},\ldots,T_{6\ell-1}
S_{F_{2}}(T^{\prime}_{2})
\ell
machines:
T_{6\ell},\ldots,T_{7\ell-1}
T^{\prime}_{2}
1 machine:
T_{7\ell}
Block for
T^{\prime}_{2}
\vdots

Let
c:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
be the complete prefix-free encoding of positive natural numbers where
c(n)
is the bitstring having
n-1
ones followed by a zero.

We can then define the machine
U
so that
U(c(n)p)=T_{n}(p)
as follows:

•

The machine
U
starts reading the input tape bit by bit until it reads the first zero.

•

Let
n
be the number of bits that were read. The machine then computes the representation
\langle T_{n}\rangle
of the
n
-th machine
T_{n}
in the enumeration and then start simulating
T_{n}
.

This machine
U
is a universal monotone Turing machine by construction (as it can simulate any machine in the enumeration
(T_{n})_{n\geq 1}
which covers all monotone Turing machine).

For every
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we have
U(c(i))=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=T_{x_{i}}(\varepsilon)=x_{i}
, and hence
K_{U}(x_{i})\leq l(c(i))=i
. Therefore,

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(x)}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-K_{U}(x_{i})}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{-i}=1-2^{-\ell}\geq 1-\epsilon\,.

Now fix
m\in\{1,2\}
and let
\mathcal{S}_{m}
be some arbitrary set which is closed under
F_{m}
, i.e.,
F_{m}(\mathcal{S}_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{m}
. Let
x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
. Since
F_{m}(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime}
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we must have
x\notin\mathcal{S}_{0}
and hence for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we must have
x\neq x_{i}=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=U(c(i))
. Let
p_{x}
be an arbitrary bit-string for which
U(p_{x})=x
. From the structure of
U
, we know that there must exist
n>0
such that
p_{x}=c(n)p^{\prime}_{x}
for some
p^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
. Hence,
x=U(p_{x})=U(c(n)p^{\prime}_{x})=T_{n}(p^{\prime}_{x})
. Since
x\neq x_{i}=T_{i}(\varepsilon)=U(c(i))
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we must have
n>\ell
.

We consider two cases for the index
n
:

1.

3\ell
does not divide
n-\ell
. In this case,
T_{n}=S_{F_{m^{\prime}}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
where

n^{\prime}=\lceil\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}\rceil

and
m^{\prime}=\lfloor(n-\ell)/\ell\rfloor\bmod 3\in\{0,1,2\}
.
This implies
x=T_{n}(p^{\prime}_{x})=F_{m^{\prime}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(p^{\prime}_{x}))=F_{m^{\prime}}(y)
for
y:=T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(p^{\prime}_{x})
.

•

If
m^{\prime}=m
, then
x=F_{m}(y)
, which contradicts
x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

•

If
m^{\prime}=0
, then since
F_{0}=F_{m}\circ F_{3-m}
, we have
x=F_{m}(F_{3-m}(y))
, which also contradicts
x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

•

Thus,
m^{\prime}
must be equal to
3-m
, and hence

F_{m}(x)=F_{m}(F_{m^{\prime}}(y))=F_{m}(F_{3-m}(y))=F_{0}(y)=F_{0}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(p^{\prime}_{x}))=S_{F_{0}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})(p^{\prime}_{x})\,.

Now notice that for
\tilde{n}=(3n^{\prime}-2)\ell+1
, which is greater than
\ell
and not divisible by
\ell
, we have

\left\lceil\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{3\ell}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{(3n^{\prime}-2)\ell+1-\ell}{3\ell}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{3(n^{\prime}-1)\ell+1}{3\ell}\right\rceil=n^{\prime}\,,

and

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3=\left\lfloor\frac{(3n^{\prime}-3)\ell+1}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3=3(n^{\prime}-3)\bmod 3=0\,,

and hence if we let
\tilde{p}_{x}=c(\tilde{n})p^{\prime}_{x}
, we get

\displaystyle U(\tilde{p}_{x})=U(c(\tilde{n})p^{\prime}_{x})=T_{\tilde{n}}(p^{\prime}_{x})=S_{F_{\left\lfloor\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3}}(T^{\prime}_{\lceil\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{3\ell}\rceil})=S_{F_{0}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})(p^{\prime}_{x})=F_{m}(x)\,.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle l(\tilde{p}_{x})-l(p_{x})

\displaystyle=l(c(\tilde{n})p^{\prime}_{x})-l(c(n)p_{x}^{\prime})=\tilde{n}-n=(3n^{\prime}-2)\ell+1-n

(56)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}(3n^{\prime}-2)\ell+1-(3n^{\prime}-1)\ell\leq-\ell+1\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the following fact:

–

As
n^{\prime}=\lceil\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}\rceil
, we have

n^{\prime}-1<\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}\leq n^{\prime}
and hence

3n^{\prime}-3<\frac{n-\ell}{\ell}\leq 3n^{\prime}\,.

Combining this with
m^{\prime}=\lfloor(n-\ell)/\ell\rfloor\bmod 3=3-m>0
, we get that we must have
(n-\ell)/\ell\geq(3n^{\prime}-3)+1
and hence
n\geq(3n^{\prime}-1)\ell
.

2.

3\ell
divides
n-\ell
. In this case,
T_{n}
is equal to the “raw” machine
T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
with
n^{\prime}=\frac{n-\ell}{3\ell}
.
Notice that for

\tilde{n}=(3n^{\prime}-2+m)\ell\,,

we have

\displaystyle\left\lceil\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{3\ell}\right\rceil

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{(3n^{\prime}-2+m)\ell-\ell}{3\ell}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{(3n^{\prime}-3+m)\ell}{3\ell}\right\rceil

\displaystyle=\left\lceil n^{\prime}-1+\frac{m}{3}\right\rceil=n^{\prime}\,,

where the last equality is true because
m\in\{1,2\}
and hence
0<\frac{m}{3}<1
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{\tilde{n}-\ell}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3

\displaystyle=\left\lfloor\frac{(3n^{\prime}-3+m)\ell}{\ell}\right\rfloor\bmod 3

\displaystyle=(3n^{\prime}-3+m)\bmod 3=m\bmod 3=m\,.

Thus
T_{\tilde{n}}=S_{F_{m}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
.
Defining
\tilde{p}_{x}=c(\tilde{n})p^{\prime}_{x}
, we get

U(\tilde{p}_{x})=U(c(\tilde{n})p^{\prime}_{x})=T_{\tilde{n}}(p^{\prime}_{x})=S_{F_{m}}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})(p^{\prime}_{x})=F_{m}(T^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(p^{\prime}_{x}))=F_{m}(x)\,.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle l(\tilde{p}_{x})-l(p_{x})

\displaystyle=\tilde{n}-n=(3n^{\prime}-2+m)\ell-(\ell+3n^{\prime}\ell)

(57)

\displaystyle=(m-3)\ell\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}-\ell\leq-\ell+1\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the fact that
m\in\{1,2\}
and hence
m\geq 1
.

We conclude that for every
p_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
for which
U(p_{x})=x
, there exists
\tilde{p}_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
satisfying
U(\tilde{p}_{x})=F_{m}(x)
and
l(\tilde{p}_{x})\leq l(p_{x})-\ell+1
. Therefore,

K_{U}(F_{m}(x))\leq K_{U}(x)-\ell+1\,,

and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(F_{m}(x))-\ell+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(F_{m}(x))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(F_{m}(x))2^{-K_{U}(F_{m}(x))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}\epsilon\sum_{y\in F_{m}(\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*}))}w_{m}(y)2^{-K_{U}(y)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\diamond)}}{{\leq}}\epsilon\sum_{y\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}w_{m}(y)2^{-K_{U}(y)}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from our choice of
\ell
,
(\dagger)
from the fact that
w_{m}
is non-decreasing under
F_{m}
,
(\ddagger)
follows because
F_{m}
is injective, and
(\diamond)
follows because
\mathcal{S}_{m}
is closed under
F_{m}
and hence
F_{m}(\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*}))\subseteq F_{m}(\mathcal{S}_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{m}
.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\cap F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}\setminus F_{m}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}^{*})}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}w_{m}(x)2^{-K_{U}(x)}.

∎

The following lemma is a version of
Lemma

C.23
for the Solomonoff mixture
\rho_{U,\tau}
in the (coupled) embedded case. We write
\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}
and
\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}
to denote the “policy-part" and the “environment-part" of the mixture universe
\rho_{U,\tau}
, respectively, i.e.,

\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=\rho_{U,\tau}(a|\text{\ae}_{*})\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e|\text{\ae}_{*},a)=\rho_{U,\tau}(e|\text{\ae}_{*},a)\,,\quad\forall(\text{\ae}_{*},a,e)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E}\,.

Clearly,
\rho_{U,\tau}=\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}^{\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}}
.

Lemma C.26
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a monotone Turing machine
U
such that the environment part
\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}
of
\rho_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, and the policy part
\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}
of
\rho_{U,\tau}

\epsilon
-approximates
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}\cup\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.

Proof.

Let
\ell=\lceil\log(2/\epsilon)\rceil
, and let
M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}
be
\ell
APOMs which are syntactically different, but which all implement the same universe
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
, i.e.,
\lambda_{M_{1}}^{\tau}=\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
. For example, we can pick any machine
M_{1}
for which
\lambda_{M_{1}}^{\tau}=\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
and then for every
1<i\leq\ell
, the machine
M_{i}
starts with executing a loop of
i
iterations in which it does some useless computation and afterwards it starts simulating
M_{1}
. Let

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\left\{\langle M_{i}\rangle:1\leq i\leq\ell\right\}\,.

Now for every APOM
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
, we define an APOM
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)
such that the “environment part" of the universe
\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}
is equivalent to (the deterministic environment)
\mu_{R,k}
, but at the same time it has the same distribution as
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}
on histories
\text{\ae}_{*}a\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}
that end with an action and which are compatible with
\mu_{R,k}
. More precisely, we would like to have the following guarantees:

\displaystyle\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\,,\quad

\displaystyle\text{if }\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})>0\,,

and

\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,\quad&\text{if }\mu_{R,k}(e_{t^{\prime}}|{\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}},a_{t^{\prime}})=1\text{ for all }1\leq t^{\prime}<t\,,\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(58)

Since
\mu_{R,k}
is deterministic, we can ensure the above by designing the “policy part" of
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
so that it satisfies

\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\quad&\text{if }t=1\,,\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(e_{t-1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t-1}}a_{t-1})\quad&\text{if }t>1\text{ and }\mu_{R,k}(e_{t-1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t-1}},a_{t-1})=1\,,\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

(59)

In order to properly implement this using an APOM, we need to be able to run a simulation of
M
and we would need the ability to restart the simulation of
M
from a previous state. Let
\mathcal{S}_{M}
be state space describing the internal state of the APOM
M
, i.e., an element
s
of
\mathcal{S}_{M}
has a complete description of the current content of the working tapes, the output tape, and the oracle tape.
110
110

110

Recall that when we run
M
to produce a universe, the input tape is ignored, so we do not need a representation of the input tape.
One computational step of
M
can be simulated as
s\leftarrow\mathrm{Simulate}(\langle M\rangle,s)
where
\mathrm{Simulate}
is a function that applies the simulation. With these conventions, we can implement
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)
as follows:

t\leftarrow 1

\text{\ae}_{*}\leftarrow\varepsilon

//
We will keep
t=l(\text{\ae}_{*})-1
so that we generally have
\text{\ae}_{*}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}
.

//
Initialize the internal state
s
to be used in a simulation of
M

s\leftarrow\mathrm{InitializeState}()

while

True

do

//
Continue the simulation of
M
until it produces an action. This part contributes the term
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
in the expression of
\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
(which would only be a factor when
t>1
) (cf. equation

).

while

True

do

s\leftarrow\mathrm{Simulate}(\langle M\rangle,s)

if

\mathrm{IsHalt}(s)

then

Halt the real machine
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

if

\mathrm{OutputTape}(s)=\langle\text{\ae}_{*}a\rangle
for some
a\in\mathcal{A}

then

Extract
a
from
\mathrm{OutputTape}(s)

a_{t}\leftarrow a

Break

Append
\langle a_{t}\rangle
to the real output tape of
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

//
Compute a percept
e_{t}
according to
\mu_{R,k}
. This ensures that
\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})=\mu_{R,k}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})
.

e_{t}\leftarrow\langle o_{0},r_{t}(a_{1:t})\rangle

Append
\langle e_{t}\rangle
to the real output tape of
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

//
Evolve the simulation of
M
until it produces a percept
e\in\mathcal{E}
. If the simulation halts before producing a percept or if it produces a percept
e\neq e_{t}
, we halt the real machine. This part contributes a multiplicative factor
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
in the expression of
\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}(a_{t+1}|\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})
(cf. equation
59
).

while

True

do

s\leftarrow\mathrm{Simulate}(\langle M\rangle,s)

if

\mathrm{IsHalt}(s)

then

Halt the real machine
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

if

\mathrm{OutputTape}(s)=\langle{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}e\rangle
for some
e\in\mathcal{E}

then

Break

if

e=e_{t}

then

Break
;

else

Halt the real machine
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

\text{\ae}_{*}\leftarrow\text{\ae}_{*}a_{t}e_{t}

t\leftarrow t+1

Algorithm 6

The "reverting" APOM
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)

We assume that the “boiler-plate" structure of
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)
is done in a way that ensures that
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)\neq M_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell
, e.g., we can just add some useless loop that does not exist in any of the machines
(M_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
.

Now we also design a machine
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)
which would follow the simulation of
M
until it produces
k
consecutive ‘down’ actions, after which it continues the simulation according to
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\mathrm{up}}}
.

t\leftarrow 1

\text{\ae}_{*}\leftarrow\varepsilon

//
We will keep
t=l(\text{\ae}_{*})-1
so that we generally have
\text{\ae}_{*}={\text{\ae}_{<t}}
.

\mathrm{streak}\text{\_}\mathrm{found}\leftarrow\mathrm{false}

//
Initialize the internal state
s
to be used in a simulation of
M

s\leftarrow\mathrm{InitializeState}()

while

True

do

if

\mathrm{streak}\text{\_}\mathrm{found}

then

//
If
k
down actions have been found, produce an ‘up’ action and a percept that is consistent with
\mu_{R,k}
.

a_{t}\leftarrow\mathrm{up}

e_{t}\leftarrow\langle o_{0},r_{t}(a_{1:t})\rangle

\text{\ae}_{t}\leftarrow a_{t}e_{t}

else

//
Continue the simulation of
M
until it produces an action-percept pair
\text{\ae}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}
.

while

True

do

s\leftarrow\mathrm{Simulate}(\langle M\rangle,s)

if

\mathrm{IsHalt}(s)

then

Halt the real machine
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)

if

\mathrm{OutputTape}(s)=\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\text{\ae}\rangle
for some
\text{\ae}\in\mathcal{A}

then

Extract
æ
from
\mathrm{OutputTape}(s)

\text{\ae}_{t}\leftarrow\text{\ae}

Break

Append
\langle\text{\ae}_{t}\rangle
to the real output tape of
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)

if

a_{t-k+1:t}=(\mathrm{down})^{k}

then

\mathrm{streak}\text{\_}\mathrm{found}\leftarrow\mathrm{true}

\text{\ae}_{*}\leftarrow\text{\ae}_{*}\text{\ae}_{t}

t\leftarrow t+1

Algorithm 7

The "reverting" APOM
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)

We further assume that the “boiler-plate" structure of
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)
ensures it is distinct from the machines in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and from the outputs of
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
. Note that by construction,
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
enforces the environment conditions of
\mu_{R,k}
, while
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
enforces the policy conditions of
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}
(as well as the environment conditions of
\mu_{R,k}
) after a streak of
k
downs.

Now how does
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M))
compare to
S_{\mu_{R,k}}(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M))
? Syntactically they are different, but they induce the same distribution, i.e.,
\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M))}^{\tau}=\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M))}^{\tau}
.

Let

\mathcal{S}:=\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{0}\}=\left\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\}\,,

where

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}\}\,,

We would like to define two functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
based on
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
and
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
(similarly to the definition of
F
in equation
50
in the proof of
Lemma

C.23
) so that we can apply
Lemma

C.25
. However, due to the constraint that we need
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
to commute, we cannot use
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
and
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
directly because they do not commute. Because of this, we will define
S_{1}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
to exactly match
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
, i.e.,

S_{1}(M)=S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)\,,\quad\forall M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\,,

but we will define
S_{2}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
in a way that does not exactly match
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
, but nevertheless is “distributionally equivalent to it”.

For this purpose, let us define for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
the number
n_{S_{1}}(M)
as the maximal
n\geq 0
for which
S_{1}^{n}(M^{\prime})=M
for some
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
, where
S_{1}^{n}(M^{\prime})
denotes the machine obtained by applying the wrapper
S_{\mu_{R,k}}

n
times on
M^{\prime}
, and we adopt the convention that
S_{1}^{0}(M)=M
.
111
111

111

Hence, if
n_{S_{1}}(M)=0
then
M\neq S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M^{\prime})
for all
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
.
Such a maximal
n
is guaranteed to exist because the wrapper always adds extra overhead (of at least one bit) in the representation of the machine, and hence
n_{S_{1}}(M)\leq l(\langle M\rangle)
. Now for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
, let
S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)
be the machine
M^{\prime}
for which
M=S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M^{\prime})
. We can now define
S_{2}:\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
as follows

\displaystyle S_{2}(M)=S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)}\left(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)\right)\right)\,.

A few comments are in order:

•

n_{S_{1}}(S_{1}(M))=n_{S_{1}}(M)+1
and
S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(S_{1}(M))}(S_{1}(M))=S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle S_{2}(S_{1}(M))

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(S_{1}(M))}\left(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(S_{1}(M))}(S_{1}(M))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)+1}\left(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{1}\left(S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)}\left(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{1}(S_{2}(M))\,,

which means that
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
commute.

•

Now since
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
and
S_{\mu_{R,k}}
“distributionally commute” in the sense that

\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M))}^{\tau}=\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M))}^{\tau}\,,\quad\forall M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\,,

we can see that

\displaystyle\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)}\left(S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)\right)\right)}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}\left(S_{1}^{n_{S_{1}}(M)}\left(S_{1}^{-n_{S_{1}}(M)}(M)\right)\right)}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}\,,

and hence
S_{2}(M)
and
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)
are “distributionally equivalent”. This will be sufficient for our purposes.

Now define two functions
F_{1},F_{2}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
as follows

\displaystyle F_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle S_{1}(M)\rangle\quad&\text{if }x=\langle M\rangle\text{ for some }M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\,,\\
x\quad&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle F_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle S_{2}(M)\rangle\quad&\text{if }x=\langle M\rangle\text{ for some }M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\,,\\
x\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Both
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are computable and injective, and map
\mathcal{S}_{0}
to itself (as the identity). Since
S_{2}
and
S_{1}
commute,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
commute as well, i.e.,
F_{1}(F_{2}(x))=F_{2}(F_{1}(x))
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

Let
U
be the universal monotone Turing machine from
Lemma

C.25
applied to
\mathcal{S}_{0}
,
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, and
\epsilon
. This ensures two key properties:

1.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{0}
has high probability:

\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\geq 1-\epsilon.

(60)

2.

For

\mathcal{S}:=\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{0}\}=\left\{\langle M\rangle:M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}\right\}\,,

with

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}\setminus\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{\ell}\}\,,

we get that
\mathcal{S}
is closed under
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. By following a similar reasoning to how we proved equation
52
, we can show that for any
m\in\{1,2\}
and any non-decreasing weight function
w:\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\to[0,1]
under
S_{m}
, i.e., it satisfies
w(S_{m}(M))\geq w(M)
for every
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we have

\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}w(M)2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{m}(M)\rangle)}\geq\left(1-\epsilon\right)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}w(M)2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,,\quad\text{for }m\in\{1,2\}\,.

(61)

We now prove the two approximation claims.

We start by showing the environment approximation, i.e., that
|\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)-\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)|\leq\epsilon
for any
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}\times\mathcal{A}
.
The environment part of the mixture univere
\rho_{U,\tau}
is given by:

\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=\frac{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)}{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)}=\frac{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)}{\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)}\,.

Since
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, any machine
M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
perfectly matches
\mu_{R,k}
, meaning

\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)=\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,.

For machines
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, consider the mapping
S_{1}
. By construction of Algorithm
6
, for any
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
, the machine
S_{1}(M)=S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)
enforces the environment
\mu_{R,k}
as long as
\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})>0
. Thus, for any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we have

\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)=\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae)=\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,.

Let
e^{*}=(o_{0},r_{t}(a_{<t}a))
so that
\mu_{R,k}(e^{*}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=1
. We have:

\displaystyle\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})+\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})

(62)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})+\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{1}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)+\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{1}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,,

By defining
w(M)=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)
, it follows from equation
58
and the fact that
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}_{\mu_{R,k}}^{*}
that

w(S_{1}(M))=\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=\lambda_{S_{\mu_{R,k}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=w(M)\,,

and hence
w
is non-decreasing under
S_{1}
. It now follows from equation
61
that

\displaystyle\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{1}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{1}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{1}(M)\rangle)}w(S_{1}(M))

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}w(M)

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,.

Combining this with equation
62
, we get

\displaystyle\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)+(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,.

Thus

\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e^{*}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=\frac{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}ae^{*})}{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)}\geq 1-\epsilon=\mu_{R,k}(e^{*}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)-\epsilon\,.

For
e\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\{e^{*}\}
, we have
\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)=0
and
\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\leq 1-\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e^{*}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\leq\epsilon
, hence

\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\leq\epsilon+\mu_{R,k}(e|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a)\,.

Therefore,
\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}

\epsilon
-approximates
\mu_{R,k}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
.

Now we turn to proving the policy approximation, i.e., that
|\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(a|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})-\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(a|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})|\leq\epsilon
for every
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}\cup\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
and every
a\in\mathcal{A}
. We split this into two cases based on the history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
.

Case 1:

{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
(Histories containing only ‘up’ actions, and also compatible with
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
).
In this case,
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(\text{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=1
.
Since
\mathcal{S}_{0}
contains machines implementing
\mu_{R,k}^{\pi_{\text{up}}}
, and since
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
, we get that
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\geq 1-\epsilon\,,

where the last inequality follows from equation
60
. Therefore,

\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(\text{up}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\rho_{U,\tau}(\text{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\frac{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})}{\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\geq\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})\geq 1-\epsilon\,.

Case 2:

{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
(Histories containing a streak of
k
downs, and also compatible with
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}_{\mu_{R,k}}
).
Since the history contains
(\text{down})^{k}
, and since machines in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
implement
\pi_{\text{up}}
, we have
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=0
for
M\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
. The probability mass is entirely supported by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Consider the transformation
S_{2}(M)
and recall that the distributions of
\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}
and
\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}
are equal. By definition (Algorithm
7
), if the history contains
k
downs,
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)
forces the action ‘up’.
Thus, for any
M^{\prime}
,
\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M^{\prime})}^{\tau}(\text{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=1
.

Now define
w(M)=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})
and let
t^{\prime}<t
be such
a_{t^{\prime}-k+1:t}=(\mathrm{down})^{k}
. We have

\displaystyle w(S_{2}(M))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t^{\prime}}})\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}(\text{\ae}_{t^{\prime}+1:t-1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(\text{\ae}_{1:{t^{\prime}}})\geq\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the fact that the wrapper
S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}
mimics
M
until the streak is complete after which it follows
\mu_{R,k}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}

(cf. Algorithm
7
) which means that
\lambda_{S_{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}\mathrm{after}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}}(M)}^{\tau}(\text{\ae}_{t^{\prime}+1:t-1})=1
because
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*\text{-}\mathrm{up}\text{-}k\text{-}\mathrm{down}\text{-}*\text{-}\mathrm{up}}_{\mu_{R,k}}
. We conclude that
w(M)=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})
is non-decreasing under
S_{2}
, and hence it follows from equation
61
that

\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{2}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\\
\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{2}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}}\text{up})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle S_{2}(M)\rangle)}\lambda_{S_{2}(M)}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\cdot 1\quad\quad\text{(since }F_{2}\text{ forces up)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{S}}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\lambda_{M}^{\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})=(1-\epsilon)\rho_{U,\tau}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

Thus,
\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}(\text{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\geq 1-\epsilon
. Since
\pi_{\mathrm{up}}(\text{up}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=1
, the approximation holds.
∎

Putting everything together, we get the following theorem:

Theorem C.27
.

Consider a
k
-E-AIXI
τ
agent, i.e., an embedded agent employing
k
-step planning for a fixed integer
k\geq 1
, with a mixture universe model
\rho=\rho_{U,\tau}
constructed from a Solomonoff prior over the
\tau
-POM-computable universes. Then, there exists a computable ground-truth environment
\mu
and a reference universal monotone Turing machine
U
for defining the Kolmogorov complexity
K_{U}
in the Solomonoff prior, such that the agent interacting with
\mu
does not converge to the infinite-horizon optimal planner w.r.t.
\rho=\rho_{U,\tau}
, despite the fact that this setup satisfies the grain-of-truth property (in case, e.g., of a reflective oracle). In particular, Self-AIXI does not always converge to AIXI.

Proof.

Fix
R
so that
\gamma^{k+1}<R<\gamma^{k}
. Let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small as indicated in
Corollary

C.20
and then construct
U
as in

Lemma

C.26
. Since
\rho_{U,\tau}=\nu_{\rho_{U,\tau}}^{\pi_{\rho_{U,\tau}}}
, the result follows.
∎

Appendix D
Embedded Bayesian agents and related solution concepts: additional information

D.1
Recursive belief updates of mixture universes

The embedded agents we consider use as their predictive model a Bayesian mixture universe
\rho
: Given a countable class of universes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
, we start with a prior belief distribution
w(\lambda):=w(\lambda\mid\varepsilon)
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
, which induces a mixture
\rho
as follows

\rho:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda\,.

Now if we observe a history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, then Bayes’ rule implies that the posterior beliefs about the universe
\lambda
should be updated to

w(\lambda|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}):=\frac{w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda^{\prime})\lambda^{\prime}({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}=\frac{w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\,.

On the other hand, if we observe a history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}
, then Bayes’ rule implies that the posterior beliefs about the universe
\lambda
should be updated to

w(\lambda|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t}):=\frac{w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}=\frac{w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\lambda(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\rho(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}=w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\frac{\lambda(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\rho(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\,.

Similarly, one can show that

w(\lambda\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\frac{\lambda(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}.

Now according to the mixture
\rho
, the probability of observing
a_{t}
after having observed
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
is given by

\displaystyle\rho(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}=\frac{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}\frac{w(\lambda)\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\lambda(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\rho({\text{\ae}_{<t}})}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\lambda(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

Similarly, one can show that

\displaystyle\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\lambda(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,.

We can summarize the above discussion with the following recursive equations, which could be taken as recursive definitions of the involved quantities:

\begin{split}\rho(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\lambda(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,\quad\quad w(\lambda\mid\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\frac{\lambda(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}{\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})}\,,\\
\rho(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\lambda(e_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,,\quad\quad w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=w(\lambda\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\frac{\lambda(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}{\rho(a_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}_{<t}})}\,.\end{split}

(63)

D.2
Additional information on the differences between the decoupled and embedded formalisms

Remark D.1

(On the differences between the embedded and decoupled formalisms)
.

The reader might be wondering whether the formalism of embedded Bayesian agents is really different from the formalism of decoupled Bayesian agents: Assuming that we have a class of universes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
with a prior
w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)
over universes
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
, which induces a mixture universe
\rho=\sum_{\lambda}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\lambda
, is it possible to define a class of environments
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
with a prior
w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu)
over environments
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
inducing a mixture environment
\xi=\sum_{\nu}w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu)\nu
in such a way that
\xi(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
(which would mean that the decoupled best response policy w.r.t.
\xi
is the same as the embedded best response w.r.t.
\rho
)?

A seemingly reasonable approach that one might try is to define for every universe
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
its induced environment
\nu_{\lambda}
given by
112
112

112

We assume for the sake of this discussion that
\lambda({\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})>0
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
so that
\lambda(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
is well defined.

\nu_{\lambda}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\lambda(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
, and then define
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}=\{\nu_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}\}
with the prior
113
113

113

Note that this prior is effectively the probability distribution of
\nu_{\lambda}
when
\lambda
is chosen according to the prior
w_{\mathrm{uni}}
.

w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}:\\
\nu_{\lambda}=\nu\end{subarray}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\,,

i.e., for every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
we add up the probabilities (according to the
w_{\mathrm{uni}}
prior) of all universes whose induced environments are all equal to
\nu
.
114
114

114

It is possible for two different universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
to have the same induced environment, but this would of course require that they have a different induced policy
\lambda(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
.
The resulting mixture environment is

\xi=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu)\nu=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}:\\
\nu_{\lambda}=\nu\end{subarray}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\nu=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\nu_{\lambda}\,.

However, the mixture environment
\xi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\nu_{\lambda}
in this approach would not necessarily match the environment
\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
induced by the mixture universe
\rho
: Consider the case where
\mathcal{A}=\{a_{0},a_{1}\}
and
\mathcal{E}=\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
, and assume that we have two universes
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
satisfying
115
115

115

For the purposes of the example, we only care about the marginal distribution of
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
over the first turn.

\begin{aligned} \lambda_{0}(a_{0}e_{0})&=(1-\epsilon)^{2}\,,\\
\lambda_{0}(a_{0}e_{1})&=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)\,,\\
\lambda_{0}(a_{1}e_{0})&=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)\,,\\
\lambda_{0}(a_{1}e_{1})&=\epsilon^{2}\,,\\
\end{aligned}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\begin{aligned} \lambda_{1}(a_{0}e_{0})&=\epsilon^{2}\,,\\
\lambda_{1}(a_{0}e_{1})&=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)\,,\\
\lambda_{1}(a_{1}e_{0})&=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)\,,\\
\lambda_{1}(a_{1}e_{1})&=(1-\epsilon)^{2}\,,\\
\end{aligned}

where
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
is some fixed small number. Define
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}=\{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\}
and assume the uniform prior
w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda_{0})=w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda_{1})=\frac{1}{2}
. A simple calculation reveals that:

•

\rho(a_{0}e_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}
and
\rho(a_{0}e_{1})=\epsilon(1-\epsilon)
, and hence
\rho(e_{0}|a_{0})=\frac{\rho(a_{0}e_{0})}{\rho(a_{0}e_{0})+\rho(a_{0}e_{1})}=1-2\epsilon(1-\epsilon)
.

•

\nu_{\lambda_{0}}(e_{0}|a_{0})=\lambda_{0}(e_{0}|a_{0})=\frac{\lambda_{0}(a_{0}e_{0})}{\lambda_{0}(a_{0}e_{0})+\lambda_{0}(a_{0}e_{1})}=1-\epsilon
, and similarly
\nu_{\lambda_{1}}(e_{0}|a_{0})=\lambda_{1}(e_{0}|a_{0})=\epsilon
. This implies that

\xi(e_{0}|a_{0})=w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda_{0})\nu_{\lambda_{0}}(e_{0}|a_{0})+w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda_{1})\nu_{\lambda_{1}}(e_{0}|a_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\,,

which is different from
\rho(e_{0}|a_{0})=1-2\epsilon(1-\epsilon)
because
\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.

Therefore, the mixture environment
\xi=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\nu_{\lambda}
is different from the environment induced by the mixture universe
\rho=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)\lambda
.

The above discussion does not preclude the possibility of defining some other class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
with an associated prior
w_{\mathrm{env}}
inducing a mixture environment
\xi
matching the environment induced by
\rho
: A trivial way to achieve this is to just define a single environment
\nu_{\rho}
as
\nu_{\rho}(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
, and then let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}=\{\nu_{\rho}\}
with the prior
w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu_{\rho})=1
, which would make
\xi=\nu_{\rho}
and hence
\xi(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
, as desired.

As far as the Bayes-optimal policy is concerned, the embedded setting with respect to
w_{\mathrm{uni}}
is mathematically equivalent to the decoupled setting with respect to the prior
w_{\mathrm{env}}
satisfying
w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu_{\rho})=1
, because
\xi(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})=\rho(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
. Nevertheless, the two settings differ in the Bayesian belief structure (assuming
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
contains at least two different universes): The decoupled Bayesian agent optimizing w.r.t.
\xi
defined above really has a single environment
\nu_{\rho}
in its hypothesis class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
, with
w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu_{\rho})=1
, and hence it is
certain about the environment it is interacting with
, while the embedded Bayesian agent optimizing w.r.t. (the environment induced by)
\rho
is
uncertain about the universe it is living in
since there is no single
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
satisfying
w_{\mathrm{uni}}(\lambda)=1
. If we are only interested in Bayes-optimal agents, then this difference is mainly philosophical, because the Bayes-optimal policy is the same in both cases. However, if one considers variants of the Bayes-optimal agents which also do some stronger form of exploration
116
116

116

E.g., something similar to the Thompson sampling variant of AIXI
(
62
)
, or the strongly optimal agent Inq introduced in
16
.
to reduce the uncertainty about the ground-truth universe (resp. ground-truth environment) before applying the best response policy, then the difference between
w_{\mathrm{uni}}
and
w_{\mathrm{env}}
becomes important because the exploration strategy would necessarily require the use of the underlying Bayesian beliefs in order to reduce the uncertainty over the universe (resp. environment) in a principled manner. If the prior over environments satisfies
w_{\mathrm{env}}(\nu_{\rho})=1
, then there is no need to do any exploration for a "decoupled exploring agent" because there is no uncertainty on the environment. On the other hand, an "embedded exploring agent" would need to explore to reduce its uncertainty about the universe it is living in. We do not consider embedded exploring agents in this paper, and leave the investigation of such agents for future research.

In
Sections

3.6
and
4
, we analyze the differences between the coupled and decoupled settings in further depth.

D.3
Spohn’s dependency equilibria and its relation to embedded equilibria

In
Section

4.2
, we introduced the subjective embedded equilibrium (SEE) and its objective counterpart, the embedded equilibrium (EE), which were inspired by the
dependency equilibrium
(DE) of
102
. The goal of this section is to formalize this relationship. We first define Spohn’s original dependency equilibrium and then investigate its precise connection to our framework. As Spohn’s dependency equilibria were originally defined for normal-form (single-shot) games, we restrict our analysis in this section to that setting. We will show that the dependency equilibrium is a special case of a
correlated
version of our embedded equilibrium.

Definition D.2

(Dependency Equilibrium - DE)
.

For a normal-form game, a dependency distribution
p(\bar{a})
with a corresponding conditional completion constitutes a
dependency equilibrium
if: For each agent
i
, any action
a^{i}
with marginal probability
p(a^{i})>0
maximizes its expected utility with respect to the completed conditionals
p(a^{-i}|a^{\prime i})
:

a^{i}\in\arg\max_{a^{\prime i}\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}|a^{\prime i})}[r^{i}(a^{\prime i},a^{-i})]\,.

The primary difference between our embedded equilibrium (EE,
Definition

4.19
) and Spohn’s dependency equilibrium (DE) lies in what the best response is computed against. For an EE, the best response is computed w.r.t. the conditional completion of the
ground-truth universe

\upsilon^{i}=(\mu^{i})^{\pi^{i}}
, which is induced by the agents’ actual policies. This grounds the equilibrium in the observable reality of the agents’ interactions. In contrast, a DE computes its best response w.r.t. a given dependency distribution
p(\bar{a})
which does not necessarily have to correspond to the ground-truth universe on the play-path. However, as we will show, when we introduce a correlation device
(
6
)
to define a
correlated embedded equilibrium
(CEE), this concept encapsulates Spohn’s original dependency equilibrium.

Definition D.3

(Correlated Embedded Equilibrium - CEE)
.

Let
(M,p)
be a correlation device, let
(\pi^{i}(a^{i}|m^{i}))_{i\in N}
be a set of policies, and let
q(a^{-i}\mid a^{i},\bar{m})
be conditional completions of a correlated dependency distribution
q(\overline{a}|\overline{m})
. Let the ground-truth distribution over actions given the joint message
\bar{m}
be given by

\upsilon(\bar{a}\mid\bar{m})=\prod_{j\in N}\pi^{j}(a^{j}\mid m^{j})\,.

We say that the tuple
(\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in[N]},(M,p),q)
forms a
correlated embedded equilibrium
(CEE) for a normal-form game if for each agent
i
and each message
m^{i}
with
p(m^{i})>0
:
The agent’s policy
\pi^{i}(\cdot\mid m^{i})
is an embedded best response w.r.t. the conditional completion of
\upsilon
by
q
. That is, the expected reward under the policy
\pi^{i}
is maximal, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{i}(a^{i}\mid m^{i})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{p(m^{-i}\mid m^{i})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{i},\bar{m})}[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})]\right]\right]=\max_{a^{\prime i}\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}\mathbb{E}_{p(m^{-i}\mid m^{i})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})}[r^{i}(a^{\prime i},a^{-i})]\right]\,,

(64)

with

\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})=\begin{cases}\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})\quad&\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ \upsilon(a^{\prime i}\mid\bar{m})=\pi^{i}(a^{\prime i}\mid m^{i})>0\,,\\
q(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})\quad&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where the term
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})
on the left-hand side is the completed conditional, whereas the term
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})
on the right-hand side (for the case
\upsilon(a^{\prime i}|\bar{m})>0
) is the conditional from the original ground-truth measure
\upsilon
. As can be seen, the conditional
q(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})
helps extend the definition of
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})
to the cases where
\upsilon(a^{\prime i}|\bar{m})=0
.

Proposition D.4
.

Each dependency equilibrium can be formulated as a correlated embedded equilibrium in the following sense: For every dependency distribution
p(\bar{a})
with conditional completions
(p(a^{-i}|a^{\prime i}))_{i\in[N]}
which form a dependency equilibrium, there exists a correlated embedded equilibrium
(\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in[N]},(M,p^{\prime}),q)
such that

p(\overline{a})=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(\overline{m})}\left[\prod_{i\in[N]}\pi^{i}(a^{i}|m^{i})\right]\,.

Proof.

Let
p(\bar{a})
be a dependency equilibrium (DE) with completed conditionals
p(a^{-i}|a^{\prime i})
. We will construct an equivalent correlated embedded equilibrium (CEE), denoted
(\{\pi^{i}\},(M,p^{\prime}),q)
.

Let the message space for each agent be its action space,
M^{i}=\mathcal{A}^{i}
. Define the probability distribution
p^{\prime}
over the joint message space
M=\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}^{i}
to be the dependency distribution
p
from the DE. So,
p^{\prime}(\bar{m}):=p(\bar{m})
. The message
\bar{m}
can be interpreted as a "recommended" joint action.

Now we define the agents’ policies to be the ones that obediently follow the recommendation, i.e., for each agent
i
, we define its policy
\pi^{i}
as:

\pi^{i}(a^{i}|m^{i})=\delta(a^{i}=m^{i})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a^{i}=m^{i}\,,\\
0\,,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Notice that for every
\overline{a}=(a^{i})_{i\in[N]}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(\overline{m})}\left[\prod_{i\in[N]}\pi^{i}(a^{i}|m^{i})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(\overline{m})}\left[\prod_{i\in[N]}\delta(a^{i}=m^{i})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(\overline{m})}\left[\delta(\overline{a}=\overline{m})\right]=p^{\prime}(\overline{a})=p(\overline{a})\,.

Now define the correlated dependency distribution
q(\overline{a}|\overline{m})
and its conditional completions
q(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})
using the DE’s dependency distribution
p(\overline{a})
and its conditional completions
p(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i})
as follows:

\displaystyle q(\overline{a}\mid\bar{m}):=p(\overline{a})\,,\quad\text{and}\quad q(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m}):=p(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i})\,.

Let us now show that
(\{\pi^{i}\}_{i\in[N]},(M,p^{\prime}),q)
indeed forms a CEE by checking the conditions of
Definition

D.3
. The ground-truth universe is given by

\upsilon(\bar{a}\mid\bar{m})=\prod_{j}\pi^{j}(a^{j}\mid m^{j})=\delta(\bar{a}=\bar{m})\,,

where
\delta(\cdot)
is the indicator function.

We must show that the obedient policy
\pi^{i}
is a best response for each
i
and
m^{i}
with
p^{\prime}(m^{i})>0
. The CEE best-response condition in equation
64
requires the expected reward from the policy (LHS) to equal the maximum possible expected reward (RHS).

The LHS in equation
64
evaluates the reward for the obedient action
a^{i}=m^{i}
. This action is on the play-path (
\pi^{i}(a^{i}|m^{i})=\pi^{i}(m^{i}|m^{i})=1>0
), so the inner expectation uses the ground-truth conditional
\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{i},\bar{m})
:

\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{i},\bar{m})=\frac{\upsilon(\bar{a}\mid\bar{m})}{\upsilon(a^{i}\mid\bar{m})}=\frac{\delta(\bar{a}=\bar{m})}{\delta(a^{i}=m^{i})}=\delta(a^{-i}=m^{-i})\,,

where in the last equality we used the fact that
\delta(a^{i}=m^{i})=1
, as
a^{i}=m^{i}
.
The expected reward for the obedient action
a^{i}=m^{i}
is:

LHS

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(m^{-i}\mid m^{i})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\delta(a^{-i}=m^{-i})}[r^{i}(m^{i},a^{-i})]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(m^{-i}\mid m^{i})}\left[r^{i}(m^{i},m^{-i})\right]=\sum_{m^{-i}}\frac{p^{\prime}(\bar{m})}{p^{\prime}(m^{i})}r^{i}(m^{i},m^{-i})\,.

Since
p^{\prime}(\bar{m})=p(\bar{m})
, this is exactly
\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}|a^{i})}[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})]
(with
a^{i}=m^{i}
).

The RHS in equation
64
considers an arbitrary action
a^{\prime i}
. If
a^{\prime i}\neq m^{i}
, this action is off the play-path (
\pi^{i}(a^{\prime i}|m^{i})=0
). Therefore, the inner expectation uses the dependency distribution
q
:

\upsilon(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})=q(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i},\bar{m})=p(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i})\,.

The expected reward for deviating to
a^{\prime i}
is:

\mathbb{E}_{p^{\prime}(m^{-i}\mid m^{i})}\left[\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i})}[r^{i}(a^{\prime i},a^{-i})]\right]=\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}\mid a^{\prime i})}[r^{i}(a^{\prime i},a^{-i})]\,.

The DE best-response condition states that for any
a^{i}
with
p(a^{i})>0
(which is equivalent to
p^{\prime}(m^{i})>0
for
m^{i}=a^{i}
),
a^{i}
maximizes the expected utility:

\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}|a^{i})}[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})]\geq\mathbb{E}_{p(a^{-i}|a^{\prime i})}[r^{i}(a^{\prime i},a^{-i})]\quad\forall a^{\prime i}\,.

This is exactly
\text{LHS}\geq\text{RHS}
for any
a^{\prime i}
. Thus, the obedient action
a^{i}=m^{i}
achieves the maximum, satisfying the CEE best-response condition.

Thus, any DE can be formulated as an equivalent CEE.
∎

D.4
Additional information on
Example

4.13

Take
Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}):=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i})}\left[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})\right]
.
We will show that for
\alpha>\frac{m^{*}_{\infty}}{1+m^{*}_{\infty}}
(with
m^{*}_{\infty}\in(0,1]
and will be defined later), we have that there exists some time
T
such that for every
t>T
and every trajectory
\bar{a}_{<t}
satisfying
a^{i}_{t^{\prime}}=a^{-i}_{t^{\prime}}
for each
t^{\prime}<t
, we have
Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=C)>Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=D)
. This means that there exists some time
T
for which both agents choose to cooperate for all
t>T
, thereby proving the main claim of
Example

4.13
.

Let us define the following helper variables:

\displaystyle L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\pi^{i}(a^{i}_{k}\mid\bar{a}_{<k})\,,\quad L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i}):=L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})\pi^{i}(a^{i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t})\,,

\displaystyle m(\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})\,,\quad m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i}):=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})\,.

Since all the policies of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
are deterministic, we have
L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})\in\{0,1\}
. Using this notation, we have that

\displaystyle\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w^{i}(\lambda)\lambda(\bar{a}_{<t})=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\hat{w}^{i}(\pi^{i},\pi^{-i})\overline{\pi}(\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\left[\alpha\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\delta(\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i})+(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\tilde{w}(\pi^{-i})\right]L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})L(\pi^{-i},\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\alpha\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})^{2}+(1-\alpha)\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\tilde{w}(\pi^{-i})L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})L(\pi^{-i},\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\alpha\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})^{2},

\displaystyle=\alpha m(\bar{a}_{<t})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})^{2}\,,

where in
(\ast)
we used the fact that
\bar{a}_{<t}
is symmetric, i.e.,
a_{t}^{i}=a_{t}^{-i}
for all timesteps, and hence
L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})=L(\pi^{-i},\bar{a}_{<t})
in case
\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i}
. In
(\dagger)
we used the fact that
L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})\in\{0,1\}
and hence
L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})^{2}=L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})
.
Using a similar derivation, we have that

\displaystyle\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\left[\alpha\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\delta(\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i})+(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\tilde{w}(\pi^{-i})\right]L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})L(\pi^{-i},\bar{a}_{<t})

\displaystyle=\alpha m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})\,,

where we used the fact that
L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})=1\implies L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t})=1
. Finally, we have that

\displaystyle\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i}a^{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\pi^{-i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\left[\alpha\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\delta(\pi^{i}=\pi^{-i})+(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi^{i})\tilde{w}(\pi^{-i})\right]L(\pi^{i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})L(\pi^{-i},\bar{a}_{<t}a^{-i})

\displaystyle=\alpha m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})\delta(a^{i}=a^{-i})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{-i})\,,

\displaystyle\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i}a^{-i})}{\rho^{i}(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})}=\frac{\alpha m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})\delta(a^{i}=a^{-i})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{-i})}{\alpha m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha\delta(a^{i}=a^{-i})+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{-i})}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\,,

where we used the fact that
m(\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})>0
as the grain-of-uncertainty property is satisfied. Using the rewards
r(C,C)=2,r(D,D)=1,r(C,D)=0,r(D,C)=3
, we have that

\displaystyle Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=C)

\displaystyle-Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=D)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=C)}\left[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=D)}\left[r^{i}(a^{i},a^{-i})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}C)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}r(C,C)+\frac{(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}D)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}r(C,D)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}C)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}r(D,C)-\frac{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}D)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}r(D,D)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}C)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\times 2-\frac{(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}C)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\times 3-\frac{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t}D)}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\times 1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\left[\alpha-(1-\alpha)(m(\bar{a}_{<t}C)+m(\bar{a}_{<t}D))\right]
