\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha-(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\,.

Hence,
Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=C)>Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=D)
iff

\displaystyle\alpha>\frac{m(\bar{a}_{<t})}{1+m(\bar{a}_{<t})}\,.

As
m(\bar{a}_{<t})\in[0,1]
,
\frac{m(\bar{a}_{<t})}{1+m(\bar{a}_{<t})}
is decreasing with decreasing
m(\bar{a}_{<t})
. Furthermore, as
\bar{a}_{<t}
is a prefix of
\bar{a}_{1:t}
, we have that
m(\bar{a}_{1:t})\leq m(\bar{a}_{<t})
, i.e.,
m(\bar{a}_{<t})
is a non-increasing sequence with time, which converges to some value
m_{\infty}
. Now let us define the maximum possible
m_{\infty}
as

\displaystyle m^{*}_{\infty}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}\max_{\bar{a}_{1:t}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t}}m(\bar{a}_{1:t})\,.

Now if we take
\alpha>\frac{m^{*}_{\infty}}{1+m^{*}_{\infty}}
, we are guaranteed that there exists some time
T
such that for all
t>T
, we have that
\alpha>\frac{m(\bar{a}_{<t})}{1+m(\bar{a}_{<t})}
and hence both agents mutually cooperate for all
t>T
. Such policies that forever cooperate after some time
T
are contained within the hypothesis class, and hence the grain-of-truth property is satisfied.

In fact, we can obtain a complete characterization of the behavior of the EBR policy in case of self-play: Let

m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}=m((D,D)^{k})\,,

where we adopt the convention that
m_{0}^{\textrm{defect}}=m((D,D)^{0})=m(\varepsilon)=1
, and

m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}m((D,D)^{k})\,.

Let
T
be the smallest
k
for which
\alpha>\frac{m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}
. Then, we have
\alpha\leq\frac{m((D,D)^{k})}{1+m((D,D)^{k})}
for all
k<T
which means that the EBR policy will defect
117
117

117

Here we assume that the default action in case of a tie is defection. If the default action in case of a tie is cooperation, then we let
T
be the smallest
k
for which
\alpha\geq\frac{m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}
.
up until the
T
-th round. Now since
\alpha>\frac{m_{T}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{T}^{\textrm{defect}}}=\frac{m((D,D)^{T})}{1+m((D,D)^{T})}
, we can see that we get cooperation on the
(T+1)
-th round. Furthermore, since
118
118

118

This is because
m((D,D)^{T}(C,C)^{\ell})\leq m((D,D)^{T})
.

\alpha>\frac{m((D,D)^{T})}{1+m((D,D)^{T})}\geq\frac{m((D,D)^{T}(C,C)^{\ell})}{1+m((D,D)^{T}(C,C)^{\ell})}
for every
\ell\geq 0
, we can see that we will get cooperation in all rounds after the
T
-th round.

If
\alpha\leq\frac{m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}
, then we have that at each timestep
Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=C)\leq Q^{i}_{\rho^{i}}(\bar{a}_{<t},a^{i}=D)
and hence both agents will always defect, starting from timestep 1.

D.5
Additional information on
Example

4.26

We analyze the decision-making of a decoupled Bayesian agent
i
at the start of round
t
, given a personal history
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
. Since the discount factor is
\gamma=0
, the agent’s optimal action
a^{i}_{t}
is the one that maximizes the expected immediate reward, given its beliefs. This is captured by the
Q
-value function:

Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i}):=\mathbb{E}_{\xi^{i}(e^{i}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i})}[r^{i}(e^{i})]\,.

Given that the percept is
e^{i}=(a^{-i},r^{i})
and the reward function
r(a^{i},a^{-i})
is known, the
Q
-value simplifies to an expectation over the opponent’s action
a^{-i}
:

Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i})=\sum_{a^{-i}\in\{C,D\}}\xi^{i}(a^{-i}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i})r(a^{i},a^{-i})\,.

where
\xi^{i}(a^{-i}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i})
is the agent’s predictive marginal probability for the opponent’s action, derived from its mixture environment
\xi^{i}
.

To proceed, we must assume a symmetric history, which arises because both agents are identical, start with the same priors, and follow the same deterministic (tie-breaking) best response policy. Thus,
a^{i}_{k}=a^{-i}_{k}=a_{k}
for all
k<t
, and the personal history is

{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}=(a_{1},(a_{1},r(a_{1},a_{1})),\dots,a_{t-1},(a_{t-1},r(a_{t-1},a_{t-1})))\,.

(65)

Let’s first define the likelihood of this history
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
under each hypothesis
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
.

•

For an environment
\nu_{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
(corresponding to an opponent policy
\pi
),
the likelihood is

\nu_{\pi}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\nu_{\pi}(e^{i}_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}},a^{i}_{k})=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\left[\pi(a^{-i}_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}})\delta(r^{i}_{k}=r(a^{i}_{k},a^{-i}_{k}))\right]\,.

On our symmetric history,
a^{i}_{k}=a^{-i}_{k}=a_{k}
and
r^{i}_{k}=r(a_{k},a_{k})
, this simplifies to:

\nu_{\pi}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\left[\pi(a_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}})\delta(r(a_{k},a_{k})=r(a_{k},a_{k}))\right]=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\pi(a_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}})\,.

We define this likelihood as
L(\pi,{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}):=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\pi(a_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}})
. (Note that the opponent’s policy
\pi
interprets
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}
as its own history, which is identical to the ego-agent’s history in this symmetric case).

•

For the
\nu_{\textrm{copy}}
environment:

\nu_{\textrm{copy}}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\nu_{\textrm{copy}}(e^{i}_{k}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}},a^{i}_{k})=\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\left[\delta(a^{-i}_{k}=a^{i}_{k})\delta(r^{i}_{k}=r(a^{i}_{k},a^{-i}_{k}))\right]\,.

On our symmetric history, this is
\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}\left[\delta(a_{k}=a_{k})\delta(r(a_{k},a_{k})=r(a_{k},a_{k}))\right]=1
.

From
Proposition

3.17
we know that the agent’s posterior belief
w(\nu\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})
is updated as follows:

w(\nu\mid\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}})=w(\nu\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\frac{\nu(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})}{\xi^{i}(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})}\,,

with

\xi^{i}(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i}):=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a_{t}^{i})\,.

A simple (reverse) induction argument on
1\leq\ell\leq t
reveals that

\prod_{k=\ell}^{t}\xi^{i}(e_{k}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{k}^{i})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<\ell}})\prod_{k=\ell}^{t}\nu(e_{k}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{k}^{i})\,.

In particular for
\ell=1
, we get

\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu|\varepsilon)\nu(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu)\nu(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})\,,

where

\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\xi^{i}(e_{k}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{k}^{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\nu(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})=\prod_{k=1}^{t}\nu(e_{k}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{k}^{i})\,.

Let
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
be as in equation
65
, and let
a_{t}^{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
e_{t}^{i}=(a^{-i}_{t},r_{t}^{i})\in\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{R}
be such that
r_{t}^{i}=r(a^{i},a^{-i})
. Since
\xi^{i}(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{t}^{i})=\frac{\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})}{\xi^{i}(e_{<t}^{i}||a_{<t}^{i})}
, we can calculate
\xi^{i}(e_{t}^{i}|{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<k}}a_{t}^{i})
by analyzing
\xi^{i}(e_{<t}^{i}||a_{<t}^{i})
and
\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})
:

\displaystyle\xi^{i}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu)\nu({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})=w(\nu_{\textrm{copy}})\nu_{\textrm{copy}}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})+\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}w(\nu_{\pi})\nu(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\alpha\cdot 1+\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi)L(\pi,{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{=}}\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\,,

where in
(\ast)
we use the fact that
{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}
is a symmetric history and hence
\nu_{\textrm{copy}}(e_{<t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{<t}^{i})=1
, and in
(\dagger)
we used the definition of
m(\cdot)
from
Example

4.26
:

m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi)L(\pi,{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\,.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{1:t}^{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}}w(\nu)\nu({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})=w(\nu_{\textrm{copy}})\nu_{\textrm{copy}}(e_{1:t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{1:t}^{i})+\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}w(\nu_{\pi})\nu(e_{1:t}^{i}\mid\mid a_{1:t}^{i})

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot\delta(a^{-i}_{t}=a^{i}_{t})\delta(r^{i}_{t}=r(a^{i},a^{-i}))+\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}(1-\alpha)\tilde{w}(\pi)L(\pi,\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:{t}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ddagger)}}{{=}}\alpha\delta(a^{-i}_{t}=a_{t}^{i})+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a^{-i}_{t})\,,

where in
(\ddagger)
we used the definition

m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\tilde{w}(\pi)L(\pi,{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\pi(a\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})\,.

We conclude that

\xi^{i}(a^{-i}_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i}_{t}):=\xi^{i}(e^{i}_{t}\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i}_{t})=\frac{\xi^{i}(e_{1:t}^{i}||a_{1:t}^{i})}{\xi^{i}(e_{<t}^{i}||a_{<t}^{i})}=\frac{\alpha\delta(a^{-i}_{t}=a^{i}_{t})+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}a^{-i}_{t})}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})}\,.

This is mathematically identical to the conditional probability
\rho^{i}(a^{-i}\mid\bar{a}_{<t}a^{i})
in
Section

D.4
.

Finally, we compute the
Q
-value difference, using
r(C,C)=2
,
r(C,D)=0
,
r(D,C)=3
,
r(D,D)=1
. Using
Z:=\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})
, we get

\displaystyle Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i}=C)

\displaystyle=\xi^{i}(C\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},C)r(C,C)+\xi^{i}(D\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},C)r(C,D)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha\cdot 1+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)}{Z}\times 2+\frac{\alpha\cdot 0+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D)}{Z}\times 0

\displaystyle=\frac{2(\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C))}{Z}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},a^{i}=D)

\displaystyle=\xi^{i}(C\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},D)r(D,C)+\xi^{i}(D\mid{\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},D)r(D,D)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha\cdot 0+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)}{Z}\times 3+\frac{\alpha\cdot 1+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D)}{Z}\times 1

\displaystyle=\frac{3(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)+\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D)}{Z}\,.

The difference is:

\displaystyle Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},C)-Q_{\xi^{i}}({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}},D)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\left[(2\alpha+2(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C))-(3(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)+\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\left[\alpha-(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)-(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\left[\alpha-(1-\alpha)(m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}C)+m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}}D))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha-(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})}{\alpha+(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})}\,.

Therefore, cooperation is the better action (i.e.,
Q_{\xi^{i}}(\cdot,C)>Q_{\xi^{i}}(\cdot,D)
) if and only if
\alpha-(1-\alpha)m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})>0
, which rearranges to:

\alpha>\frac{m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})}{1+m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})}\,.

This is the exact same condition as in
Section

D.4
. Let
\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:k}
be the personal history corresponding to
k
rounds of mutual defection. We use the example’s notation
m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}:=m(\text{\ae}^{i}_{1:k})
. At time
t=k+1
, the agent’s history satisfies
m({\text{\ae}^{i}_{<t}})=m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}
.

The condition for cooperation at time
k+1
is
\alpha>\frac{m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}
. As
k
increases,
m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}
decreases and converges to
m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}
.
If
\alpha>\frac{m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}
, there will be a finite time
T
(the smallest
k
such that
\alpha>\frac{m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{k}^{\textrm{defect}}}
) after which the agent’s BR is to cooperate. Since both agents are identical, they both switch to cooperation at
T+1
. Furthermore, since
\alpha>\frac{m((D,D)^{T})}{1+m((D,D)^{T})}\geq\frac{m((D,D)^{T}(C,C)^{\ell})}{1+m((D,D)^{T}(C,C)^{\ell})}
for every
\ell\geq 0
, we can see that we will get cooperation in all rounds after the
T
-th round.
If
\alpha\leq\frac{m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}{1+m_{\infty}^{\textrm{defect}}}
, the condition to cooperate is never met (assuming defection is the tie-breaking action), and both agents defect forever. This confirms the claims in the example.

Appendix E
Structural similarities through the lens of algorithmic information: additional information

In this section, we generalize the concept of coupledness for priors over fully supported universes
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
towards possibly non-fully-supported universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, and provide additional results and proofs supporting
Section

5.4
. Throughout this section, we assume a fixed choice of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{E}
and a complete prefix-free encoding thereof.

Let us first define how we can combine two priors
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
into a prior over the set of universes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
.

Definition E.1
.

Let
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
be two probability priors on the spaces of policies and environments, respectively. We define the probability prior
w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
as follows:

(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}})_{\lambda}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}:\\
\nu^{\pi}=\lambda\end{subarray}}w^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}\,,\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\,.

(66)

In other words,
w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
is the probability distribution on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
which is induced by the interaction of a policy and an environment that are independently drawn from
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
according to
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
, respectively.

It is worth noting that the main reason why we have the complicated equation
66
rather than simply defining
(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}})_{\nu^{\pi}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}
is because it is possible for two different pairs
(\pi,\nu)\neq(\tilde{\pi},\tilde{\nu})
to give rise to the same universe
\nu^{\pi}={\tilde{\nu}}^{\tilde{\pi}}
: This can happen, e.g., if
\pi(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=\tilde{\pi}(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=0
for some
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
which would then allow for
\nu
and
\tilde{\nu}
to differ on
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)
(i.e.,
\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*},a)\neq\tilde{\nu}(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*},a)
for some
e\in\mathcal{E}
) while still have
\nu^{\pi}={\tilde{\nu}}^{\tilde{\pi}}
. The following remark summarizes this:

Remark E.2
.

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, then the universe
\lambda
does not necessarily "factorize" into a unique pair of a policy and an environment, i.e., there can be more than one pair
(\pi,\nu)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}
.

Definition E.3
.

We say that the prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, if the restriction of

w^{\tau}
to
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
can be seen as the result of interaction between a policy and an environment which are chosen independently, i.e., there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, we have

w_{\lambda}^{\tau}=C\cdot(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}})_{\lambda}=C\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}:\\
\nu^{\pi}=\lambda\end{subarray}}w^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}\,,

(67)

where

C:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w_{\lambda}^{\tau}\,.

We define the (normalized) probability prior
w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
as follows:

w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}:=\frac{w^{\tau}_{\lambda}}{\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\,,\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\,.

Remark E.4
.

It follows immediately from
Definition

E.3
that
w^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
if and only if there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that
w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
.

However, due to
Remark

E.2
, if
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, then there can be multiple pairs of priors
(w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}})\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}\times\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that
w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}=w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
. In other words, a decoupled prior does not necessarily "factorize" into a unique pair of priors on policies and environments, respectively.

Motivated by the above remark, we define a notion of a degree of structural similarity relative to priors
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
,
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
:

Definition E.5
.

Let
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
be a semiprobability prior on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
, and let
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
be two probability priors on the spaces of policies and environments, respectively.

We define the degree of structural similarity in a universe
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
with respect to the priors
w^{\tau}
,
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
as

S\left(\lambda,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right):=\log\frac{w_{\lambda}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\lambda}}\,.

For a policy
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and an environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we define the degree of structural similarity between
\pi
and
\nu
with respect to the priors
w^{\tau}
,
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
as

S\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right):=S\left(\nu^{\pi},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)\,.

The average degree of structural similarity in
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
with respect to the priors
w^{\tau}
,
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
is defined as

S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right):=\frac{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda}S(\lambda,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}})}{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}S(\lambda,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}})\,.

One thing that is not very satisfying about the above definition is that it does not define the degree of structural similarity only relative to the prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
but it also requires the choice of priors
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
. We will come back to this point later, but for now let us study the properties of the notion the degree of structural similarity of
Definition

E.5
to provide further intuition about it:

Proposition E.6
.

The average degree of structural similarity in
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
with respect to the priors
w^{\tau}
,
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
is equal to the KL divergence between
w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
and
w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
:

S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=KL\left(w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)\,.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward implication of the definitions:

\displaystyle S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}S(\lambda,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}\log\frac{w_{\lambda}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\lambda}}=KL\left(w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)\,.

∎

Corollary E.7
.

For every
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

For every
\pi\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
, we have
S(\nu,\pi,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}})=0
.

(b)

The average degree of structural similarity in
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
with respect to
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
is equal to zero:

S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=0\,.

Proof.

In the light of
Proposition

E.6
, we have

\displaystyle S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=0\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad KL\left(w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad w^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}=\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\lambda}\,,\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad S\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=0\,,\quad\forall\pi\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}\,,\forall\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}\,.

∎

Corollary E.8
.

A prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
if and only if there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that

S\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau},w^{\mathrm{p}},w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)=0\,.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Corollary

E.7
and
Definition

E.3
.
∎

Our ultimate goal in this section is to show the "coupledness" of the Solomonoff prior. In order to simplify the analysis and overcome the technicalities arising from the non-unique "factorization" of a decoupled prior (as described in
Remarks

E.2
and
E.4
), we will focus on studying the "coupledness and structural similarity aspects" on the sets of fully-supported policies and environments, which we define as follows (see also
Definition

5.34
):

Definition E.9
.

We say that a policy
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
is fully supported if
\pi(a|\text{\ae}_{*})>0
for all
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
, and
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=1
for all
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
. We denote the set of fully supported policies as
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
.

We say that an environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
is fully supported if
\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*},a)>0
for all
(\text{\ae}_{*},a,e)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E}
, and
\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*},a)=1
for all
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
. We denote the set of fully supported environments as
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

We say that a universe
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is fully supported if
\lambda(\text{\ae}_{*})>0
for all
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
, and
\lambda
is a measure.
119
119

119

I.e.,
\sum_{\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.04861pt\mathcal{E}$}^{t}}\lambda(\text{\ae}_{1:{t}})=1
for all
t>0
.
We denote the set of fully supported universes as
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
.

We let

\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}:=\left\{\nu^{\pi}:\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau},\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}\right\}\subseteq\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}

be the set of universes which are obtained by letting fully-supported policies and fully-supported environments interact.
120
120

120

It is worth noting that one can show that
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
and
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
are in fact equal. In order to see why we also have
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}\subseteq\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, let
\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
. Since
\lambda
is a fully-spported measure, there exist a unique policy
\pi:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta\mathcal{A}
and a unique environment
\nu:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta\mathcal{E}
such that
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}
, so we need to show that
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
. Since
\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
, there exists
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
such that
\lambda=\lambda_{M}^{\tau}
. We can use
M
to construct an implementation of
\pi
(resp.
\nu
) as a
\tau
-POM through rejection sampling (refer to the proof of
Theorem

E.22
below for further details). Obviously
\pi
and
\nu
are fully supported and hence
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, which implies that
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
.

The following two (almost trivial) propositions describe the structure of an interaction between a fully-supported policy with a fully-supported environment:

Proposition E.10
.

For every
\pi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and every
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we have
\nu^{\pi}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
if and only if
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from the definitions and by observing that for
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}

we have

\nu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{*})>0
if and only if
\pi(a_{i}|{\text{\ae}_{<i}})>0
and
\nu(e_{i}|{\text{\ae}_{<i}},a_{i})>0
for all
1\leq i\leq l(\text{\ae}_{*})
.
∎

Proposition E.11
.

For every
\pi,\tilde{\pi}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
\nu,\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we have
\nu^{\pi}=\tilde{\nu}^{\tilde{\pi}}
if and only if
\nu=\tilde{\nu}
and
\pi=\tilde{\pi}
.

Proof.

The proof is an immediate consequence of the fact that for every
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

\pi(a|\text{\ae}_{*})=\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\nu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{*}ae)}{\nu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{*})}\,,

and for every
(\text{\ae}_{*},a,e)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E}
, we have

\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*},a)=\frac{\nu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{*}ae)}{\sum_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\nu^{\pi}(\text{\ae}_{*}ae^{\prime})}\,.

∎

An immediate corollary of the above two propositions is a simplification of equation
66
in the case of fully supported policies and environments:

Corollary E.12
.

For every priors
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, if
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
then

\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\nu^{\pi}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}\,.

In particular, for every
w^{\mathrm{p}},\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}},\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we have
w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}=\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{e}}
if and only if
w^{\mathrm{p}}=\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}=\tilde{w}^{\mathrm{e}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from
Definition

E.1
and
Propositions

E.10
and
E.11
.
∎

Motivated by the above results, we introduce the notion of "decoupledness on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
" (which is the analog of
Definition

E.3
) as follows:

Definition E.13
.

We say that the prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, if the restriction of

w^{\tau}
to
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
can be seen as the result of interaction between a policy and an environment which are chosen independently, i.e., there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that for all
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and all
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we have

w_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau}=C\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\nu^{\pi}}=Cw^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}\,,

(68)

where

C:=\sum_{\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w_{\lambda}^{\tau}\,.

We define the (normalized) probability prior
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
as follows:

\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}:=\frac{w^{\tau}_{\lambda}}{\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda^{\prime}}}\,,\quad\forall\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\,.

(69)

Remark E.14
.

It follows immediately from
Definition

E.13
that
w^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
if and only if there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}=w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
.

An immediate corollary of the definitions is that coupledness on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
implies coupledness on
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
:

Proposition E.15
.

If
w^{\tau}
is decoupled on
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, then it is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
as well.

In particular, if our goal is to show that the Solomonoff prior is always coupled
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, it suffices to show that it is coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
.

Now since decoupled priors on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
"factorize uniquely" (as implied by
Corollary

E.12
), we can explicitly characterize the factors:

Definition E.16
.

For every policy
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and every environment
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, define

\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}:=\sum_{\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\quad\text{and}\quad\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}:=\sum_{\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}}\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\,,

(70)

Proposition E.17
.

w^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
if and only if
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}
.

Proof.

If
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}
then
w^{\tau}
is trivially decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
due to
Remark

E.14
.

Conversely, if
w^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, then
Remark

E.14
implies that there exist some
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
such that
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}
. Then, for all
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}=\sum_{\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}\left(w^{\mathrm{p}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{e}}\right)_{\nu^{\pi}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\sum_{\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}}w^{\mathrm{p}}_{\pi}w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}=w^{\mathrm{e}}_{\nu}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from
Corollary

E.12
. We deduce that
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}=w^{\mathrm{e}}
. A similar argument shows that
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}=w^{\mathrm{p}}
. Therefore,
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}=\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}
.
∎

Motivated by the above proposition, we define a notion of a degree of structural similarity for fully supported universes relative to a prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
:

Definition E.18
.

Let
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
be a semiprobability prior on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
. We define the degree of structural similarity in a fully-supported universe
\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
with respect to the prior
121
121

121

Notice here that we only specify a prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
relative to which we define the degree of structural similarity. This is in contrast with
Definition

E.5
where we also specify priors
w^{\mathrm{p}}
and
w^{\mathrm{e}}
.

w^{\tau}
as

\check{S}\left(\lambda,w^{\tau}\right):=S\left(\lambda,\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}},\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}\right)=\log\frac{\check{w}_{\lambda}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\left(\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}\right)_{\lambda}}\,.

For a policy
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}
and an environment
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
, we define the degree of structural similarity between the fully-supported
\pi
and
\nu
with respect to the prior
w^{\tau}
as

\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau}\right):=\check{S}\left(\nu^{\pi},w^{\tau}\right)=\log\frac{\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}}\,.

The average degree of structural similarity in
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
with respect to the prior
w^{\tau}
is defined as

\displaystyle\check{S}\left(\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sum_{\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda}\check{S}(\lambda,w^{\tau})}{\sum_{\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}\check{S}(\lambda,w^{\tau})

\displaystyle\;\;=\sum_{\lambda\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\lambda}\log\frac{\check{w}_{\lambda}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\left(\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}\right)_{\lambda}}=KL\left(\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}\otimes\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}\right)\,.

Proposition E.19
.

Let
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
be a semiprobability prior on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
. The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

The prior
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
is decoupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
.

(b)

For every
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and every
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
, we have
\check{S}(\nu,\pi,w^{\tau})=0
.

(c)

The average degree of structural similarity in
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
with respect to
w^{\tau}
is equal to zero:

\check{S}\left(\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}},w^{\tau}\right)=0\,.

Proof.

The equivalence between (a) and (b) follows from
Proposition

E.17
and the definition of
\check{S}(\nu,\pi,w^{\tau})
.

The same proof for
Corollary

E.8
can be used to show the equivalence between (b) and (c).
∎

We now introduce the concept of bounded coupledness and unbounded coupledness on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

Definition E.20
.

Let
w^{\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
be a semiprobability prior on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}
. We call
w^{\tau}

boundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
iff there exists absolute positive constants
C_{1}<C_{2}
such that for all
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
we have

C_{1}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}(\nu)\leq\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}(\nu^{\pi})\leq C_{2}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}(\pi)\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}(\nu)\,.

From
Proposition

E.17
we can see that a decoupled prior on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
is also boundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

We call
w^{\tau}
unboundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
iff it is not boundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

Lemma E.21
.

w^{\tau}
is boundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
iff there exist two absolute real numbers
s_{1}<s_{2}
such that for every
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and every
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
, we have
s_{1}<\check{S}(\nu,\pi,w^{\tau})<s_{2}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Definitions

E.18
and
E.20
.
∎

Now we are ready to study the structural similarity and the coupledness of the Solomonoff prior.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
be the set of abstract probabilistic oracle machines and for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}
, let
\langle M\rangle
be the binary encoding of
M
according to some canonical encoding.

Let
U
be a universal monotone Turing machine and let
w_{M}^{U}=2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}
be the Solomonoff prior over abstract probabilistic oracle machines with respect to
U
. Let
w^{U,\tau}\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
be the induced prior on the space
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
of universes that are implementable as POMs with access to the probabilistic oracle
\tau
, i.e.,

w_{\lambda}^{U,\tau}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\lambda\end{subarray}}w^{U}_{M}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\lambda\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,.

The next theorem shows that the restriction of the Solomonoff prior on
\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{univ}}
is always (unboundedly) coupled:

Theorem E.22
.

For every fixed choices of:

•

a canonical encoding of
\langle M\rangle
of APOMs as binary strings,

•

a universal monotone Turing machine
U
,

•

a probabilistic oracle
\tau
(which may or may not be a RUI-oracle),

•

finite spaces
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{E}
, with
|\mathcal{A}|\geq 2
and
|\mathcal{E}|\geq 2
, and complete prefix encodings thereof,

we have:

(a)

The Solomonoff prior
w^{U,\tau}
is always coupled on
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
, i.e., for every
w^{\mathrm{p}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and every
w^{\mathrm{e}}\in\Delta\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
, there are always a policy
\pi\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and an environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
such that

w^{U,\tau}_{\nu^{\pi}}\neq C\cdot\left(w^{\mathrm{e}}\otimes w^{\mathrm{p}}\right)_{\nu^{\pi}}\,,

where

C:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}w_{\lambda}^{\tau,U}\,.

(b)

The Solomonoff prior
w^{U,\tau}
is always unboundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
.

(c)

There are fully-supported policies and environments sharing arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect to
w^{\tau}
, i.e., for every
s>0
, there is at least some
\pi\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and some
\nu\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
such that

\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau,U}\right)>s\,.

Proof.

From
Lemma

E.21
we can see that
(c)
implies
(b)
.

Now assume that
(b)
is correct, i.e.,
w^{U,\tau}
is unboundedly coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
. It follows from
Definition

E.20
and
Proposition

E.17
that
w^{U,\tau}
is coupled on
\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}
and hence from

Proposition

E.15
, we can see that
w^{U,\tau}
is also coupled on
{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
. This means that
(b)
implies
(a)
, and hence
(c)
implies both
(a)
and
(b)
. Therefore, it suffices to prove
(c)
.

For simplicity, we will write
w^{\tau}
to denote the Solomonoff prior
w^{U,\tau}
that is induced by
U
.

Let us fix some subsets
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
such that
|\tilde{\mathcal{A}}|=|\tilde{\mathcal{E}}|=2
and let
\tilde{a}_{0},\tilde{a}_{1}
(resp.
\tilde{e}_{0},\tilde{e}_{1}
) be the elements of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
).

Let us further fix some computable probability distribution
p_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{c}}
on
\mathcal{A}\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, and some computable probability distribution
p_{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{c}}
on
\mathcal{E}\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.

Let us focus our attention on the family
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
of fully-supported universes
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
which share the following structure:

•

Percepts and actions at time
t
are conditionally independent given history
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
, i.e., for every
({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t},e_{t})\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{E}
, the conditional probability
\lambda(a_{t}e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
factorizes as

\lambda(a_{t}e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})\,,

with
\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
depending only on
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
. We will slightly abuse notation and write
\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})
instead of
\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}}a_{t})
to emphasize that it depends only on
{\text{\ae}_{<t}}
. With this notation, we can write

\lambda(a_{t}e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

•

For every
({\text{\ae}_{<t}},a_{t})\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
, we have

–

\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\in\{1/4,1/2\}
if
a_{t}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
.

–

\pi(a_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\frac{1}{4}p_{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{c}}(a_{t})
if
a_{t}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

For every
({\text{\ae}_{<t}},e_{t})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{E}
, we have

–

\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\in\{1/4,1/2\}
if
e_{t}\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.

–

\nu(e_{t}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})=\frac{1}{4}p_{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{c}}(e_{t})
if
e_{t}\notin\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
.

•

For every
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
, we have
\pi(\tilde{a}_{0}|\text{\ae}_{*})=\nu(\tilde{e}_{0}|\text{\ae}_{*})
and
\pi(\tilde{a}_{1}|\text{\ae}_{*})=\nu(\tilde{e}_{1}|\text{\ae}_{*})
.

It is worth noting that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
is countably infinite: For example, there are infinitely many computable functions
F:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
, and each
F
yields a different
\lambda_{F}=\nu_{F}^{\pi_{F}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
by letting

\displaystyle\pi_{F}(\tilde{a}_{0}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}F({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,

\displaystyle\pi_{F}(\tilde{a}_{1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}-\pi_{F}(\tilde{a}_{0}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{F}(\tilde{e}_{0}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}F({\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{F}(\tilde{e}_{1}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}-\nu_{F}(\tilde{e}_{0}|{\text{\ae}_{<t}})\,.

We claim that it is sufficient for our purposes (i.e., proving the claim
(c)
of the theorem) to show that there are absolute constants
\alpha_{\mathrm{p}},\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}>0
such that for every
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
, we have

\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\leq\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}\,,

(71)

and

\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}\leq\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}\,.

(72)

Indeed, if the above two equations hold, then

\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau,U}\right)=\log\frac{\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}}{\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}}\geq\log\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}}{\left(\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}\right)^{2}}=\log\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}+\log\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}-\log\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}\,.

Now since the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
is countably infinite and since
\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
is a universal prior on
\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}
which contains the countably infinite set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
, we conclude that for every
s>0
, there exists some
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
such that
0<\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}<\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}e^{-s}
, hence

\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{\tau,U}\right)\geq\log\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}+\log\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}-\log\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}>s\,.

In other words, there are fully-supported policies and environments sharing arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect to
w^{\tau}
, as desired.

It remains to prove equation
71
and equation
72
. In the following we will only prove equation
71
, as the proof of the other equation is similar.

From equation
69
, we see that if

C:=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}}w^{\tau}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\,,

(73)

then

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C}w_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}:\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\nu^{\pi}\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,.

Similarly, from equation
69
, equation
70
and equation
73
, we have

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}}\check{w}_{\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}}w_{\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\mathcal{M}:\\
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}\end{subarray}}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,.

Therefore, if we define

\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})=\{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\nu^{\pi}\}\,,

and

\mathcal{M}(\pi)=\big\{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\exists\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}\,,\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}\}\,,

then

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,.

and

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\,.

We claim that it is sufficient for the proof of equation
71
to show that for every
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
there exists an injective function

f:\mathcal{M}(\pi)\to\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})

such that there exists an absolute constant
C_{K}>0
(which does not depend on
\lambda
) such that

K_{U}(\langle f(M)\rangle)\leq K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)+C_{K}\,,\forall M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)\,.

(74)

Indeed, if this is the case then

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\geq}}\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle f(M)\rangle)}\geq\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)-C_{K}}=2^{-C_{K}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the fact that
f
is injective. By choosing
\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}=2^{-C_{K}}
we get our equation
71
.

It remains to show the existence of an injective
f:\mathcal{M}(\pi)\to\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})
satisfying equation
74
. For this, let us fix
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
and an arbitrary machine
M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)
. In the following, we will describe how we can construct the machine
f(M)\in\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})
.

Notice that since
M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)
, then there exists some
\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}
such that
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}
. In particular, for every
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
we have
122
122

122

Note that
\pi
and
\tilde{\nu}
are fully supported, which makes
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}
fully supported as well.

\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})=\pi(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})\,.

So
M
already has the potential of "simulating the policy part" of
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}
. We will formalize this notion by constructing an oracle machine
M_{\mathrm{p}}
which takes
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
as input and produces
a\in\mathcal{A}
such that by equipping
M_{\mathrm{p}}
with the oracle
\tau
, we get that
M_{\mathrm{p}}^{\tau}(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})=\pi(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})
for all
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in(\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*})\times\mathcal{A}
:

Input:

An encoded history
\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
with
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
.

Output:

\langle a\rangle
with
a\in\mathcal{A}

h^{\prime}\leftarrow\varepsilon

while

h^{\prime}\neq\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle

do

Simulate running the machine
M
to produce
l(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)
bits

Let
h^{\prime}
be the first
l(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)
bits on the output tape of the simulated machine
M

a\leftarrow\varepsilon

while

a
is not a valid coding word of an action in
\mathcal{A}

do

Continue simulating the machine
M
to produce one additional output bit

Append the last output bit to
a

return

a

Algorithm 8

Oracle machine
M_{\mathrm{p}}
such that when equipped with
\tau
, it samples from
\pi(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})

Note that since
M
has an output distribution
\lambda_{M}^{\tau}
which is a fully supported universe we have:

•

\lambda_{M}^{\tau}
a measure (cf.
Definition

E.9
), hence the simulation of
M
does not get stuck into infinite loops (otherwise
\lambda_{M}
would be a proper semimeasure, not a measure). Therefore, every simulation of
M
is guaranteed to produce at least
l(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)
bits.

•

\lambda_{M}^{\tau}(\text{\ae}_{*})>0
and hence the while loop in the above algorithm is guaranteed to eventually produce the history
\text{\ae}_{*}
.

We conclude that the above algorithm correctly samples
\pi(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})
because it follows a rejection sampling procedure.

Now since
\nu
and
\pi
share a common structure as described in the definition of the family
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
, we can use the ability of
M_{\mathrm{p}}
to simulate
\pi
to also simulate
\nu
. We formalize this by constructing a machine
M_{\mathrm{e}}
as follows:

Input:

An encoded history
h=\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
with
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
.

Output:

\langle e\rangle
with
e\in\mathcal{E}

a\leftarrow M_{\mathrm{p}}(h)

//
Convert
a\in\mathcal{A}
to
e\in\mathcal{E}
so that
M_{\mathrm{e}}^{\tau}(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*})=\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*})

if

a=\langle\tilde{a}_{0}\rangle

then

e\leftarrow\langle\tilde{e}_{0}\rangle

else if

a=\langle\tilde{a}_{1}\rangle

then

e\leftarrow\langle\tilde{e}_{1}\rangle

else

//
I.e.,
a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{A}}

Sample
e
from
\mathcal{E}\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
according to the distribution
p_{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{c}}

return

e

Algorithm 9

Oracle machine
M_{\mathrm{e}}
such that when equipped with
\tau
, it samples from
\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*})

Now we are ready to describe the computational procedure of the machine
f(M)
:

h\leftarrow\varepsilon

t\leftarrow 1

while

True

do

a_{t}\leftarrow M_{\mathrm{p}}(h)

Append
a_{t}
to the output tape

e_{t}\leftarrow M_{\mathrm{e}}(h)

Append
e_{t}
to the output tape

h\leftarrow ha_{t}e_{t}

Algorithm 10

Oracle machine
f(M)
such that
\lambda_{f(M)}^{\tau}=\nu^{\pi}

It is not hard to see that the
K_{U}(f(M))\leq K_{U}(M)+C_{K}
where
C_{K}
is some absolute constant related to the program code overhead needed to apply the above procedure on top of using the code of
M
(recall that we can define the procedure of
M
once and then refer to it in the simulation of
M_{\mathrm{p}}
and
M_{\mathrm{e}}
). Furthermore, we can see that changing the machine
M
yields a syntactically different machine
f(M)
. Therefore,
M\mapsto f(M)
is indeed injective.
∎

Remark E.23
.

Theorem

E.22
is not too sensitive to the particular form
w_{M}^{U}=2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}
of the Solomonoff prior: If

•

g:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
is a decreasing function whose decay is not faster than exponential, i.e.,
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{-\log g(t)}{t}<\infty
, and

•

\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}}g(K_{U}(\langle M\rangle))\leq 1
,

then we can adapt the proof of
Theorem

E.22
to show that it still applies to
w_{M}=g(K_{U}(\langle M\rangle))
.

E.1
Universes sharing a common algorithmic structure

Recall that in the proof of
Theorem

E.22
, in order to show that there are (fully-supported) policies and environments sharing arbitrary large degrees of structural similarity according to the Solomonoff prior, we constructed a family
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
of (fully-supported) universes in which policies and environments share a common structure, and then we showed that inside
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
we can always find
\lambda=\nu^{\pi}
for which the degree of structural similarity between
\nu
and
\pi
according to the Solomonoff prior is arbitrarily large.

This fact does not depend on the particular form of the structure which is shared among all universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{sim}}^{\tau}
. In this section, we formalize a general notion of "algorithmic structure" and then show that as long as this structure satisfies a few conditions, then we can always find policies and environments respecting this structure and which share arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect to the Solomonoff prior.

Intuitively, an algorithmic structure can be understood as a "boilerplate template" structure that can be respected by multiple computer programs. Let us consider the following two programs

Function

f(
x
)
:

return

x+1

Function

main_func(
x
)
:

y\leftarrow 0

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

x

do

y\leftarrow\textnormal{{f}}(y+\textnormal{{f}}(x))

return

y

Algorithm 11

Pseudocode using the subroutine function
f(x)=x+1

Function

f(
x
)
:

return

x^{2}

Function

main_func(
x
)
:

y\leftarrow 0

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

x

do

y\leftarrow\textnormal{{f}}(y+\textnormal{{f}}(x))

return

y

Algorithm 12

Pseudocode using the subroutine function
f(x)=x^{2}

As one can see, the main function
main_func
has a similar algorithmic structure in both programs, as it uses the subroutine function
f
in the same way, and they differ only by the specification of
f
.

We will use this idea to define a notion of an algorithmic structure that can be shared by a family of universes. We will basically define a fixed "boilerplate template" structure which refers to some subroutine that is not specified, and we will define the family of all universes that can be implemented by an algorithm that respects the "boilerplate template" structure. Different specifications of the subroutine yield different universes in the family.

The reader might have noticed that the notion of subroutine is very similar to the notion of an oracle that can be called by an oracle machine, and this is indeed how we will formalize this notion.

For this, we need to introduce machines that can use more than one oracle.

Definition E.24

(Multi-Oracle Machines)
.

An
n
-abstract probabilistic oracle machine (
n
-APOM)
is a monotone Turing machine equipped with
n
distinct oracle tapes, denoted
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
, and a corresponding set of
n
distinct query instructions,
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n}
. When instruction
Q_{i}
is executed, the machine interacts with oracle tape
T_{i}
.

An
n
-probabilistic oracle machine (
n
-POM)
is a pair
(M,(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n}))
, where
M
is an
n
-APOM and each
\tau_{i}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
is a probabilistic oracle
123
123

123

Recall that if
x
is the query to the oracle
\tau_{i}
, the oracle returns 1 with probability
\tau_{i}(x)
, and returns 0 with probabilty
1-\tau_{i}(x)
.
. When instruction
Q_{i}
is called, the content of the tape
T_{i}
is interpreted as a query which is answered by oracle
\tau_{i}
and the content of the tape
T_{i}
is replaced with the answer of the oracle. The standard APOM and POM are equivalent to a
1
-APOM and
1
-POM, respectively.

Each of the
n
probabilistic oracles can be understood as a distinct, black-box
subroutine
that the main program (the
n
-APOM) can call. The
n
-APOM defines the high-level logic or "template", while the specific oracles provide the implementation details for its sub-components.

Remark E.25
.

While the above definition only uses probabilistic oracles that return single random bits, the definition is general enough to model probabilistic oracles that can return random bit strings. Basically, if
O:\{0,1\}^{*}\to\Delta\{0,1\}^{*}
is a probabilistic oracle that returns random bit strings, we can effectively represent it using two probabilistic oracles,
\tau_{e}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
and
\tau_{b}:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
, where:

•

\tau_{e}(\langle x,y\rangle)
represents the probability that
O(x)=y
conditioned on
y
being a prefix of
O(x)
.

•

\tau_{b}(\langle x,y\rangle)
represents the probability that
O(x)=y1
conditioned on
y
being a strict prefix of
O(x)
.

The following pseudocode illustrates how we can use
\tau_{e}
and
\tau_{b}
to simulate sampling from
O
:

Input:

A bit string
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Output:

A random sample from
O(x)

y\leftarrow\varepsilon

while

True

do

if

O^{\tau_{e}}(\langle x,y\rangle)=1

then

return

y
;

else if

O^{\tau_{b}}(\langle x,y\rangle)=1

then

y\leftarrow y1

else

y\leftarrow y0

Algorithm 13

Sampling from
O
using
\tau_{e}
and
\tau_{b}

The framework with multiple oracles allows us to compose machines. An
n
-APOM can be reduced to a machine with fewer free subroutines by specifying what some of its oracles are. For instance, if we fix a base probabilistic oracle
\tau
, we can construct a complex
1
-POM by using an
n
-APOM where the first oracle remains
\tau
, but the other
n-1
oracles are themselves implemented by other
1
-POMs that also rely on
\tau
. The resulting composite machine is a single, more complex
1
-POM that depends only on the base oracle
\tau
.

More formally, we can think of this composition syntactically. Given an
n
-APOM,
M
, and
n-1
standard
1
-APOMs,
M_{2},\dots,M_{n}
, we can construct a new
1
-APOM, denoted
M[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]
. This new machine simulates
M
as follows:

•

When
M
calls its first oracle,
Q_{1}
, the new machine passes this query to its own single oracle.

•

When
M
calls any other oracle
Q_{i}
(for
i\in\{2,\dots,n\}
), it instead simulates the execution of the corresponding machine
M_{i}
, feeding the query string as input to
M_{i}
and using the output of
M_{i}
as the oracle’s answer. While executing the machine
M_{i}
, any call to the (single) oracle of
M_{i}
is directed to the single oracle of the new machine.

This allows us to formally define the notion of a shared algorithmic structure.

Definition E.26

(Algorithmic Structure)
.

An
n
-APOM,
M
, defines an
algorithmic structure
. A
1
-APOM,
M^{\prime}
, is said to
possess the structure
defined by
M
if there exist
n-1
other
1
-APOMs,
M_{2},\dots,M_{n}
, such that
M^{\prime}
is computationally equivalent to the composite machine
M[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]
.

Now we can define specific types of algorithmic structures for policies, environments and universes.

Definition E.27

(Algorithmic Structure for policies)
.

An
algorithmic structure for policies
is an
n
-APOM,
M
, that is syntactically constrained to take as inputs valid encodings
\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
from
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and produce outputs
\langle a\rangle
with
a\in\mathcal{A}
. We say that a policy
\pi:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}
possesses the structure
M
with respect to a probabilistic oracle
\tau:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
if there exists a 1-APOM
M_{\pi}
such that

•

\pi(a\mid\text{\ae}_{*})=\mathbb{P}[M_{\pi}^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)=\langle a\rangle]
, with
\mathbb{P}[M_{\pi}^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)=\langle a\rangle]
denoting the probability that
M_{\pi}^{\tau}
outputs
\langle a\rangle
upon input
\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
, and

•

M_{\pi}
possesses the structure defined by
M
.

We denote the set of policies that possess the structure
M
with respect to
\tau
as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M)
.

Definition E.28

(Algorithmic Structure for environments)
.

An
algorithmic structure for environments
is an
n
-APOM,
M
, that is syntactically constrained to take as inputs
\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle
with
(\text{\ae}_{*},a)\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}
and produce outputs
\langle e\rangle
with
e\in\mathcal{E}
. We say that an environment
\nu:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}
possesses the structure
M
with respect to a probabilistic oracle
\tau:\{0,1\}^{*}\to[0,1]
if there exists a 1-APOM
M_{\nu}
such that

•

\nu(e\mid\text{\ae}_{*}a)=\mathbb{P}[M_{\nu}^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle)=\langle e\rangle]
, with
\mathbb{P}[M_{\nu}^{\tau}(\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle)=\langle e\rangle]
denoting the probability that
M_{\nu}^{\tau}
outputs
\langle e\rangle
upon input
\langle\text{\ae}_{*},a\rangle
, and

•

M_{\nu}
possesses the structure defined by
M
.

We denote the set of environments that possess the structure
M
with respect to
\tau
as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M)
.

Definition E.29

(Algorithmic Structure for universes)
.

An
algorithmic structure for universes
is an
n
-APOM,
M
, that does not have any inputs, and writes sequences from
\mathcal{B}^{\#}
on its output tape. We say that a semimeasure
\sigma:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to[0,1]
possesses the structure
M
with respect to a probabilistic oracle
\tau
if there exists a 1-APOM
M_{\sigma}
such that

•

\sigma=\lambda_{M_{\sigma}}^{\tau}
, and

•

M_{\sigma}
possesses the structure defined by
M
.

We denote the set of universes that possess the structure
M
with respect to
\tau
as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M)
.

One way to get an algorithmic structure for universes is by specifying a pair of algorithmic structures, one for policies and one for environments:

Definition E.30
.

Let
M_{p}
be an algorithmic structure for policies and
M_{e}
be an algorithmic structure for environments with the same number
n
of oracles. We can syntactically define an
n
-APOM that alternates between applying
M_{p}
and
M_{e}
. This induces an algorithmic structure for universes. We refer to the set of universes which possess this induced structure with respect to a probabilistic oracle
\tau
as
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
.

It is worth noting that if
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M_{p})
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M_{e})
, then we do not necessarily have
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
. The reason is that
\nu
and
\pi
might be using different sets of 1-APOMs
M_{2},\ldots,M_{n}
for the specification of their subroutines. More precisely, if
M_{2,\pi},\ldots,M_{n,\pi}
are 1-APOMs such that
\pi=M_{p}[M_{2,\pi},\ldots,M_{n,\pi}]^{\tau}
, and if
M_{2,\nu},\ldots,M_{n,\nu}
are 1-APOMs such that
\nu=M_{e}[M_{2,\nu},\ldots,M_{n,\nu}]^{\tau}
, then
\nu^{\pi}
is not guaranteed to be in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
if
(M_{2,\pi},\ldots,M_{n,\pi})\neq(M_{2,\nu},\ldots,M_{n,\nu})
. However, if
(M_{2,\pi},\ldots,M_{n,\pi})=(M_{2,\nu},\ldots,M_{n,\nu})
, then we would indeed have
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
.

We will show that if the structures
M_{p}
and
M_{e}
satisfy a few properties, then we can always find
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M_{p})
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M_{e})
such that
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
and
\pi
and
\nu
share arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect to the Solomonoff prior. A key property that is needed is the "identifiability" of subroutines. In the following, we will formally define this property, but first, we need to introduce proper subroutines:

Definition E.31

(Proper subroutines)
.

A POM
M^{\tau}
is said to be a proper subroutine if for every input
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, the POM
M^{\tau}
almost surely halts with some
y\in\mathcal{B}
written on the output tape. In other words,
M^{\tau}
induces a mapping
M^{\tau}:\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\Delta\mathcal{B}
.

Definition E.32

(Distributional equivalence of POMs)
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be two 1-APOMs. We say that
M_{1}^{\tau}
and
M_{2}^{\tau}
are distributionally equivalent if for every
x,y\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[M_{1}^{\tau}(x)=y]=\mathbb{P}[M_{2}^{\tau}(x)=y]\,.

We write
M_{1}^{\tau}\equiv M_{2}^{\tau}
to denote the distributional equivalence of
M_{1}^{\tau}
and
M_{2}^{\tau}
.

Definition E.33

(Identifiable subroutines)
.

Let
M
be an
n
-APOM. We say that the algorithmic structure
M
has identifiable subroutines with respect to the probabilistic oracle
\tau
if there are
n-1
2-APOMs
I_{2},\ldots,I_{n}
such that for every
n-1
1-APOMs
M_{2},\ldots,M_{n}
for which
M_{2}^{\tau},\ldots,M_{n}^{\tau}
are proper subroutines, and every
2\leq i\leq n
, the POMs
I_{i}[M[M_{2},\ldots,M_{n}]]^{\tau}\equiv M_{i}^{\tau}
, i.e., for every
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
and every
y\in\mathcal{B}
,
124
124

124

Note that by definition, a proper subroutine can only output single bits, so we only need to check
y\in\mathcal{B}
.
we have

\mathbb{P}[I_{i}[M[M_{2},\ldots,M_{n}]]^{\tau}(x)=y]=\mathbb{P}[M_{i}^{\tau}(x)=y]\,.

In other words, if we had access to an oracle that on any query
x
returns samples from
M[M_{2},\ldots,M_{n}]^{\tau}(x)
, then we can write a program to simulate samples from
M_{i}^{\tau}(x)
for all
x
as well.

Let us construct a simple example to illustrate the concept of subroutine identifiability:

Example E.34

(A simple example of an Algorithmic Structure with Identifiable Subroutines)
.

Consider the algorithmic structure defined by the following 2-APOM
M_{struct}
:

Input:

An input string
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Output:

A canonical encoding of a pair of strings
\langle y,z\rangle
.

y\leftarrow Q_{2}(x)

z\leftarrow Q_{1}(y)

return

\langle y,z\rangle

Algorithm 14

Algorithmic Structure
M_{struct}

This structure has "identifiable subroutines" because we can construct an "identifier" machine,
I_{2}
, that can recover the behavior of any proper subroutine
M_{2}
that may be plugged into the second oracle slot of
M_{struct}
.

Input:

An input string
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Output:

A string
y
.

s\leftarrow Q_{2}(x)

Decode
s
into a pair
\langle y,z\rangle

return

y

Algorithm 15

Identifier machine
I_{2}

Now, let us verify that
I_{2}
can indeed identify any subroutine specification for the second oracle
Q_{2}
of
M_{struct}
. Let
M_{2}
be an arbitrary 1-APOM. We need to show that the 1-POM
I_{2}[M_{struct}[M_{2}]]^{\tau}
is distributionally equivalent to
M_{2}^{\tau}
. Let’s trace the execution of
I_{2}[M_{struct}[M_{2}]]^{\tau}
on an input
x
:

1.

The machine begins by simulating
I_{2}
. The first step of
I_{2}
is to call its second oracle,
Q_{2}
, with input
x
.

2.

According to the composition rule, this call to
I_{2}
’s second oracle is replaced by a simulation of the machine plugged into that slot, which is
M_{struct}[M_{2}]
. This simulation is run with the base oracle
\tau
.

3.

So, we execute
M_{struct}[M_{2}]^{\tau}(x)
. This machine, in turn, first simulates
M_{2}
with the oracle
\tau
. Let the probabilistic output of
M_{2}^{\tau}(x)
be the string
y_{out}
.

4.

Next,
M_{struct}[M_{2}]^{\tau}
calls its first oracle (which is passed through to the base oracle
\tau
) with
y_{out}
. Let the result be
z_{out}
. It then outputs the pair
\langle y_{out},z_{out}\rangle
.

5.

This pair is the result returned to
I_{2}
from its oracle call in step 1.

6.

I_{2}
then proceeds to its second step: It decodes the pair
\langle y_{out},z_{out}\rangle
and outputs the first element, which is
y_{out}
.

The final output is
y_{out}
, which is precisely the probabilistic output of
M_{2}^{\tau}(x)
. Therefore, the machines are distributionally equivalent, and we have successfully "identified" or extracted the behavior of the subroutine
M_{2}
.

The structure
M_{struct}
in the above example has identifiable subroutines because it explicitly includes the output of its subroutines in its own final output. This "pass-through" design is a straightforward way to ensure that the behavior of sub-components is not obscured by subsequent computations. Nevertheless, there are more complex algorithmic structures that can still have identifiable subroutines, as illustrated in the following example:

Example E.35

(A Slightly More Complex Example of an Algorithmic Structure with Identifiable Subroutines)
.

Consider the algorithmic structure defined by the following 3-APOM
M_{struct}
:

Input:

An input string
x\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Output:

A canonical encoding of a pair
\langle r,y\rangle
or
\langle x^{\prime},y\rangle
.

if

x=\varepsilon

then

Flip a random coin
r\in\{0,1\}

if

r=0

then

y\leftarrow Q_{2}(\varepsilon)

else

y\leftarrow Q_{3}(\varepsilon)

return

\langle r,y\rangle

else

Draw a random string
x^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{l(x)}

x^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow x\oplus x^{\prime}

//
We compute the bit-wise XOR between
x
and
x^{\prime}
.

if

the number of ones in
x^{\prime}
is even

then

y\leftarrow Q_{2}(x^{\prime\prime})

else

y\leftarrow Q_{3}(x^{\prime\prime})

return

\langle x^{\prime},y\rangle

Algorithm 16

A Slightly More Complex Algorithmic Structure
M_{struct}

The structure
M_{struct}
also has identifiable subroutines, although extracting the subroutines requires a more sophisticated process than simply decoding the output. To identify
M_{2}^{\tau}
, we must repeatedly sample from the composite machine
M_{struct}[M_{2},M_{3}]^{\tau}
and filter for the cases where the random string
x^{\prime}
has an even number of ones.

The following pseudocode illustrates the algorithm of
I_{2}
.

Input:

An input string
x_{in}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Output:

A random sample from
M_{2}^{\tau}(x_{in})

if

x_{in}=\varepsilon

then

while

True

do

Call the second oracle
Q_{2}
with input
x_{in}=\varepsilon
. Let the result be
s

Decode
s
as a pair
\langle r,y\rangle

if

r=0

then

return

y

else

while

True

do

Draw a random string
x\in\{0,1\}^{l(x_{in})}
using the internal randomness tape

Call the second oracle
Q_{2}
with input
x
. Let the result be
s

Decode
s
as a pair
\langle x^{\prime},y\rangle

if

x\oplus x^{\prime}=x_{in}

and
the number of ones in
x^{\prime}
is even

then

return

y

Algorithm 17

Identifier machine
I_{2}

An analogous machine
I_{3}
can be designed by checking for
r=1
when
x_{in}=\varepsilon
, and checking for an odd number of ones in
x^{\prime}
in case
x_{in}\neq\varepsilon
.

To prove that the structure is identifiable, we must show that
I_{2}[M_{struct}[M_{2},M_{3}]]^{\tau}
is distributionally equivalent to
M_{2}^{\tau}
. We analyze the two cases based on the input
x_{in}
to
I_{2}
.

•

Case 1:
x_{in}\neq\varepsilon
.
The machine
I_{2}
enters a loop where it draws a random string
x
of the same length as
x_{in}
and queries its second oracle (the composite machine
M_{struct}[M_{2},M_{3}]^{\tau}
) with this
x
. The composite machine, in turn, draws another random mask
x^{\prime}
and calls either
M_{2}
or
M_{3}
with the input
x^{\prime\prime}=x\oplus x^{\prime}
. The identifier
I_{2}
accepts the result
y
only if the number of ones in
x^{\prime}
is even (indicating
M_{2}
was called) and if
x\oplus x^{\prime}=x_{in}
. Since both
x
and
x^{\prime}
are drawn uniformly at random, the resulting input
x^{\prime\prime}
to the subroutine is also uniformly random over strings of that length. Therefore, for any target
x_{in}
, the condition
x^{\prime\prime}=x_{in}
will eventually be met. When it is, the returned
y
is a correct sample from
M_{2}^{\tau}(x_{in})
.

•

Case 2:
x_{in}=\varepsilon
.
The machine
I_{2}
enters a simpler loop. It repeatedly queries its second oracle with the empty string
\varepsilon
. In response, the composite machine
M_{struct}[M_{2},M_{3}]^{\tau}
flips a coin
r
. If
r=0
, it calls
M_{2}(\varepsilon)
; otherwise, it calls
M_{3}(\varepsilon)
. It returns the pair
\langle r,y\rangle
. The identifier
I_{2}
waits until it receives a pair where
r=0
. Since the coin flip is fair, this is guaranteed to happen eventually. When it does, the corresponding
y
is a correct sample from
M_{2}^{\tau}(\varepsilon)
.

In both cases, the identifier machine
I_{2}
successfully simulates a sample from
M_{2}^{\tau}(x_{in})
using its oracle access to the composite machine. This process of filtering executions is a form of rejection sampling. Thus, the structure has identifiable subroutines.

With this general framework for algorithmic structures, we can now state the main result of this section.

Theorem E.36
.

For every fixed choice of:

•

a canonical encoding
\langle M\rangle
of APOMs as binary strings,

•

a universal monotone Turing machine
U
,

•

a probabilistic oracle
\tau
(which may or may not be a RUI-oracle),

•

sets
\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{E}
, with
|\mathcal{A}|\geq 2
and
|\mathcal{E}|\geq 2
, and corresponding complete prefix-free codes,

if
M_{p}
and
M_{e}
are algorithmic structures for policies and environments, respectively, such that:

•

M_{p}
and
M_{e}
have the same number of oracles
n\geq 2
,

•

M_{p}
and
M_{e}
have identifiable subroutines with respect to
\tau
, and

•

the set of fully-supported universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
is infinite,

then there are fully-supported policies
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M_{p})
and fully-supported environments
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M_{e})
sharing arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity with respect to the Solomonoff prior
w^{U,\tau}
, i.e., for every
s>0
, there exists at least one such pair
(\pi,\nu)
for which

\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{U,\tau}\right)>s\,.

Furthermore, we can choose this pair
(\pi,\nu)
such that their subroutines are identical, which implies that their interaction
\nu^{\pi}
is an element of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
.

Proof.

The proof strategy mirrors that of
Theorem

E.22
. We first establish the existence of an infinite family of suitable pairs
(\pi,\nu)
, then show that complexity savings arguments lead to arbitrarily large degrees of structural similarity for some members of this family.

The theorem assumes that the set of fully-supported universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
is infinite. By definition, any universe
\lambda
in this set is formed by the interaction of a policy
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M_{p})
and an environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M_{e})
that share the
same
underlying subroutines
\{M_{i}\}_{i=2}^{n}
. Since each such universe is fully-supported, the pair
(\pi,\nu)
that generates it is unique (
Proposition

E.11
) and both
\pi
and
\nu
are also fully-supported (
Proposition

E.10
). Therefore, we have an infinite family of pairs
(\pi,\nu)
of fully-supported policies
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\tau}(M_{p})
