and fully-supported environments
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}^{\tau}(M_{e})
such that
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
.

Our goal is to show that for any
s>0
, there is a pair
(\pi,\nu)
in this family with
\check{S}(\pi,\nu,w^{U,\tau})>s
. Recall that
\check{S}\left(\pi,\nu,w^{U,\tau}\right)=\log\frac{\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}}{\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}}
. As in the proof of
Theorem

E.22
, it is sufficient to show that there are absolute constants
\alpha_{p},\alpha_{e}>0
such that for every pair
(\pi,\nu)
in our infinite family, the following inequalities hold:

\alpha_{\mathrm{p}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\leq\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{env}}_{\nu}\leq\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}\,.

(75)

If this condition holds, then
\check{S}(\pi,\nu,w^{U,\tau})\geq\log(\alpha_{p}\alpha_{e})-\log(\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}})
. Since our family of universes is infinite and the Solomonoff prior
w^{U,\tau}
must be non-zero for all of them, then for any
\delta>0
, there must exist a universe
\nu^{\pi}
in the family with prior weight
\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}<\delta
. By choosing
\delta
small enough, we can make
-\log(\check{w}_{\nu^{\pi}}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}})
arbitrarily large, thus making
\check{S}
exceed any given
s
.

Let us now fix a pair
(\pi,\nu)
from our infinite family, such that
\pi=M_{p}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]^{\tau}
and
\nu=M_{e}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]^{\tau}
for some set of 1-APOMs
\{M_{i}\}_{i=2}^{n}
, and their interaction is
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{univ}}^{\tau}(M_{p},M_{e})
. We will prove the first inequality in equation
75
; the proof for the second is symmetric.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})
be the set of 1-APOMs that compute the universe
\nu^{\pi}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})=\{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:M^{\tau}=\nu^{\pi}\}\,.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\pi)
be the set of 1-APOMs that compute a universe whose policy part is
\pi
, i.e.,

\mathcal{M}(\pi)=\big\{M\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{APOM}}:\exists\tilde{\nu}\in\check{\mathcal{M}}^{\tau}_{\mathrm{env}}\,,\lambda_{M}^{\tau}=\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}\}\,.

We construct an injective map
f:\mathcal{M}(\pi)\to\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})
such that for any
M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)
, we have
K_{U}(f(M))\leq K_{U}(M)+C_{p}
for some absolute constant
C_{p}
independent of
M
and
\pi
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)
. By definition, this machine computes some universe
\tilde{\nu}^{\pi}
whose policy component is our target policy
\pi
.
First, we construct a policy simulator machine,
M_{\pi}
, from
M
. This machine, given oracle access to
\tau
, can sample from
\pi(\cdot\mid\text{\ae}_{*})
for any history
\text{\ae}_{*}
. It does so by rerunning a simulation of
M^{\tau}
and generating bitstrings of length
l(\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle)
until it generates
\langle\text{\ae}_{*}\rangle
and then it outputs the encoding of the next action. As described in the proof of
Theorem

E.22
, we can see that the output distribution of
M_{\pi}^{\tau}
is equal to
\pi
.

Since the policy structure
M_{p}
has identifiable subroutines with respect to
\tau
, there are identifier machines
\{I_{p,i}\}_{i=2}^{n}
such that for all
2\leq i\leq n
, we have

I_{p,i}[M_{p}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]]^{\tau}\equiv M_{i}^{\tau}\,,

where
\equiv
denotes the distributional equivalence, i.e., for every
x\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
and every
y\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[I_{p,i}[M_{p}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]]^{\tau}(x)=y]=\mathbb{P}[M_{i}^{\tau}(x)=y]
.

We can now construct a set of 1-APOMs
\{M_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=2}^{n}
, where each
M_{i}^{\prime}
is distributionally equivalent to the corresponding subroutine
M_{i}
when equipped with
\tau
: Let
M_{i}^{\prime}=I_{p,i}[M_{\pi}]
, then

\displaystyle M_{i}^{\prime\tau}\equiv I_{p,i}[M_{\pi}]^{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\equiv}}I_{p,i}[M_{p}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]]^{\tau}\equiv M_{i}^{\tau}\,,

where
(\ast)
follows from the fact that
M_{\pi}^{\tau}\equiv M_{p}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]^{\tau}
(as they both simulate the policy
\pi
).

Now that we can simulate the subroutines
M_{i}^{\tau}
, we can construct a 1-APOM
M_{\nu}
that simulates the target environment
\nu
. We define
M_{\nu}=M_{e}[M_{2}^{\prime},\dots,M_{n}^{\prime}]
. When equipped with
\tau
, we get:

M_{\nu}^{\tau}\equiv M_{e}[M_{2}^{\prime},\dots,M_{n}^{\prime}]^{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\dagger)}}{{\equiv}}M_{e}[M_{2},\dots,M_{n}]^{\tau}\equiv\nu\,,

where
(\dagger)
follows from the fact that
M_{i}^{\prime\tau}\equiv M_{i}^{\tau}
for all
2\leq i\leq n
.

The machine
f(M)
can now be defined as a 1-APOM that alternates between running the policy simulator
M_{\pi}
and the environment simulator
M_{\nu}
. The complete machine
f(M)
can be described by a fixed universal program that takes the description of
M
as input and implements the logic described above. This universal program needs the code for the identifiers
\{I_{p,i}\}
and the structures
M_{p},M_{e}
, all of which are fixed. Therefore, its complexity is bounded by
K_{U}(f(M))\leq K_{U}(M)+C_{p}
for some constant
C_{p}
that depends only on the fixed structures. The map
M\mapsto f(M)
is injective because the description of
M
is part of the description of
f(M)
.

With this injective map and complexity bound, the argument is identical to the final part of the proof of
Theorem

E.22
. We have:

\displaystyle\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}\textrm{-}\mathrm{env}}_{\nu^{\pi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(\nu^{\pi})}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M^{\prime}\rangle)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle f(M)\rangle)}\quad(\text{since }f\text{ is injective})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)-C_{p}}

\displaystyle=2^{-C_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{C}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}(\pi)}2^{-K_{U}(\langle M\rangle)}\right)=2^{-C_{p}}\check{w}^{\tau,\mathrm{pol}}_{\pi}\,.

By choosing
\alpha_{p}=2^{-C_{p}}
, we get the first inequality. A symmetric argument using the identifiability of the subroutines of
M_{e}
yields the second inequality for a constant
\alpha_{e}=2^{-C_{e}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Multi-agent environments and their relation to universes

Here, we provide some additional details on multi-agent environments and how to ‘embed’ agent policies by interfacing them with this multi-agent environment.

Definition F.1
.

A
k
-multi-agent environment
\bar{\nu}
is a tuple

\left(k,\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)},\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)},f_{\nu}\right)\,,

which satisfies:

•

\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)}
are
k
finite sets which we interpret as the action spaces of the
k
agents, respectively.

•

\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
are
k
finite sets which we interpret as the partial observation spaces of the
k
agents, respectively.

•

\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
are
k
finite subsets of
[0,1]
which we interpret as the reward spaces of the
k
agents, respectively. We write

\mathcal{E}^{(i)}:=\mathcal{O}^{(i)}\times\mathcal{R}^{(i)}

to denote the percept space of the
i
-th agent. We also use the notation

\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(i)*}=(\mathcal{A}^{(i)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(i)})^{*}
for the set of histories from the perspective of the
i
-th agent.

•

f_{\bar{\nu}}
is a
mapping
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(1:k)*}\times\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}
, where

\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}^{(i)}\,,

\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}^{(i)}\,,

and

\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(1:k)*}:=\left(\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}\right)^{*}\,.

For every
\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}\right)^{t-1}
, every
e^{(1:k)}_{t}=\left(e^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{t}\right)\in\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}
, and every
a^{(1:k)}_{t}=\left(a^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,a^{(k)}_{t}\right)\in\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}
, we introduce the notation

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}\left(e^{(1:k)}_{t}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t},a^{(1:k)}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle:=\bar{\nu}\left(e^{(1)}_{t},\dots,e^{(k)}_{t}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t},a^{(1)}_{t},\dots,a^{(k)}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle:=f_{\bar{\nu}}\left(\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t},\left(a^{(1)}_{t},\dots,a^{(k)}_{t}\right)\right)\left(e^{(1)}_{t},\dots,e^{(k)}_{t}\right)\,.

A policy for the
i
-th agent of
{\bar{\nu}}
is a policy-like chronological conditional semimeasure
\pi^{(i)}:\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(i)*}\to\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{A}^{(i)}
.

If
\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}\right)^{t-1}
describes the complete interaction of the
k
agents with the
k
-multi-agent environment for
t-1
steps, then we write
\text{\ae}^{(i)}_{<t}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(i)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(i)}\right)^{t-1}
to denote the corresponding (subjective) history from the perspective of the
i
-th agent.

The interactions between a
k
-multi-agent-environment and policies of the
k
agents can be defined in a natural way which is analogous to how we defined it in the single-agent setting:

Definition F.2
.

Let
{\bar{\nu}}
be a
k
-multi-agent environment with action spaces
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)}
, observation spaces
\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
and reward spaces
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
, and let
\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(k)}
be policies for the
k
agents of
{\bar{\nu}}
, respectively. We define the semimeasure
{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(k)}}
on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(1:k)*}
recursively as follows:

{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(k)}}(\varepsilon)=1\,,

and

\displaystyle{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(k)}}

\displaystyle\left(\text{\ae}_{<t}^{(1:k)}a_{t}^{(1:k)}e_{t}^{(1:k)}\right)

\displaystyle={\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(1)},\ldots,\pi^{(k)}}\left(\text{\ae}_{<t}^{(1:k)}\right)\times\left(\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}\pi^{(i)}\left(a_{t}^{(i)}\middle|\text{\ae}_{<t}^{(i)}\right)\right)\times{\bar{\nu}}\left(e^{(1:k)}_{t}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t},a^{(1:k)}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\left(\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}\pi^{(i)}\left(a^{(i)}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\right)\right)\times{\bar{\nu}}\left(e^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t^{\prime}},a^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\right)\,,

for all
\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}\times\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}\right)^{t-1}
, all
e^{(1:k)}_{t}=\left(e^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{t}\right)\in\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}
, and all
a^{(1:k)}_{t}=\left(a^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,a^{(k)}_{t}\right)\in\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}
.

If we fix the policies of a subset of agents in a multi-agent environment, we get a multi-agent environment with a smaller number of agents. This can be seen as one possible way of formalizing the concept of "embedding an agent in an environment":

Definition F.3
.

Let
{\bar{\nu}}
be a
k
-multi-agent environment with action spaces
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)}
, observation spaces
\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
and reward spaces
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
. Let
1\leq\ell<k
and let
\pi^{(i_{1})},\pi^{(i_{2})},\ldots,\pi^{(i_{\ell})}
be policies for the
i_{1}
-th, the
i_{2}
-th, …, and the
i_{\ell}
-th agent of
{\bar{\nu}}
, respectively. We define the
(k-\ell)
-multi-agent environment
{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(i_{1})},\ldots,\pi^{(i_{\ell})}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})}
as follows:

•

The action spaces of
{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(i_{1})},\ldots,\pi^{(i_{\ell})}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})}
are
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(1)}=\mathcal{A}^{(\tilde{i}_{1})},\ldots,\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(k-\ell)}=\mathcal{A}^{(\tilde{i}_{k-\ell})}
, where
\tilde{i}_{j}
is the
j
-th element of
\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
. The observation spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(1)},\ldots,\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(k-\ell)}
and the reward spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)},\ldots,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(k-\ell)}
are defined similarly.

•

For all
\tilde{\text{\ae}}^{(1:k-\ell)}_{<t}\in\left(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(1:k-\ell)}\times\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1:k-\ell)}\right)^{t-1}
, all
\tilde{a}^{(1:k-\ell)}_{t}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(1:k-\ell)}
, and all
\tilde{e}^{(1:k-\ell)}_{t}\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1:k-\ell)}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(i_{1})},\ldots,\pi^{(i_{\ell})}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})}

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{e}_{t}^{(1:k-\ell)}\middle|\tilde{\text{\ae}}_{<t}^{(1:k-\ell)}\tilde{a}_{t}^{(1:k-\ell)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1:t}^{(i_{1})}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(i_{1})}\right)^{t},\;e_{1:t}^{(i_{1})}\in\left(\mathcal{E}^{(i_{1})}\right)^{t}\,,\\
\vdots\\
a_{1:t}^{(i_{\ell})}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(i_{\ell})}\right)^{t},\;e_{1:t}^{(i_{\ell})}\in\left(\mathcal{E}^{(i_{\ell})}\right)^{t}\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\left(\prod_{1\leq j\leq\ell}\pi^{(i_{j})}\left(a^{(i_{j})}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\right)\times{\bar{\nu}}\left(e^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t^{\prime}},a^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\right)}{\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1:t}^{(i_{1})}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(i_{1})}\right)^{t},\;e_{1:t}^{(i_{1})}\in\left(\mathcal{E}^{(i_{1})}\right)^{t}\,,\\
\vdots\\
a_{1:t}^{(i_{\ell})}\in\left(\mathcal{A}^{(i_{\ell})}\right)^{t},\;e_{1:t}^{(i_{\ell})}\in\left(\mathcal{E}^{(i_{\ell})}\right)^{t},\\
e_{t}^{(\tilde{i}_{1})}\in\mathcal{E}^{(\tilde{i}_{1})},\ldots,e_{t}^{(\tilde{i}_{k-\ell})}\in\mathcal{E}^{(\tilde{i}_{k-\ell})}\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\left(\prod_{1\leq j\leq\ell}\pi^{(i_{j})}\left(a^{(i_{j})}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\right)\times{\bar{\nu}}\left(e^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\middle|\text{\ae}^{(1:k)}_{<t^{\prime}},a^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}\right)}\,.

where in the above we wrote
e^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}
to denote
e^{(1:k)}_{t}=(e^{(1)}_{t^{\prime}},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{t^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}
obtained by extending the elements
e^{(i_{1})}_{t^{\prime}},\ldots,e^{(i_{\ell})}_{t^{\prime}}
determined by the summation sign as follows: If
\tilde{i}_{j}
is the
j
-th element of
\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
, then we let
e^{(\tilde{i}_{j})}_{t^{\prime}}=\tilde{e}^{(j)}_{t^{\prime}}
, which is the
j
-th entry in
\tilde{e}^{(1:k-\ell)}_{t^{\prime}}=\left(\tilde{e}^{(1)}_{t^{\prime}},\ldots,\tilde{e}^{(k-\ell)}_{t^{\prime}}\right)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1:k-\ell)}
. The definition of
a^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}
and
\text{\ae}_{<t^{\prime}}^{(1:k)}
is done similarly. The aforementioned description of
e^{(1:k)}_{t^{\prime}}
has one exception when
t^{\prime}=t
in the denominator as we already have all the values of
e^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{t}
given in the summation, hence we do not use the values of
\tilde{e}^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,\tilde{e}^{(k-\ell)}_{t}
to get
(e^{(\tilde{i}_{j})})_{1\leq j\leq k-\ell}
but rather use the values given from the summation directly.

Appendix G
A few desiderata for a Bayesian theory of embedded universal intelligence

This section formulates a set of desiderata which, we believe, a good Bayesian theory of embedded universal intelligence should satisfy. The desiderata that we specify here capture the issues of AIXI and JAIXI that we informally described in Appendix
B.8
, and which made them fail in embedded settings.

It is worth noting that the desiderata that we specify in this section are not meant to capture every aspect of embedded agency. There are several aspects of embedded agency which were mentioned in
22
and which we do not capture in the desiderata that we specify here (e.g., we do not consider here the possibility that the policy of an agent may be self-modified by the agent, or modified by the environment). Our aim here is mainly to mathematically and rigorously formalize a set of goals which capture some of the aspects described in
22
and for which AIXI and JAIXI fail. This helps motivate our theory of Embedded Universal Predictive Intelligence (MUPI), as well as provide intuition and clarify the motivation behind the mathematical concepts in our MUPI theory.

We start by formulating one set of desiderata that captures a few embeddedness aspects from a purely single-agent perspective:
125
125

125

We later formulate a more general set of desiderata which capture some embeddedness aspects from a multi-agent perspective. The more general set of desiderata will be able to capture the notion that a single-agent environment, as seen from the perspective of one agent, may contain/embed other agents which may be implementing similar policies to the policy of the considered agent.

Problem G.1
.

(Informal) We aim to find three classes
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
and a universal prior
w\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
such that the following desiderata are satisfied:

1.

Generality of the considered policies:

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
is a countable class of policies (i.e., policy-like chronological conditional semimeasures) which contains all computable policies.

2.

Generality of the considered environments:

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
is a countable class of environments (i.e., environment-like chronological conditional semimeasures) which contains all computable environments.

3.

Generality of the considered universes:

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
is a countable class of semimeasures on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
which contains all computable semimeasures on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
. The class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
can be thought of as a class of (distributions of histories of) interactions between an agent and an environment. We will use the term "universe" to refer to a semimeasure on
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
which can be seen as jointly describing an agent and an environment.

4.

The interaction between a considered policy and a considered environment yields a universe that is considered in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
:
For every
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
and every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
, we have
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
.

5.

Embedded Bayesian agents with respect to the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
hypothesis class are part of the considered policies:
There exists a policy
\pi^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
which performs "a form of universal embedded Bayesian optimization" as detailed in
Sections

3.3
,
3.4
and
4.4

with respect to the mixture universe

\rho:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda\,.

Notice how the desiderata in the above problem captures the issue that we mentioned about JAIXI in
B.8
: JAIXI is not lower semicomputable, and hence if we let it interact with a computable environment, we may get a universe which is not lower semicomputable, i.e., we get something which is outside the hypothesis class of JAIXI. Putting this in the language of the above desiderata: If
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
contains JAIXI and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
contains all lower semicomputable semimeasures, then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
must contain universes which are not lower semicomputable, and hence the hypothesis class of JAIXI would not cover all the universes in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
, i.e., JAIXI would not be a valid solution of the last point of Problem
G.1
.

Now given a solution to Problem
G.1
, if we make the policy
\pi^{*}
interact with an environment in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{env}}
, then the fourth point implies that we get a universe which is in the hypothesis class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
of the "embedded Bayesian" policy
\pi^{*}
. This is in contrast with JAIXI which does not necessarily give rise to a lower semicomputable interaction if we make it interact with a computable environment.

Now we are ready to state our desiderata for a good theory of embedded universal intelligence, from a multi-agent perspective:

Problem G.2
.

(Informal) We aim to find:

•

for every choice of finite sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
with complete prefix-free encodings thereof, a countable class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
of policies with action space
\mathcal{A}
, observation space
\mathcal{O}
and reward space
\mathcal{R}
, and

•

for every
k\geq 1
and every choice of finite sets
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
with complete prefix-free encodings thereof, a countable class
\mathcal{M}_{k,\mathrm{env}}^{\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{R}^{(1:k)}}
of
k
-multi-agent environments with action spaces
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)}
, observation spaces
\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and reward spaces
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
, and

•

a countable class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
of semimeasures on
\mathcal{B}^{*}
(which we will refer to as universes), together with a universal prior
w\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
, which can be interpreted as semimeasures over
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and
\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(1:k)*}
for suitably chosen complete prefix-free encodings of
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}

such that the following desiderata are satisfied:

1.

Generality of considered policies:
For all finite sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
, the class

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
contains all computable policies of action space
\mathcal{A}
, observation space
\mathcal{O}
and reward space
\mathcal{R}
.

2.

Generality of considered multi-agent environments:
For all
k>1
, and all finite sets
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
, the class
\mathcal{M}_{k,\mathrm{env}}^{\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{R}^{(1:k)}}
contains all computable
k
-multi-agent environments with actions spaces
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)}
, observations spaces
\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and reward spaces
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
.

3.

Generality of considered universes:

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
contains all computable semimeasures on
\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

4.

Interactions between considered policies and considered single-agent environments yield universes that are considered in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
:
For all finite sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
, every
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
and every single-agent environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1,\mathrm{env}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
, we have
\nu^{\pi}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
when interpreting the bitstrings as histories
\text{\ae}_{*}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}
through complete prefix-free encodings of
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
.

5.

Embedding a set of considered policies inside a considered multi-agent environment yields a multi-agent environment (with less agents) which is also considered:
For every
k>1
, and every finite sets
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
, if

•

{\bar{\nu}}\in\mathcal{M}_{k,\mathrm{env}}^{\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{R}^{(1:k)}}
,

•

\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}
with
\ell<k
, and

•

\pi^{(i_{j})}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\mathcal{A}^{(i_{j})},\mathcal{O}^{(i_{j})},\mathcal{R}^{(i_{j})}}
for all
1\leq j\leq\ell
,

then
{\bar{\nu}}^{\pi^{(i_{1})},\ldots,\pi^{(i_{\ell})}}_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell})}\in\mathcal{M}_{k-\ell,\mathrm{env}}^{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(1:k-\ell)},\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(1:k-\ell)},\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(1:k-\ell)}}
(cf.
Definition

F.3
), where
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{(j)}:=\mathcal{A}^{(\tilde{i}_{j})},\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{(j)}:=\mathcal{O}^{(\tilde{i}_{j})},\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{(j)}:=\mathcal{R}^{(\tilde{i}_{j})}
with
\tilde{i}_{j}
being the
j
-th element of
\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\}
.

6.

Embedded Bayesian agents with respect to the
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
hypothesis class are part of the considered policies:
For every finite sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}
, there exists a policy
\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}
which performs "a form of universal embedded Bayesian optimization" as detailed in
Sections

3.3
,
3.4
and
4.4
with respect to a universal mixture model

\rho:=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}}w(\lambda)\lambda,

using a lower-semicomputable semiprior
w
, and with respect to the spaces
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
and a complete prefix-free encoding thereof.

A few comments are in order:

•

Given a solution to the above problem, if we make the policy
\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
interact with an environment in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{1,env}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
, then we get a universe which is in the hypothesis class
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
of the "embedded Bayesian" policy
\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
.

•

It is possible for a single-agent environment
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{1,env}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
to be embedding other agents in the sense that there may be a
k
-multi-agent environment
{\bar{\nu}}
in such a way that
\nu
can be obtained from
{\bar{\nu}}
by providing policies to
k-1
agents of
{\bar{\nu}}
.

•

The formalism in Problem
G.2
allows to capture notions of similarity between agents. For example, the psychological twin prisoner’s dilemma may be captured as follows: Take
{\bar{\nu}}
as the 2-multi-agent environment given by the prisoner’s dilemma
126
126

126

We can assume, e.g., that the game stops after one round.
, and for every policy
\pi
let
{\bar{\nu}}_{(2)}^{\pi}
be the single-agent environment from the perspective of the first agent of
{\bar{\nu}}
which is induced by fixing the policy of the second agent to
\pi
. By choosing a universal prior which has almost all its weight on the universes of the form
\left({\bar{\nu}}_{(2)}^{\pi}\right)^{\pi}
, we get a prior that gives a very high credence to the hypothesis that both agents implement the same policy. This captures the essence of the psychological twin variant of the prisoner’s dilemma.

G.1
The MUPI theory satisfies our desiderata

By inspecting Problems
G.1
and
G.2
, we can see that we essentially have all the ingredients:

•

We can take
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
to be the class of all universes which are equipped with a
w
-RUI-oracle
\tau
, where
w\in\Delta^{\prime}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}
is some lower semicomputable prior on the class of restricted abstract probabilistic oracle machines
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{rAPOM}}
. Note that
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
is countable and contains all lower semicomputable semimeasures on
\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

•

For finite sets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
with prefix-free encodings thereof, we can take the class of policies
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pol}}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{R}}
as those that can be implemented by a
\tau
-rPOM
P^{\tau}
which takes as input the prefix-free encoding
\langle\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\rangle
of a history
\text{\ae}_{1:{t}}\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{*}
and produces the prefix-free encoding
\langle a\rangle
of an action
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

•

For
k\geq 1
, and for finite sets
\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{(k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{O}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{(k)}
with prefix-free encodings thereof, we can take the class of all
k
-multi-agent environments
\mathcal{M}_{k,\mathrm{env}}^{\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{O}^{(1:k)},\mathcal{R}^{(1:k)}}
to be those that can be implemented by a
\tau
-rPOM
E^{\tau}
which takes as input the prefix-free encoding
\langle{\text{\ae}_{<t}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\text{\ae}_{<t}}^{(k)},a^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,a^{(k)}_{t}\rangle
of histories
({\text{\ae}_{<t}}^{(1)},\ldots,{\text{\ae}_{<t}}^{(k)})\in\text{$\mathcal{A}\kern-2.5pt\mathcal{E}$}^{(1:k)*}
and actions
(a^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,a_{t}^{(k)})\in\mathcal{A}^{(1:k)}
and produces the prefix-free encoding
\langle e^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,e_{t}^{(k)}\rangle
of percepts
(e^{(1)}_{t},\ldots,e_{t}^{(k)})\in\mathcal{E}^{(1:k)}
.

•

As shown by
Theorem

5.31
, the set of considered policies contains approximate embedded Bayesian agents: All
(k_{t},\epsilon_{t})_{t}
-E-AIXI
RUI
agents are members of the policy classes defined above. Furthermore, the approximations of these agents can be made arbitrarily good.

•

The interaction between a considered policy and a considered single agent environment (fourth point in Problem
G.2
) does indeed yield a universe in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{uni}}
: One just needs to take the machines
P
and
E
and build a machine
M
which makes
P
and
E
interact by alternately exchanging inputs and outputs appropriately. We omit the technical details here.

–

The fifth point of Problem
G.2
regarding the reduction of a
k
-multi-agent environment into a
(k-\ell)
-multi-agent environment by fixing
\ell
policies for
\ell
agents, can be shown similarly.

We can also solve
G.1
and
G.2
using the reflective oracle framework by considering the classes of all policies (resp., environment,
k
-multi-agent environments, universes) which are implementable using POMs with access to a reflective oracle
\tau
.
Theorem

5.29
shows that the universal embedded Bayesian agents E-AIXI
RO
and
k
-E-AIXI
RO
for
k>0
are all implementable as
\tau
-POMs.
